TOTAL: 53.00 29.00 53.00  
 
3.2.5 In regard to reduction at RE 2009-10 stage in the scheme of Strengthening of Fire and 
Emergency Services, the Ministry clarified that as it was sanctioned in the third quarter of the 
financial year, the allocated fund has been reduced to Rs.14 crore. The reduction in budgetary 
provision was mainly due to non-utilization of funds at RE stage. It was further stated that the Scheme 
relating to Revamping of Civil Defence was regularly monitored by the High Power Committee 
chaired by Secretary (Border Management) and Director General, NDRF and Civil Defence. 

3.2.6  The Committee was informed that the Scheme envisaged up-gradation of existing State level 
training institutes, purchase of equipments, and a pilot project for involving of Civil Defence 
volunteers in the internal security and law and order situation. One of the components of the Scheme 
was weeding out inactive and infirm Civil Defenc e Volunteers. So far, 53,787 Civil Defence 
volunteers and wardens had been taken out of the system and 29,538 new Civil Defence volunteers 
have been enrolled. The total strength of Civil Defence volunteers in the country, up to Feb., 2010, 
was 5,51,091 of which 4,61879 had been trained. 
3.2.7 The Committee has made detailed recommendati ons in its One Hundred and Thirty-sixth 
Report on the Revamping and Revitalization of Civil Defence in the country and it intends to 
present an Action Taken Report thereon, whic h would contain detailed observations and 
recommendations in the light of action taken by  the Government. Since the Government is re-
vamping the Civil Defence set up, fund requirements may be assessed properly. Regular 
monitoring on the re-vamping may be made. Enrolling young and active volunteers should be 
made a regular feature. The total volunteer force should also be trained in disaster 
management. The Committee urges upon the Gov ernment to create awareness amongst all 
citizens for the imperative need to discharge their fundamental duties, to uphold and protect the 
sovereignty, unity and integrity of the country and to defend the country  and render national 
service when called upon to do so. The Committee also urges upon the Government to prepare 
detailed plans to provide training  to all able-bodied citizens so  that they can perform their 
fundamental duties. 
3.3  National Disaster Management 
3.3.1 The Disaster Management Act, 2005 provides for effective management of disasters and for 
matters connected therewith or incidental thereto. It  provides institutional mechanisms for drawing up 
and monitoring the implementation of the disaster management plans, ensuring measures by various 
wings of the Government for prevention and mitigation of the effects of disasters and prompt response 
to any disaster situation. The Ministry of Home Affairs informed the Committee that the National 
Policy on Disaster Management (NPDM) had been prepared in tune with and in pursuance of Disaster 
Management Act, 2005 with a vision to build a safe and disaster resilient India 
3.3.2  Capital outlay on National Disaster Management has been increased to Rs.123.23 crore in BE 
2010-11 from Rs.63.79 crore in BE 2009-10. On being asked about the new programmes to be 
initiated with enhanced allocation, the Ministry of Home Affairs stated that the capital outlay has been 
increased in order to take up following new programmes/augmenting the on-going programmes: 
(a) National Emergency Communication Plan : Phase-I has been completed and Rs.10.01 
crore has been kept in BE 2010-11 for pr ocurement of machinery and equipments for 
providing broad-band voice, data and video communication links between National 
Emergency Operation Centre and State Emergency Operation Centre/ District Emergency 
Operation Centre. 
(b) National Institute of Disaster Management : This Institute is functioning from rented 
premises. Hence, Rs.12.5 crore has been  provided for procurement of land and 
construction of new office/residential buildings. 

(c) National Disaster Response Force : 
(i)  Presently, 8 battalions of NDRF are in position. They have been located at different 
strategic locations in the country. For three battalions, land/buildings are yet to be 
acquired / constructed. Accordingly, Rs.34.96 crore has been provided for office 
and residential building constructions. 
(ii) Rs.65.74 crore has been kept for pr ocurement of motor vehicles, machinery and 
equipments towards augmentation of existing resources and modernization of 
NDRF battalions. 
3.3.3  It was stated that for supporting the NDMA to discharge its mandate, the Ministry has taken the 
following steps: 
(i) 124 posts for the organizational structure of NDMA have been sanctioned. In addition, 
NDMA have also been authorized to engage subject specialists in 23 disciplines along 
with the supporting staff. 
(ii) Recruitment Rules for various Group ‘A’ and ‘C’ posts in NDMA have been notified so 
as to facilitate the recruitment of persons mostly in specialized disciplines. 
(iii) Financial powers have been delegated to Vice-Chairperson and Secretary of NDMA. 
(iv) Rs.100 crore has been provided during the current Plan period for preparation of Detailed 
Project Reports on national level mitigation projects; and 
(v) Functional autonomy has been given to NDMA for discharging their duties as mandated 
under Disaster Management Act, 2005. 
3.3.4 The Committee understands the need for stren gthening mitigation,  preparedness and 
response measures for effective and efficient management of natural as well as manmade 
disasters keeping in view vulner able geo-climatic conditions, of the country such as 58.6% 
earthquake prone landmass, 12% area floods pron e and 68% cultivable area drought prone. 
The Committee, however, observes that most of the new programmes/augmentation of on going 
programmes are at the rudimentary stages. It would take considerable time for the new 
programmes to take off. The Comm ittee is constrained to note that even though the Act was 
passed in 2005, there is no perceptible improvement in the di saster management preparedness 
in the country. The 26/11 terrorist attack in Mumbai is a glaring example when it took a lot of 
time for the NSG commandoes to reach the spot . There are several inst ances of handling of 
major fires, floods/droughts and major accidents, includi ng recently radioactive leakages 
(Cobalt 60), which should have been tackled with great promptness and effectively. The 
Committee therefore, urges upon the Ministry of Home Affairs and NDMA to take this task as a 
major challenge so that tangible results are vi sible for the countrymen. National Institute of 
Disaster Management is functioning from rente d premises. Land/Buildings for three battalions 
of NDRF are yet to be acquired. Though Phase I of National Emergency Communication Plan 
has been completed, the Committee apprehends that the provision of a meagre amount of Rs. 10 
crore is not sufficient in view of the vastness of the country and its vu lnerability. Therefore, 
Fund requirements need to be re-assessed. Th e Committee also recommends that 124 posts 

sanctioned for the NDMA may be filled up at the ea rliest. There should not be any delay in this 
regard. 
3.3.5  The Committee in its 115th Report on the Disa ster Management Bill 2005 dealt with in 
detail about the mechanism to be  put in place for tackling the natural disasters like earthquake, 
cyclones, floods. The Committee understands that there should be requisite mechanism for 
drawing up and monitoring impl ementation of the disaster management plans, ensuring 
measures for preventing and mi tigating disaster. The NDMA may also chalk out a plan for 
giving training to Gov ernment officials, on disaster manage ment. Training on this should be 
made compulsory for all new recruits. 
3.4  Home Guards 
3.4.1 Home Guards is a voluntary force to assist the police in controlling civil disturbance and 
communal riots. The role of Home Guards is to serve as an auxiliary to the police in the maintenance 
of law and order and internal secu rity; help the community in any ki nd of emergency such as air-raid, 
fire, cyclone, earthquake, epidemic etc.; help in maintenance of essential services; promote communal 
harmony; assist the administration in protecting weaker sections; participate in socio-economic and 
welfare activities; and perform civil defence duties. Home Guards are raised under the Home Guards 
Acts and Rules of the States/Union Territories. 
3.4.2 The Ministry of Home Affairs informed th e Committee that the Ministry would formulate a 
policy in respect of role, raising, training, equipping, establishment and other important matters of 
Home Guards Organisation. Expenditure on Home Gu ards is generally shared between Centre and 
State Governments in the ratio of 25% by the Centre and 75% by the State Governments, for raising, 
training and equipping, on reimbursement basis. For North Eastern States, the sharing pattern between 
the Centre and States is in the ratio of 50:50. 
3.4.3  The Ministry stated that during 2008-09, out of the allotted budget of Rs. 48 crore, Rs. 46.5 
crore had been reimbursed to various States on rais ing, training and equipping of Home Guards. For 
BE 2009-10, Rs. 50 crore was allocated which was reduced to Rs. 47.5 crore at RE stage and the 
actual expenditure was Rs. 44.14 crore. Again in BE 2010-11, Rs. 42 crores has been allocated. 
3.4.4  Replying to the Committee’s question as to how far the services of Home Guards had been 
proved to be effective for maintaining law and order, protection of public property, rescue and relief 
work during disasters, calamities and other entrusted wo rk so far, the Ministry of Home Affairs stated 
that Home Guards in States/UTs were governed, employed and administered under the respective 
State Home Guards Acts and Rules. The role of Central Government was advisory in nature and 
limited to reimbursement upto specified limits by allocating grants-in-aid. It was also stated that the 
services of Home Guards had been found to be eff ectiye by most of the Stat es. Home Guards relieve 
the police by taking over certain responsibilities such as traffic control and internal security duties, 
which help the police in carrying out other important activities. Though Disaster Management does 
not specifically form part of duties of Home Guards, but due to one of their roles being auxiliary to 
police, their services do get utilized  for disaster management. Certain States in the last few years had 
started training Home Guards in disaster management and this was likely to further increase the 
utilization of Home Guards in future. 

3.4.5  The Ministry informed the Committee that fund s were allocated to States for Home Guards as 
grant-in-aid on reimbursement basis. It was also st ated that as the rates for reimbursement are fixed, 
the demand for grants was made on the basis of number of claims for reimbursement and the amount 
as received from the State Governments after being duly audited at the State level. As regards the 
reduction in BE 2010-11 as compared to RE 2009-10,  the Ministry stated that the decrease was due to 
non-receipt of claims from State Governments. 
3.4.6  The Committee was further informed that the Second Administrative Reforms Commission 
(ARC) in its Third Report titled “Crisis Management From Despair to Hope” made the following 
recommendations in respect of Home Guards: 
(a) Home Guards at the field level who are among the first responders should be adequately 
trained in handling crises/disasters. Such traini ng should be specific to the types of crises 
envisaged in an area. More importantly, they should be fully involved in preparation of 
the local Crisis/Disaster Management Plan and also be fully conversant with them. 
(b) A section of Home Guards should also be given Para-medical training. 
3.4.7  The Committee was informed that the above recommendations made by Second Administrative 
Reform Commission (ARC) had been circulated to all States/UTs for implementation and 15 
States/UTs had confirmed that they were implementing the same. The Ministry also stated that at the 
Central level, the training in handling crises/disasters and the Para-medical training was conducted at 
the National Civil Defence College, Nagpur. Adequate publicity had been planned to revitalize the 
role of Home Guards. Towards this end, a promotional Video Spot was made in March, 2010 to attract 
the youth of the country to join the Home Guard. 
3.4.8  The Committee appreciates the role played by the Home Guards in assisting police in 
maintaining law and order and in various othe r security related duties including disaster 
management. The Committee agrees with the suggestions made by 2nd ARC. In the present day 
scenario, disaster management a nd para medical training, are very important and these should 
be made an integral part of the training of  Home Guards. The Committee is however given to 
understand that the picture is not so rosy as it is being projected. Home Guard Jawans do not 
receive the needed moral support, proper leadership and financial incentives. They are always 
under the feeling of job insecurity and are inducted mostly on part-time basis. The Committee is 
unhappy to note that the States are not submitting their claims on time and therefore allocation 
is getting reduced and so is the expenditure whic h also gets reduced reluctantly. It is an 
unfortunate situation where States are not properly making use of the scheme and the funds. 
The centre should write to all the States to impl ement the scheme in letter and spirit and make 
their claims on a regular basis. The Committee recommends that the Union Home Minister may 
take up this issue with all the State Governme nts impressing upon them the need to make 
optimum use of the scheme, submit claims regular ly so that adequate f unds could be allocated 
and released. A review of the role and responsib ilities of Home Guards may be undertaken to 
revitalize their role and consequent augmentation of the resources. 
3.5  Fire Services 
3.5.1 Fire prevention and fire fighting services are organized by the States/Union Territories. Ministry 
of Home Affairs renders technical advice to States/U nion Territories and Cent ral Ministries on Fire 
Protection, Fire Prevention, Fire Legislation and Training. The Committee was informed that with a 

view to upgrade Fire and Emergency Services in the States, Ministry of Home Affairs would arrange 
soft loans from General In surance Corporation through the Ministry of Finance (Insurance Division) 
for the purchase of capital equipments and also co nstruction of Fire Station Buildings. From 1980-81 
till date, a total sum of Rs. 404.97 crore by way of loans had been arranged by the Ministry of Home 
Affairs for the development of State Fire Services. The Tenth and Eleventh Finance Commissions had 
allocated Rs. 80 crore and Rs. 201 crore, respectively,  as grant-in-aid for the modernization of fire 
Services in the States. 
3.5.2 The Ministry further stated that with the objective to fill the critical gaps in the Fire and 
Emergency Services in the country, a Centrally Sponsored Scheme namely “Strengthening Fire and 
Emergency Services” at an estimated cost of Rs .200 crore had been approved on 22-10-2009 by the 
Government as a supplemental initiative. The Scheme would be implemented within the remaining 
three years of the Eleventh Plan period and the pr oposed expenditure of Rs. 200 crore would be on 
non-recurring items. The State Governments would bear the recurring expenditure. 
3.5.3 The share of Centre and States’ contribution on procurement of equipments was in the ratio of 
75:25 and for North-Eastern States in the ratio of 90:10. During 2009-10, Rs. 38 crore were allocated 
at BE which was reduced to Rs.14 core at RE. Again for BE 2010-11, Rs.38 crore were allocated. 
3.5.4  The Committee was also informed that the Mini stry decided to carry out Fire Risk and Hazard 
Analysis in the country and Rs.10 crore has been earmarked for undertaking the task. 
3.5.5  The Ministry then informed the Committee that Central Government’s contribution of Rs.200 
crore would be utilized for procurement of capital  equipments worth Rs.178.12 crore which include 
277 Advance Fire Tenders at an estimated cost of Rs.69.25 crore, 1146 High Pressure Pumps with 
Water Mist Technology at an estimated cost of Rs. 68.76 crore, 573 Quick Response Team Vehicles at 
a cost of Rs.17.19 crore and 11.46 Combi-Tools at a cost of Rs.22.92 crore. 
3.5.6  The Committee having taken note of the major fire accidents with alarming regularity in 
the country, causing heavy causalit ies, particularly in commercial buildings in Bangalore and 
the Stephen’s Court Building in Ko lkata, wishes to highlight the weaknesses in the fire fighting 
mechanism of the country. The Committee notes wi th dismay that lessons have not been learnt 
from the heart-rendering incidents of Dabw ali (1995) Kumbakonam  (2004), and Uphaar 
Cinema, (Delhi) (1997) tragedies. The National  Building Code (NBC) published in 1970 and 
updated in 2005 does prescribe th e minimum fire safety and rescue measures to be provided in 
the buildings but what is missing is the actual enforcement mechanism. Transparency in giving 
clearance to construction of structures must be in place and public ac cess to building safety 
data, a practice common in other parts of the world, ought to adopted in our country. The 
Committee, therefore, recomme nds that Government must undertake required steps for 
strengthening of fire fighting mechanism. The old and the new structures should comply with 
the mandated rules and the State Governments must be persuaded to ensure enforcement and 
compliance of the fire fighting measures and safety code. The Committee also recommends that 
a complete overhauling of fire services needs on an emergent basis be done. The Committee 
takes a serious note of the fact that the allocation of Rs. 38 crore made at BE 2009-10 was 
reduced to Rs. 14 crore at RE. This needs to be seriously looked into. 

3.5.7  The Committee desires that reassessment of the fire and emergency preparedness for the 
Commonwealth Games in Delhi may be done as  any untoward incident during the Games may 
bring bad name to the nation. 
3.6  Modernization of Prison Administration 
3.6.1  The Central Government launched a non-plan scheme in 2002-03 for construction of new jails, 
to reduce over-crowding, repair, renovation and construction of additional barracks in the existing 
jails, improvement in sanitation and water supply and construction of living accommodatin for prison 
personnel. The Committee was informed that the scheme known as Modernization of Prisons has been 
implemented in 27 States over a period of five years (2002-07) with an outlay of Rs.1,800 crore. The 
Ministry of Home Affairs stated that that as per reports received for the period ending December 
2009, 99 new jails, 1,365 additional barracks in the existing prisons and 7,852 staff quarters for the 
prison personnel had been constructed by the State Governments under the Scheme. 
3.6.2  The allocation for Modernization of Prison Administration had been drastically reduced from 
Rs.5.00 crore in BE 2009-10 to Rs.1.00 crere in RE 2009-10 and again to a token provision of Rs. 
0.01 Crore in BE 2010-11. Explaining about the reduction in allocation, the Ministry explained that 
since the second phase of the Scheme of Modernisation of Prisons was under consideration at the time 
of finalization of BE 2009-10, a token amount of Rs. 5 crore was proposed in the BE 2009-10 with a 
view to get suitable budget provision from supplemen tary grant after approval of the second phase of 
the scheme by the Cabinet. The proposal for second phase of the Scheme was formulated and placed 
before the Committee on Non-Plan Expenditure (CNE) in Ministry of Finance. 
3.6.3  It was stated that considering the recommendations of the Department-related Parliamentary 
Standing Committee on Home Affairs, keeping in vi ew the demands of various States/UTs for grant 
of further financial assistance for prison infrastructure and correctional administration, the proposal 
for second phase of the Scheme of Modernization of  Prisons was initiated by the Ministry of Home 
Affairs. Before seeking Cabinet approval, the proposal was considered in the meeting of the 
Committee of Non-Plan Expenditure (CNE) on August 27, 2009 under the Chairmanship of Secretary 
(Expenditure). In the said meeting, it was decided to defer the second phase of the Scheme for the 
time being owing to heavy commitments of Government of India on other important sectors. 
3.6.4  The Committee in its One Hund red and Forty Second Report on Implementation of 
Central Scheme of Modernisation of Prison Admi nistration, made a detailed study of the same 
and made several recommendations for prison infrastructure a nd correctional administration. 
The Ministry of Home Affairs in its Action Taken Notes stated that the Committee’s 
recommendations, had been forwarded to the St ate/UT Governments for due compliance and 
the experience gained during the first phase of th e implementation of the scheme would be duly 
utilized in its second phase. The Committee fo und the Action Taken Notes of the Ministry 
evasive and incomplete and asked the Ministry to  furnish concrete Acti on Taken Notes on its 
recommendations after due consultation with the States/UT Governments. While proper Action 
Taken Notes of the Ministry are still awaited,  the Committee is surpri sed to note that the 
Government has decided to defer the second phase of the scheme while the jails in the country 
needs immediate modernization. Howeve r, the Committee would make detailed 
recommendations on the subject in  its Action Taken Report (ATR) after receiving proper 
Action Taken Notes from the Mi nistry of Home Affairs in the matter. In the meanwhile, the 

Committee would like the Government to reconside r the decision to defer the second phase of 
the scheme. 
3.7  Displaced persons from Sri Lanka 
3.7.1 The Ministry of Home Affairs informed the Committee that due to ethnic violence and continued 
disturbed conditions in Sri Lanka, a large number of Sri Lankans had entered India since July 1983. 
The position of influx of such displaced persons in phases is indicated below: 
Phase Period No. of Refugees 
Phase- I 24.7.1983 to 31.12.1987 1,34,053 
Phase-II 25.8.1989 to 30.4.1991 1,22,078 
Phase-III 31.7.1996 to 30.4.2003 22,418 
Phase-IV 12.1.2006 to 31.12.2009 24,512* 
 T OTAL :  3,03,061 
 
* Upto December 31, 2009 
3.7.2   According to the Ministry, these people are of the following two categories:–– 
(i) Stateless persons who had not applied for Indian citizenship or those not yet conferred Sri 
Lankan citizenship; and 
(ii) Sri Lankan citizens. 
3.7.3  For these categories, the liability was basically of Sri Lanka. It was also stated that the 
Government of India’s approach was to discourage their movement but if those belonging to these 
categories came over to India, they were granted relief on humanitarian grounds with the ultimate 
objective of their repatriation back to Sri Lanka, i.e., the process of rehabilitation does not start in their 
cases and relief is given pending such repatriation. 
3.7.4  While 99,469 displaced persons were repatriated to Sri Lanka upto March, 1995, there had been 
no organized repatriation thereafter. 
3.7.5  In response to the Committee’s pointed question whether the Sri Lankan Tamils are treated as 
displaced persons as such, the Ministry of Home Affairs apprised the Committee as under:–– 
“India is not a signatory to the 1951 Convention on the status of refugees and its 1967 Protocol. 
We have permitted a limited role for UNHCR for refugees. Due to the continued disturbed 
conditions and ethnic violence in Sri Lanka, a large number of Sri Lankan refugees crossed over 
to India since July, 1983. In pursuance of th e Committee of Secretaries (CoS) decision of 
September, 1983, the policy of the Government in this regard has been to discourage the influx 
of refugees but in case any refugee(s) from Sri Lanka do arrive, they are to be accommodated in 
relief camps and relief provided on humanitarian grounds with the ultimate objective of 
arranging for their early repatr iation. As per the said CoS decision of 1983, various relief 
facilities are provided to Sri Lankan refugees du ring their stay in Refugee Relief Camps. These 
include shelter in camps, cash doles, clothing , subsidized ration, utensils and medical care. 

Further, it may also be mentioned that in October 2009, Ministry of External Affairs have 
communicated that Sri Lankan Government is willing to take back Sri Lankan Tamil Refugees.” 
3.7.8  Rs. 40.00 crore allocated in RE 2009-10 has been reduced to Rs. 35.00 crore in BE 2010-11. 
Explaining about variation, the Ministry stated that during the year 2009-10, Rs. 40.00 crore was kept 
in RE to clear the backlogs of bills of previous years received fro m the Government of Tamil Nadu. 
Keeping in view the general trends of expenditure, towards relief and rehabilitation assistance for Sri 
Lankans, BE for 2010-11 kept at Rs.35 crore was considered sufficient. In principle approval has been 
given to Government of Tamil Nadu to incur expenditure to the extent of Rs. 30 crore for up-gradation 
of infrastructure in the relief camps. It was also stat ed that if any claims were received from the Govt. 
of Tamil Nadu on this account during 2010-11, additional funds would be sought by way of re-
appropriation/ supplementaries. 
3.7.9  The Committee takes note of the fact that a large number of Sri Lankan Tamils have 
crossed over to India during the internal disturba nces in that country and the Government of 
India is providing relief to them on humanitarian grounds. The Committee however desires that 
no Sri Lankan Tamil who has crossed over to India should starve even if India not a signatory to 
the 1951 Convention, and its 1967 Protocol. 
3.8  Displaced persons from Afghanistan 
3.8.1  Afghan nationals migrated to India in two phases. The first phase began in 1980 and continued 
up to 1992 and the second phase started in June, 1992 and is still continuing. The Afghans, who came 
to India because of infighting between different ethnic groups in their country, comprised of both 
Muslim and Hindu/Sikh communities. Some of these Afghan nationals have also been recognized by 
United Nations High Commission for Refugees (UNHCR)  as refugees and certificates to this effect 
have been given to them. The Afghan nationals migrating to India are broadly categorized as Ethnic 
and Non-ethnic. The general policy is encourage th e ethnic Afghan Nationals to go back to their 
country since political situation has improved there. Extension in visa/stay permit is liberally granted 
to non-ethnic Afghans who are registered with FRROs/FROs. 
3.8.2  Some of the salient features of the current policy for extension of stay of Afghan nationals in 
India are given below:- 
(i) Ethnic and non-ethnic Afghan nationals are granted extension of stay up to 30.06.2010. 
(ii) All Afghan nationals entering India on fresh vi sa are required to register within 14 days 
(temporary registration) except some specified  cases like diplomats, official passport 
holders, sponsored Afghan nationals on studies/training etc. 
(iii) In keeping with the policy of the Government to encourage them to leave the country 
permanently, ethnic Afghan nationals are not granted re-entry facility for any purpose 
whatsoever. 
(iv) All non-ethnic Afghan nationals ( i.e. Hindus and Sikhs) who are registered with 
FRRO/FROs may be granted one re-entry fac ility for a maximum period of 90 days or 
else two re-entry facilities in a year for a maximum period of 45 days each for disposal of 
their property in Afghanistan; or visiting a country other than Afghanistan and Pakistan, if 
the request is found justified. 

(v) All Afghan nationals who have entered into marriage in India and have their 
children/family staying in India should in due course be encouraged to apply for Indian 
Citizenship by naturalization. 
(vi) Extension of visa or stay permits shall not be granted on the basis of certificates from the 
UNHCR granting refugee status to Afghan nationals in respect of those who have arrived 
recently in India i.e; after 01.01.2009. 
(vii)  Extension of visa to Afghan nationals on pressing medical grounds is allowed. 
3.8.3  The Ministry of Home Affairs has informed the Committee that India is not a signatory to the 
1951 United Nations Convention on the Status of Refugees and the 1967 Protocol thereof. The basic 
objective of the Convention is to assure refugees widest possible exercise of rights and freedoms. The 
Convention basically deals with post Second World War refugee issues and its scope was broadened 
by the 1967 Protocol. None of India’s neighbouring countries like Pakistan, Bangladesh, Sri Lanka, 
Nepal, Myanmar, Afghanistan, Thailand or Bhutan are signatories to this Convention/Protocol. 
3.8.4  The Ministry has also informed the Committee that there is no national legislation at present 
with regard to the refugees. The enactment of such a national law dealing with refugees is now under 
active consideration of the Government. 
3.8.5  The Committee takes note of the fact that a large number of displaced persons from 
Afghanistan have crossed over to India due to in ternal disturbances in that country and that 
Government is providing relief to them on humanitarian grounds. 
3.9  Coastal Security 
3.9.1  A supplemental scheme called ‘Coastal Security Scheme’ is under implementation in the nine 
coastal States and four coastal Union Territories (U Ts) since 2005 for streng thening infrastructure for 
coastal patrolling and surveillance. Under the scheme, assistance has been/is being given to all the 
coastal States of Gujarat, Maharashtra, Goa, Karnataka, Kerala, Tamil Nadu, Andhra Pradesh, Orissa 
and West Bengal and the Union Territories of Daman & Diu, Lakshadweep, Puducherry and 
Andaman & Nicobar Islands to set up 73 coastal police stations, 97 check posts, 58 outposts, and 30 
operational barracks and to equip them with 204 boats, 153 jeeps and 312 motorcycles for mobility on 
the coast and in close coastal waters. A lump-sum assistance of Rs. 10 lakh per police station is also 
given for equipment, computers .and furniture. The approved outlay of the scheme is Rs. 400 crore for 
five years on fuel, repair and maintenance of boats and training of manpower, which is provided by 
the States and UTs. 
3.9.2  The Committee wishes to highlight the imperative need for a robust coastal security in the 
backdrop of the modalities of the 26/11 attack. The Committee recommends that the 
Government should make all efforts to ensu re that comprehensive and fool-proof coastal 
security is put in place so that another 26/11 type attack may not take place. 
3.10   Crime and Criminal Tracking and Networking System (CCTNS) 
3.10.1 Crime and Criminal Tracking and Networki ng System (CCTNS) project has been launched in 
the Eleventh Five Year Plan with an outlay of Rs. 2,000 crore with the following objectives: 

(i) Provide the Investigating Officers of th e Civil Police with tools, technology and 
information to facilitate investigation of crime and detection of criminals; 
(ii) Improve Police functioning in various other areas such as law & order, traffic 
management etc.; 
(iii) Facilitate interaction and sharing of crime and criminal information among Police 
Stations, Districts, State/UT headquarters and other police Agencies; 
(iv) Keep track of the progress of cases, including in courts; 
(v) Make the police functioning citizen-fri endly and transparent by automating the 
functioning of Police Stations; and 
(vi) Improve delivery of citizen-centric services through effective usage of ICT. 
3.10.2  Rs.164.43 crore was allocated in BE for the year 2009-10 for CCTNS project under Demand 
No.53 (Police). However, after mid-term appraisal, the allocation was reduced to Rs.104 crore, even 
though MHA had plans for the utilization of the full amount. Around 98% of the revised allocation of 
Rs.104 crore had already been released to the Stat es and the Union Territories. There is a proposal for 
utilization of another Rs.17.03 crore subject to the av ailability of additional funds. The Ministry felt 
that more funds could have been utilized had the RE not been limited the allocation of Rs.104 crore in 
the year 2009-10. 
3.10.3  The Ministry was optimistic that since the project is scheduled to be completed by end of 
Eleventh Five Year Plan, the physical targets are likely to be achieved within the stipulated timeframe. 
3.10.4  The under-utilization of the allocated funds was because of delayed take off of the project in 
view of the General Elections 2009. The project was approved by the Cabinet Committee on 
Economic Affairs (CCEA) only on 19th June, 2009 and by Planning Commission on 8th July, 2009. 
Since the project is mainly to be implemented by the State Governments and Union Territories, it took 
some time for them to fulfill all preconditions for release of funds which included signing of 
Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) by them, setting up of governance mechanism at the State 
level and identification / appointment of State Designated Agency to receive the funds. 
3.10.5 The Committee appreciates the ad option of principles of e-governance and creation of a 
nationwide networked in frastructure of IT – enabled stat e-of-the-art tracking system of 
“investigation of crime and detection of criminals” in real time, which is a critical requirement 
in the context of the present day internal security  scenario. The Committee believes that the 
project would enable a comprehensive and integr ated system for enhancing the efficiency and 
effectiveness of policing at the police station level and arresting the increasing criminal 
activities. The Committee recommends that the a llocated funds for the purpose should be 
optimally utilized for timely completion of the project. 
3.11  Security for Commonwealth Games 2010 
3.11.1 Explaining about the security arrangements being made for the Commonwealth Games 2010, 
the Ministry of Home Affairs stated that an Integrated Security System, for the competition and non-
competition/training venues for the CWG 2010, through the ECIL had been taken up. ECIL would 

implement the solution for 36 venues within 180 days from the date of commencement of the contract. 
ECIL had committed to do the needful by 30th June. Most of the equipments for the CCTVs and 
access control would be made available within 30th April. Bandwidth would need be provided by the 
MTNL for the security network, games data Network and broadcast video network purposes. The 
work regarding providing the Wide Area Network would be completed / delivered by 15th April and 
the installation, commission and testing would go on up to 3rd September, 2010. 
3.11.2  It was also stated that a number of feat ures had been introduced in the preparation for the 
CWG 2010 on the lines of major international sports events held in the recent past and were to take 
place shortly. Briefly, perimeter, access control and advance security checks had been contemplated 
for each venue. Area security would be beefed up through mobile patrolling, commando strike teams, 
mobile Quick Reaction Teams (QRT), foot patrolling and air space security. Multi-level security 
arrangement for each venue for the outer cordon - by  traffic regulation, CCTV cameras, soft checks, 
authorized access; middle cordon channelization of spectators and physical segregation; inner cordon - 
by sensitization, bar code readers, vehicle checking of VIPs and; the exclusion zone - wherein frisking 
and intensive CCTV camera coverage would be used . All the routes have been identified and are 
being secured through anti-sabotage checks, armed mobile escort teams, and roof top arrangements by 
deploying snipers. The Committee was informed that technology would play a major role and the 
venues - competition, non-competition and practice and the parking facilities were to be completely 
covered with CCTV coverage which would be networked to the Delhi Police HQ for easy analysis and 
response. Prevention devices to protect from any bomb attack through bollards, boom barriers, tyre 
killers and under-vehicle surveillance systems etc. had been built into all the venues. All the personnel 
who would be working for the Games would be accredited which would imply that they would be 
subject to a verification process and would be provided with a badge which would have certain inbuilt 
devices (Radio Frequency Identification Device - RF ID) to facilitate easy identification which cannot 
be replicated. All the venues would be kept under the guard of CPMFs from 1st April until the 
completion of the Games. Special perimeter walls have been built and overall security measures 
inbuilt into them. To ensure that chemical, biol ogical, radiological and nuclear attacks could be 
detected, surveillance systems were being proc ured. Counter-terrorism measures were being 
implemented through multi-agency intelligence collection and response mechanism as well as 
heliborne/ mobile assault teams. 
3.11.3  Replying to the Committee’s query whether a provision of Rs.50.00 crore in BE 2010-11 
would prove sufficient for completion of remaining projects in time, the Ministry of Home Affairs 
stated that the Government of NCT of Delhi has informed that the provision of Rs.50 crore made in 
BE 2010-11 is not sufficient for completion of remaining projects and it needed to be enhanced to 
Rs.I000.00 crore. 
3.11.4  Explaining about safe and successful conduct of Commonwealth Games 2010, the Committee 
was informed by the Ministry that an Empowered Security Committee (ESC) to coordinate, oversee 
and approve the security arrangements for the mega sporting event, had been constituted. The ESC is 
headed by the Union Home Secretary and has the Se cretaries of Youth Affairs and Sports, Health & 
Family Welfare, Defence, Urban Development, Chief Secretaries of Delhi, Haryana and Uttar Pradesh 
as well as the Directors General of CRPF, CISF, Director IB, Commissioner of Delhi Police and the 
CEO of the Organizing Committee for the Games as Members alongwith other officials of the MHA. 

The ESC has met over 15 times since 23rd August 2006 and the last meeting was held on the 3rd 
March 2010. 
3.11.5  The Chief Secretary, Government of NCT of Delhi during his deposition before the Committee 
on 23 April 2010 also apprised it of the preparedness of CWG 2010. 
3.11.6  Organization of a sporting event like the Co mmonwealth Games is a major challenge for 
any country not only from the point of view of investment in infrastructure but also from the 
security aspect, given the global security scenario. The Committee urges upon the Central 
Government, Government of NCT, Delhi and all ag encies concerned to ensure that there should 
not be any lapse in security arrangements for want of resources. Government may, therefore, 
provide funds to put in place a fool proof security system. 
3.11.7  The Committee takes note of the fact that an Empowered Security Committee (ESC) has 
been constituted to co-ordinate, oversee a nd approve the security arrangement for the 
Commonwealth Games 2010. In this regard, the Committee would like to recommend that co-
ordination and monitoring need to be done at every level right up to the lowest level of security 
deployment. Intelligence agencies should also be actively involved in co-ordination. There should 
not be any time gap in the dissemination and pe rcolation of intelligence inputs. The Committee 
also recommends that the security personnel may be sensitized to be people-friendly. 
3.11.8 The Committee expresses its displeasure over  the under-utilization of funds meant for 
modernization of Traffic and Communication Ne twork for which Rs. 40 crore was allocated in 
BE 2009-10 but only an amount of Rs. 10.63 crore could be utilized, which indicates serious 
lacunae in the implementation aspects of the project. It is a matter of great concern that the 
project was not initially timed to be comple ted before the. Commonwe alth Games 2010. The 
Committee is perturbed to note that the remaini ng amount has been appropriated for other 
purpose namely, ‘Delhi Police Building Programme’. 
3.12  National Investigation Agency (NIA) 
3.12.1 The National Investigation Agency (NIA) is a new entity set up in the backdrop of the 26/11 
Mumbai attack to combat terrorism in the countr y. NIA has been empowered to investigate terror-
related crimes across the States without the requirement of special permission from the concerned 
State. 218 posts at various levels including that of Director General have been created. One time 
method of recruitment for Group ‘A’ and ‘B’ posts has been finalized in consultation with Union 
Public Service Commission. The Recruitment Rules for Group ‘C’ posts have been notified. 108 posts 
have been filled up on deputation/attachment basis. Filling up of remaining posts is an ongoing 
process. 
3.12.2  The NIA has so far been assigned 14 cases for investigation and prosecution. Chargesheets 
have been filled in 2 cases. The NIA headquarters in  Delhi has been designated as a ‘Police Station’. 
Four Special Courts have been created under the National Investigation Agency Act, 2008, at Delhi, 
Guwahati, Mumbai and Kochi, for trial of cases investigated by the National Investigation Agency. 
3.12.3 The Committee was also informed that against an allocation of Rs. 15.00 crore, NIA has been 
able to incur the expenditure of about Rs. 12.12 crore in 2009-10 and keeping in view the expenditure 
in 2009-10, allocation of Rs. 16.33 crores in BE 2010-11 for the NIA appeared to be sufficient. 
However, due to expension of infrastructure, etc., further amount, if any, required in 2010-11, would 

be met from funds available at RE stage. The Comm ittee was further informed that the office of NIA 
functions from a rented building and a note for the Cabinet Committee on Accommodation has been 
moved, for approval of its office building in Delhi. 
3.12.4 The Committee appreciates the constitution of  the National Investigation Agency (NIA) 
and hopes that necessary steps would be taken for ma king available the required infrastructure 
so that it can discharge its mandate efficiently. The Commi ttee recommends that the vacant 
posts in NIA should be filled up at the earliest. The Committee also recommends that permanent 
office accommodation should be provided to NIA within the shortest possible time. 
3.13  Bureau of Police Research and Development (BPR&D) 
3.13.1 BPR&D was set up in 1970 to identify the needs and problems of police in the country to 
undertake appropriate research project and studies and to suggest modalities to overcome the same. It 
was also mandated to keep abreast of latest developments in the fields of science and technology, both 
in India and abroad, with a view to promote the use of appropriate technology in police work. The 
Committee was informed that over the years, this organization had been entrusted with the 
responsibility of monitoring the training needs .and quality of training in States towards modernization 
of police forces and correctional administration. 
3.13.2  The Plan allocation made to BPR&D at BE 2009-10 was Rs. 20.00 crore which was reduced to 
Rs. 14.67 crore at RE stage and the actual expenditure was Rs. 2.25 crore. Again for 2010-11 Rs. 
20.00 crore has been allocated. Explaining about the variation between BE & RE, 2009-10, the 
Ministry informed the Committee that in BE 2009-10, Rs.15 crore was provided for construction of 
BPR&D HQrs, two Central Detective Training School (CDTS) at Gandhinagar and Lucknow and 
Central Academy for Police Training (CAPT), Bhopal. 
3.13.3  The Ministry of Home Affairs stated that  the under-utilization on these works was mainly due 
to the following reasons:–– 
z “The land originally allotted to BPR&D was taken back on the direction of Cabinet 
Secretariat, for construction of an underpass near Delhi Airport. It was later restored to 
BPR&D on 7.3.2010 after consistent efforts. This Ministry has released Rs. 1.70 crore for 
construction of BPR&D HQrs in March, 2010. A time bound action plan has also been 
prepared for timely implementation of the project. 
z The allotment of land by the Government of Gujarat for CDTS at Gandhinagar is in the 
final stages. All efforts are being made the obtain possession of the land at the earliest. 
z Ministry of Defence have been requested to transfer the vacant defence land at Lucknow 
to ITBP, who in turn could allocate the required land to BPR&D . Decision in this regard 
is likely to be taken by MOD shortly. 
z The land for construction of Central Academy of Police Training (CAPT) at Bhopal was 
alloted only in December, 2009. An amount of Rs. 1.67 crore has been released to CPWD 
for architectural design, soil testing and construction of pillars for CAPT, Bhopal. Major 
construction activities will start for setting up of CAPT at Bhopal after CPWD submit 
architectural design & planning”. 

3.13.4  So far as the ‘Training Intervention’ scheme was concerned, the Ministry of Home Affairs 
replying to the Committee’s query stated that the main reason for under-utilization of funds (Rs. 2 
Crore in BE 2009-10) for ‘Train ing Intervention’ was the time consuming process of development of 
educative materials on training, which has since been completed and the process for publishing the 
same was under active process. Process to identify experts / agencies/institutions had been started. 
Since the above process took considerable time, no expenditure could be incurred. The process was 
likely to be completed by April - May 2010 and the training would start immediately. 
3.13.5 With regard to the scheme of “Research  & Development”, the Committee was informed by the 
Ministry of Home Affairs that the main reasons fo r under-utilization of funds (Rs.3 crore in BE 2009-
10) were that out of 10 Projects approved under th is Scheme, four Research & Development Study 
were being carried out and Rs. 47 lakh had been released for this purpose and a grant of Rs. 1.5 crore 
to Punjab and West Bengal for construction of Model Police Station was being processed in IFD and 
likely to be released shortly. 
3.13.6  The Committee was further informed that in order to make the BPR&D more effective and 
innovative, the Ministry had sanctioned 72 additional posts which include 07 DIGs, 07 Principal 
Scientific Officers and 06 SP level officers. 
3.13.7  BPR&D is the premier police research organi zation which has the pivotal responsibility 
of monitoring the training needs and quality of  training in States. However, the Committee 
expresses its displeasure over th e fact that out of Rs. 20 crore allocation, only Rs. 2.25 crore 
could be spent during the year 2009-10. Though th e Ministry has tried to  explain, the reasons, 
the Committee is not convinced with those ex planations. The Committee therefore strongly 
recommends that the “Training Intervention” scheme be implemented without further 
procrastination. The Committee also recomme nds that the projects relating to BPR&D 
headquarters, Central Detective Training Schools at Gandhinagar and Lucknow and Central 
Academy for Police Training (CAPT), Bhopal shou ld be completed as per the current targets. 
Being the premier police research organization, BPR&D needs more staff. The additional posts 
recently sanctioned should be filled up without delay. 
3.14  Redressal of grievances of CISF Direct Recruit Officers 
3.14.1 From 1987 onwards, the CISF had chosen to recruit Group A Officers through the Civil 
Services Examination, conducted by the UPSC. These officers claimed parity in service conditions 
vis-a-vis organized Central Services Group A and contended that grave injustice had been done on 
being denied the same in CISF as, the day they were allotted CISF by the DoPT, they were prohibited 
to join organized Group A Central Services like IRS, IC&CES, IA&AS, IRTS, IRAS, etc (even after 
being selected for these services) by the applic ation of Rules 4 and 17 of the Civil Services 
Examination Rules, which were in vogue from 1988 to 1996. 
3.14.2  Replying to the query of the Committee regarding the steps taken for redressal of grievances of 
the CISF Direct recruit Group A Officers, the Ministry of Home Affairs stated as under:- 
“Since 1999 Government has constituted two Committees (Nikhil Kumar Committee in 1999) 
and (Shri A.K. Bhandari Committee in 2001) to ex amine issues relating to grievances of Group 
‘A’ Officers. Based on the recommendations of the Committee, the following steps have been 
taken :- 

(i) Liberal deputation policy is followed; during the years 1999 to 2003, 15 Group’ A’ 
Officers were sent on deputation. 
(ii) Eligibility period for grant of Senior Time  Scale pay has been reduced from six to four 
years. 
(iii) Cadre restructuring has been implemented in 2004 wherein an additional 85 Group’ A’ 
posts have been created. 
3.14.3  The Committee, while noting the steps take n by the Government in regard to the 
redressal of grievances of Group’ A’ CISF Officers, expresses its concern that despite the 
recommendations made in 54th, 59th, 83rd Reports of this Committee, the Nikhil Kumar 
Committee and Bhandari Committee recommendation s and the few steps taken by MHA, the 
grievances of Group ‘A’ officers of CISF remai n unresolved. There has been a severe attrition 
amongst these officers and the rest are totally demoralized. The very fact that many of them 
have been petitioning the auth orities for the redressal of thei r grievances is a pointer for 
immediate action on the part of the Government. 
3.14.4  The Committee has learnt that MHA has iden tified substantial cumulative vacancies in the IPS 
in most of the State and is finalizing schemes for recruiting serving officers from the Central Para-
military Forces to fill those vacancies. In view of  this development, precipitated by the demands for 
national security concerns, the Committee wanted to know from MHA if it. would re-examine the 
long pending grievances of CISF Direct Recruit Offi cers including the feasibility of filling a fraction 
of vacancies in IPS, by the Direct Recruit Officer s of CISF, in conformity with recommendation of 
this Committee. 
3.14.5 In response to the query of the Committee, the Home Secretary made the following submission 
before the Committee in its sitting held on 23rd March, 2010: 
“Sir, we have this proposal now before us, on direct recruitment, both from the State Police 
Services as well as from CPMFs into the IPS. A scheme has been made and we have sent it to 
the UPSC. The UPSC is not in favour of it. We are now going to take a decision basically to 
overrule the UPSC and go ahead with the scheme, subject to the approval of the Prime Minister. 
It would then be implemented, Sir” 
3.14.6 On being enquired by the Chair, whether the Ministry would also include CISF in the scheme, 
the Home Secretary replied that it would include all CPMFs. In reply to a query of the Committee, the 
Ministry has replied that CISF would also be eligible under the proposed scheme. 
3.14.7 The Committee urges upon the Government to implement the scheme at the earliest so 
that the outstanding grievances of the Direct Recruit Officers could be addressed substantially. 
3.15  Allocation of BADP and HADP 
3.15.1 The unresolved issue of  transferring the Border Area Development Programme (BADP) and 
Hill Area Development Programme (HADP) in NE Region between MHA and DoNER has been 
engaging Committee’s attention for the last few year s. In terms of the Allo cation of Business Rules, 
the work relating to the Border Area Developmen t Programme (BADP) has been allocated to the 
Department of Border Management, Ministry of Home Affairs and the Ministry of Development of 
North Eastern Region (DoNER). While the Department of Border Management, Ministry of Home 

Affairs has been allocated BADP (excluding the subject allocated to DoNER), the subject matter of 
HAQP and BADP pertaining to NE Region falls  under the purview of DoNER. The MHA has 
submitted that the Planning Commission had desired that the work relating to BADP should be 
handled by MHA in respect of all the border States including the North Eastern States, in view of its 
reach to borders and pr esence of its manpower i.e. Border Guarding Forces and capacity to supervise 
the works in border areas. It was further stated that handling of BADP by two different Ministries may 
not be desirable as it would be impendent for its effective implementation. It would also be desirable 
from the practical and administrative point of view that one Ministry/Department should handle 
BADP. Multiplicity of agencies in handling BADP  would adversely affect the implementation of the 
Programme. 
3.15.2  Keeping this in view, the Cabinet Secretariat was requested to amend the Allocation of 
Business Rules who in turn advised that MHA may send a proposal after obtaining the approval of the 
Minister In-charge of Ministry of DoNER and Ministry of Defence. While Ministry of Defence has 
given its consent, Ministry of DoNER has not agreed to the proposal of MHA so far. 
3.15.3 The Committee is of the view that Government should finally arrive at a decision without 
further delay, with regard to the allocation of BADP and HADP which has remained unresolved 
between Ministry of Home Affa irs and Ministry of DoNER, fo r better implementation of the 
schemes. 
3.16   Procurement of Bullet Proof Jackets 
3.16.1 The issue of procurement of Bullet Proof Jackets for the Para-Military Forces came up for brief 
discussion in the Committee on 22nd March, 2010. It was pointed out by a Member that the right 
terminology for the jackets is not being used as there is no jacket which could be bullet proof. It was 
suggested that the term ‘Body Armour’ could be a better expression. 
3.16.2 The Ministry in its response has replied that the name ‘Bullet Proof Jacket’ is slightly 
misnomer and instead, it should be called ‘Bullet Resistant Jacket’ or ‘Body Armour’ because no 
Bullet Proof Jacket can be 100% bullet proof agains t all types of bullets at all times. Depending upon 
the level of protection of the jacket for which it has been designed, it will provide resistance against 
piercing of the bullets. 
3.16.3  The Quality Requirements (QRs) of Light Weight Bullet Proof Jackets, notified by Ministry of 
Home Affairs for use of Central Para Military Forces were prepared by a Committee under the 
Chairmanship of DG, BPR&D and members from all the CPMFs as well as from DRDO and IIT, 
Delhi it is a Level III protection jacket and provides resistance against the following weapons:- 
(i)  7.62 mm SLRs 
(ii) 7.62 mm x 39 mm AK-47 Rifles with soft lead core ammunition and not against hard steel 
core ammunition 
(iii) 9 mm Carbine/ Pistols 
3.16.4  The QRs of BP Jackets notified by MHA does not provide protection against hard steel core 
ammunition being manufactured by OFB. In order to provide protection against this ammunition, the 
protection level of the jacket has to be of level IV category and the specifications are being developed 
by BPR&D and the next procurement will be against these specifications.” 

3.16.5  The Committee is of the considered view that the level of bo dy armour provided to the 
Central para-military forces should be raised to level IV category. The Ministry should make 
concerted efforts to ensure that the next proc urement takes place on time as per level IV 
category, so that those could be used by our fo rces in combat operation s. It should also be 
ensured that the armour is made available to all the personnel in the combat zone. 
3.17  Training of Police Personnel 
3.17.1 Training of Police Personnel is an essential ingredient for their efficient and effective 
functioning. The importance of police training is re cognized by the authorities. Apart from the Sardar 
Vallabhai Patel National Police Academy, Hyderabad, there are 62 Training Institutes of Central 
Police Forces and 170 Police Training Institutes of the States. These centres ha ve been designated as 
Centres of Excellence, imparting training in specialised skills, not only to the personnel of the CPFs, 
but also to the personnel of the State Police forces. As per available information, 4968 indoor and 
4600 outdoor instructors are available in the States’ Police Force and CPMFs/CPOs. 
3.17.2 The Ministry has informed that the police pe rsonnel of States/UTs/CPO s are also sent abroad 
for training to acquaint themselves with m odem techniques of crime prevention, detection, 
investigation, anti-terrorism combats, etc. These courses have been organized in countries like Japan, 
Singapore, USA, Italy, etc. With the help of the officers receiving training abroad, courses are 
replicated in India to have a multiplier effect. 
3.17.3 The Committee was also informed that as pe r available information 330 Indian Officers have 
been trained in Training of Trainers Course conducted at ‘Centre of Excellence for Stability Police 
Unit, Vicenza, Italy’. During the last five years, 940 Police Officers have been trained in various 
courses under Anti- Terrorist Assistance programme of US Government. During the year 2008-09 an 
amount of Rs. 754.33 crore has been spent on training of CPMFs. 
3.17.4 With regard to number of people required to be trained every year in each force, the Ministry 
submitted that as per the draft National Policy on Police Training, a minimum of 8.5 per cent of the 
total strength of the States’ Police should get training in a year, as per a BPR&D study. 
3.17.5 Information on the number of personnel needed to be trained in respect of each CPMF, was not 
made available to the Committee. 
3.17.6 In response to a query that the allocation of Rs. 25 lakh to each CPMF for Education and 
Training appeared to be grossly inadequate, the Mi nistry clarified that the budgetary provision under 
the Scheme of Police Education and Training, which was Rs. 25 lakh in respect of each CPMF, does 
not reflect allocation given to them for training purpose. It was further clarified that the budgetary 
provisions for training of CP MFs was given mainly from the re spective non-plan budget of each 
CPMF. The allocation given under Police Education and Training only supplement the main budget. 
3.17.7 In reply to a question about training slots made available by the BSF, ITBP and NSG during the 
last five years in commando training to the States and to other CPMFs, information was furnished by 
the Ministry as per the following table:-
