3.17.8  The current security scenario of the country be ing what it is, the urgency to have Quick 
Response Teams and Commandos in State Police and Central Police Organisations needs hardly 
to be emphasized. In fact during the meetin g of the Committee held on 23 March, 2010, the 
Home Secretary informed that the suggestion that all forces should have Quick Response Teams 
and Commandos right across the States, has been accepted. 
3.17.9  The total strength of the police force in all the States and UTs as on 30th September, 2009 
stood at 15,04,153 which was projected to go up to 15,81,439 by 31st March, 2010. The total 
strength of the CPMFs as on 1st April, 2009, was 7,79,552
@. As against this massive force, the 
infrastructure in terms of four training institut es offering commando tr aining with only 4270 
slots available during the last five years, as info rmed by the Ministry of Home Affairs, present a 
dismal picture of the police forces to fight t errorist, insurgents and left wing extremists, who 
have no dearth of latest arms and weapons, who are highly trained in guerrilla warfare and 
highly politically and ideologically motivated. 
3.17.10 The Committee believes that in addition to th e information furnished by the Ministry of 
Home Affairs to it, there would be more specialized  training centres in some States, particularly 
the Naxal affected States, to impart commando training to th e forces, but their numbers would 
be miniscule as compared to the need of training of 8.5% of the total st rength of the State Police 
Forces. While surfing the net, the Committee came  across the information relating to a training 
centres for jungle warfare located in Kank er (Chhattisgarh) and another training centre 
Chandaka (Orissa) and yet anoth er recently set up by Government of Maharashtra to train the 
recently raised Force One battalion of Maharashtra Police. 

3.17.11 The Committee also learnt th at the Central Government proposed to set up an 
Advanced Training Centre for CRPF Commandos in Uttar Pradesh. The Government of Bihar 
is also reported to have plan to  set up four institutes to impart  training in counter insurgency 
and jungle warfare. 
3.17.12  Be that as it may, the Committee, therefore, with all the emphasis at its command, urges 
upon the Ministry of Home Affairs to take this aspect of co mmando training seriously and 
reformulate its Policy on Education and Training of Central Police Forces with a view to 
ensuring that adequate number of police personnel, especially  trained in commando, counter-
insurgency, anti-terrorist and guerrilla warfare, are available to counter the menace of 
terrorism, insurgency and left wing extremism in all their hues. Besides, the inadequacies in 
training infrastructure, the Committee has been given to understand that there is shortage of 
trainers and the Government shou ld look into this aspect by  recruiting sufficient number of 
trainers and also updation of the Training of Trainers programme. Th e Central Government 
may also proactively assists the State Governme nt particularly those affected by cross-border 
terrorism, insurgency and left wing extremism, in  setting up Training Centres for training their 
police personnel in guerilla a nd jungle warfare and help them  set up special units like the 
Greyhound of Andhra Pradesh Police and COBRA of CRPF. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

CHAPTER - IV 
NAXALISM 
4.1   Naxalism is not merely a law and order problem - it has acquired deep-rooted socio-economic 
dimensions - and the violence caused by naxalites has become an area of foremost concern for internal 
security. The situation is very well explained by the statement of the Prime Minister made in the Chief 
Ministers’ Conference held on 20 December, 2007, in which he described naxal menace as a ‘virus’ 
and the single biggest threat to India’s security. 
4.1.1 According to the Report (2008) submitted by the Expert Group set up by the Planning 
Commission under the Chairmanship of Shri D. Bandopadhyaya, on development issues to deal with 
the causes of Discontent, Unrest and Extremism, the Ministry of Home Affairs has admitted the 
spread of the naxal movement across 76 districts in nine States of the country. The said Report has 
also indicated that according to the Institute for Conflict Management, at least, 165 districts in 14 
States of the country are affected by various levels of Maoist mobilization and violence. 
4.1.2 The Home Secretary, during the course of his presentation before the Committee, admitted that 
the incidents and killings were on the increase and it was nearing the peak level of 1971. It was also 
stated that 11 States were witnessing naxal violence and mobilization was being done in eight other 
States, which could translate into violence in due course. 
4.1.3 The Ministry, in the background note submitted to the Committee, further stated that there was 
an increase in the number of naxal incidents by 42.96 per cent; number of civilian casualties by 7.57 
per cent and number of casualties of security forces by 42.86 per cent in the year 2009 as compared to 
the period from January 01 to August 31, 2008. On the other hand, there was a decrease in the number 
of naxalites neutralized, by 2.90 per cent while the number of naxalites arrested increased by 5.39 per 
cent in 2009 as compared to 2008 in the same period. Table showing State-wise extent of naxal 
violence during 2005 to 2009 (upto August) (Annexure- I). 
4.1.4 In the wake of the recent spurt of naxalite violence in the country, there can be no two opinion 
that it is the single largest threat to our internal security. Left-Wing Extremism also constitutes gave 
threat to nation’s economic development and growth due to the reported fact that the Maoists have 
spread their influence in large part of the country. Another area of serious concern is that the country’s 
substantial geological wealth, forest resources and agrarian land is concentrated in the left-wing 
extremism affected States of Andhra Pradesh, Orissa, Bihar, Maharashtra, Madhya Pradesh, 
Chhattisgarh which seriously impedes economic growth and poverty alleviation. These problems get 
aggravated as the arterial netw ork of the country’s surface transp ort network passes through these 
regions. 
4.2  Genesis 
4.2.1 The Left-Wing Extremist (LWE) movement, a radical form of communism, advocates armed 
struggle and elimination of ‘class enemies’ in its bid to capture power. This movement is over forty 
years old and has undergone a metamorphosis. In 1967 ‘Naxalites’ organized the All India 
Coordination Committee of Communist Revolution aries (AICCCR), and later broke away from CPI 
(M). Uprisings were organized in several parts of the country. In  1969 AICCCR gave birth to the 
Communist Party of India (Marxist Leninist). But, pretty soon the naxal movement had to bear with 

two major jolts, one was the revolt of Satyanarayan Singh in 1971 and the second was the death of 
Charu Mazumdar, the very next year. These two incidents caused fragmentation in the ranks of 
naxalism.
# 
4.2.2  During the 1970s, the movement was fragmented into several disputing factions. By 1980 it was 
estimated that around 30 Naxalite groups were active, with a combined membership of 30,000. But 
the significant fact is that almost all the Naxalite  groups trace their origin to the CPI (ML). The two 
strongest ones, namely, PWG and M CC merged to form of the Communi st Party of India (Maoist) in 
2004.
$ 
4.3  Naxal Strategies and Impact 
4.3.1 The Left-Wing Extremists believe that the innate structural inequality of Indian society can only 
be redressed by the violent overthrow of the In dian State. The Committee views with concern the 
statement reportedly made by the Home Secretary rece ntly that the Maoists are planning to overthrow 
the Indian State by 2050 or 2060. According to media reports and as per the oral evidence of the 
Home Secretary before the Committee, after every attack, the Maoists would do a post-mortem and 
analysis. The analysis is as good as one made by the, armed force of a country. According to the 
Home Secretary, the Maoists are highly motivated, and highly trained. He also conceded before the 
media that stamping out of the menace was going to be a long bloody war. 
4.3.2  The Left-Wing Extremism s cenario in the country is characterized by growing spatial spread, 
increased intensity of violence, targeted attacks on the police and alleged police informers, rapid 
militarization and stepped-up efforts at mass mobilization, posing a grave threat to the national 
security. The Ministry of Home Affairs agreed before the Committee that the incidents of naxalite 
violence increased following the merger of the Communist Party of India (Marxist-Leninist) and 
Maoist Communist Centre in 2004, thus forming the Communist Party of India (Maoist). The CPI 
(Maoist) is the pre-dominant Left-Wing Extremist outfit that has engaged in a ‘protracted’ war against 
the Government apparatus in various States. Accord ing to the Ministry, the outfit is the most potent 
group in terms of spread, strength and violence prof ile, accounting for nearly 90 per cent of all naxal 
violence. The strategies of the naxalites include exploiting the real and perceived grievances of the 
people, particularly in the backward/underdevelope d areas, and thereby attempt to create a support 
base for themselves. They attack the police forces and other institutions of governance to project them 
as weak and ineffective. Attempts  are also made to revive the movement and create a mass base by 
exploiting issues such as land acquisition for large projects, etc. The naxalites resist developmental 
activities, by taking up issues of displacement due to land acquisition for large developmental projects 
and Special Economic Zones (SEZs) and also use their frontal civil society organizations for such 
resistance. The Ministry of Home Affairs informed the Committee that  the recent trend of activities 
inter alia  include attacks on political leadership of the concerned areas and on the security forces, 
targeting key economic infrastructure such as bridges, railway lines/stations and communication 
towers used for mobile telephony, and also several public utility buildings such as Primary Health 
Centres (PHCs), schools, panchayat bhawans, forest buildings, etc. In the recent past, they had killed 
ex-naxalites and others opposed to them, branding them ‘police informers’.  
4.3.3  In the not too distant past, Maoists were also found to be involved in incidents of jailbreaks in 
the States of Bihar and Chhattisgarh. The biggest strike ever by Naxalites which rocked the nation 
when hundreds of uniformed militants of the CPT (Maoist) struck Jehanabad District jail with crude 

bombs, dynamites and landmines in November, 2005. They blew up the Jehanabad Police Station, and 
stormed the jail, freeing more than 300 prisoners, including many fellow guerrillas. The rebels entered 
the town in small groups, cut off power, and killed over a dozen people, including three policemen. 
This incident showed their growing strength in the State.  
4.3.4  It may also be recalled here that on 15 Apr il, 2006 armed cadres belonging to CPI (Maoist) had 
attacked the Narayanpur sub-jail in the Bastar region  of Chhattisgarh using explosives. In the same 
way, in the incident of jailbreak at the Dantewada jail a total of 299 inmates, including 110 Naxal 
activists, fled from the jail in Chhattisgarh on 16 December, 2007. 
4.3.5  The naxals are also involved in political killings, the latest being the brutal killing of JMM MP 
Sunil Mahato and former Chief Minister, Babulal Marandi’s son Anup Marandi at Jamshedpur in 
Jharkhand sometime ago. 
4.3.6  When the Government of Chhattisgarh was asked to explain the incidents of jail break by the 
Maoists and strategies adopted to stop such incidents in future, the State Government had replied as 
under:–– 
“Naxalites in-mates of Dantewada jail captured the jail security from inside the jail and fled 
away. There was no failure of intelligence; general intelligence input regarding threat to jails 
was there. But, security framework of the jail was not up to the mark. Government had 
suspended jail authorities and disciplinary actions against erring officers are being taken. One 
platoon of armed police has been deployed at each jail where Naxalite under-trials are kept.” 
4.3.7  The Committee also noted that the Government of India on 22.06.2009 included Communist 
Party of India (Maoist) in the list of terrorist organizations banned under the Unlawful Activities 
(Prevention) Act, 1967. 
4.3.8  The strategies of the Maoists can be better  explained in the words of Home Secretary, who 
submitted before the Committee, as under: 
“They are basically in three different levels. Ei ght thousand is roughly the armed militant 
cadre. They call it ‘naxal army’. Then, they have got the second level which is called ‘Dalam’ or 
groups which are roughly about twenty thousand. So, they have about twenty thousand in 
Dalam. Then, of course, they use villagers, in many cases, either for cutting down trees at a 
particular time when they don’t want forces to come or they use them as observers. That is, of 
course, a much larger number because they take people along.” 
4.3.9 Some of the recent major naxalite strikes against the State establishment took the authorities by 
surprise. When the Ministry was posed with a querry on this aspect, it submitted as below: 
“Some incidents of naxalite attacks have an element of Intelligence failure. Attention is being 
given to strengthen the intelligence capabilities and capacities, with emphasis on intelligence-
based precise police operations, adherence to standing operating procedures (SOP) and 
immediate retaliation in the event of attack. States have been asked to take necessary steps to 
strengthen their special branches. IB-assisted Special Branch sub-plans have been formed as 
part of the Annual Plans for 2007-08 of Bihar, Chhattisgarh, Jharkhand and Orissa under the 
MPF Scheme.” 

4.3.10  There, are reports of naxalities having established their links with the terrorist organizations, 
separatist groups, ISI in Pakistan and Maoist in Nepal for modernizing themselves. However, 
according to the Ministry of Home Affairs:–– 
“There are no specific inputs available to indicate such operational links. The Central 
Government, however, maintains a close and continuous watch on the situation. There is no 
specific evidence available to indicate that the naxalities in India have operational links with the 
Maoists in Nepal. However, the Government is taking steps on a continuous basis for 
strengthening the arrangements for border management. In addition, there is close and 
continuous interaction with the authorities in Nepal at the bi-lateral level through various 
institutional mechanisms such as the Joint Working Group on Border Management, Home 
Secretary level talks, etc., in which security concerns of both sides are discussed. The Indo-
Nepal border is guarded by the Sashastra Seema Bal (SSB) and the State Police Forces.” 
4.3.11 The Home Secretary while replying to a que ry of the Members of the Committee, stated as 
under:–– 
“As far as external support is concerned, we don’t have any direct evidence of any foreign 
support for the Maoists groups. We know that the Maoists groups have been in touch with some 
of the other militant groups in different parts of the country like NSCN(I-M), ULFA, etc. They 
have talks with them in an effort to widen the movement. But, we don’t have any evidence of 
direct outside support. This, in one sense, is so far a fully home grown and very clever and 
intelligent movement.” 
4.4  Causes 
4.4.1 The Committee is concerned that in spite of several steps taken by Government, the movement 
is spreading to different parts. In fact, according to some reports and also as per the statistics furnished 
by the Ministry of Home Affairs, the movement is spreading to new areas. The naxalite ideology is 
flawed and their formulations are irrelevant. They are able to mobilize people in certain areas in view 
of the lethargy in local administration and long pending grievances of the people and also lack of 
effective co-ordination between Centre and States. 
4.4.2  The main socio-economic problems like poverty, land related problems, unemployment, 
problems of resettlement and rehabilitation are taken advantage by the Maoists in certain areas 
particularly in tribal inhabited areas. 
4.5  Governmental Initiatives 
4.5.1 Governments, both Central and States concerned, have been taking several measures to quell the 
menace of naxalism. In this context, the Committee takes note of the statement made by the Prime 
Minister during the Chief Minister’s Conference on Internal Security held on 7th February, 2010 in 
which he stated that the State Governments should ensure that re-establishment of the civil 
administration is quickly followed by implementation of development and welfare schemes. 
4.5.2  When the Ministry was asked to explain the steps taken by the Government to curb the menace 
of Maoist activities, the Ministry of Home Affairs stated that the Government had adopted an 
integrated approach in dealing wi th LWE activities in the arenas of  security, development and public 
perception. Basically, the State Governments would deal with various issues related to naxalite 

activities in the States, and the Central Government would supplement their e fforts over a wide range 
of schemes. It was also stated that the naxal situation was regularly reviewed by the Union Home 
Minister and several meetings with Chief Ministers of naxal-affected States had been held. In order to 
generate a degree of consensus among the affected States in the approach and strategy to counter the 
naxal problem, various institutional mechanisms had been established, which include, inter alia , 
Coordination Centre under the Chairmanship of Union Home Secretary, a Task Force on inter-State 
Coordination headed by the Special Secretary, Internal Security. 
4.5.3   According to the Ministry of Home Affairs, the Government’s approach was to deal with LWE 
activities in a holistic manner, in the areas of security, development, administration and public 
perception. In dealing with this decades-old problem, it had been felt appropriate, after various high-
level deliberations and interactions with the State Governments concerned, that an integrated approach 
aimed at the relatively more affected areas would deliver results. With this in view, a detailed analysis 
of the spread and trends in respect of LWE violence had been made and thirty-three affected districts 
in eight States had been taken up for special attention on planning, implementation and monitoring of 
development schemes. The Committee was also informed that within these thirty three districts, eight 
most affected districts in four States, namely, Bi har, Chhattisgarh, Jharkhand and Orissa, had been 
taken up for implementation of integrated security and development action plans. 
4.5.4  The Ministry of Home Affairs further stated that a number of review and monitoring 
mechanisms had been established in the context of the different aspects of the LWE situation, and the 
measures needed to deal with it. These include: 
z A Standing Committee of Chief Ministers of concerned States, under the Chairmanship of 
Union Home Minister, to work out a coordinated policy and specific measures to deal 
with the LWE problem on political, security and development fronts. 
z In furtherance of the approach of dealing w ith the LWE problem in an integrated manner, 
a high-level Task Force under Cabinet Secretary has been formed for promoting 
coordinated efforts across a range of development and security measures. 
z A Coordination Centre chaired by the Union Home Secretary to review and coordinate the 
efforts of the concerned State Governments, where the State Governments are represented 
by Chief Secretaries and Directors General of Police. 
z A Task Force under Special Secretary (Internal Security) in the Ministry of Home Affairs, 
with senior officers from intelligence agencies, central paramilitary forces and State police 
forces, to deliberate upon the operational steps needed to deal with the LWE activities and 
bring about coordination between authorities of different State, as may be necessary: 
z An Inter Ministerial Group (IMG), headed by Additional Secretary (Naxal Management), 
in the Ministry, with officers from development Ministries and Planning Commission, to 
oversee effective implementation of development schemes in LWE affected areas for 
accelerated socio-economic development. 
4.5.5  The Home Secretary, during the oral ev idence before the Committee, stated that the 
Government was formulating a plan in consultation with the State Governments. This time they had 
held a separate meeting with the seven Chief Ministers of the naxal-affected areas; wide ranging 

discussions took place and there was a broad consensu s on the way they should proceed further. He 
assured the Committee that the Government would go ahead in a concerted manner. 
4.5.6  The Ministry also stated that the Government had taken some major initiatives to deal with the 
menace of Naxalism and, after wide-ranging consulta tions with the Naxal-affected States, approved a 
joint action plan to kick off coordinated and combined action, especially at the bi-junctions and tri-
junctions of the affected States. A Conference of the Chief Ministers on Internal Security, chaired by 
the Prime Minister, was also held on August 17, 2009 and February 7, 2010, in which the internal 
security situation was deliberated in detail, and ar eas and measures requiring priority attention were 
identified. A meeting with the Chief Ministers and senior officers of naxal-affected States of Bihar, 
Jharkhand, Orissa and West Bengal, was also held after the Chief Minister’s conference on February 
9, 2010 at Kolkata, which was chaired by the Union Home Minister. 
z The Committee was also informed that the Central Government also supplements the 
efforts and resources of the States to effect ively deal with the naxal menace in a number 
of ways. 
4.5.7   The Ministry also stated that special efforts were being made to give focused attention to 
development activities in identified thirty four comp aratively more affected districts in certain States 
and under the aegis of the Task Force under the Cabinet Secretary, a list of 69 schemes of 20 
development Ministries had been identified, with a view to giving a focussed attention to their 
implementation in these districts. The Ministry of  Road Transport and Highways had also prepared a 
comprehensive road requirement plan in respect of these 34 focus districts in consultation with the 
State Governments, which was being finalized for implementation in the respective States. 
4.5.8  According to the Ministry of Home Affairs, the approach of the Government was to ensure 
governance and effective delivery of socio-economic development programmes, coupled with 
focussed and coordinated action to ensure a secure environment. The public perception management 
was intended to project the role of the State as we ll as to educate the impressionable youth about the 
true agenda of the naxalites and to wean them aw ay from the effect of the naxal propaganda and its 
ideological appeal by improving security environment and critical services like health, education, etc. 
4.5.9 The Committee was also informed that naxal-affected States were taking measures to fill up the 
vacancies in their police force; increasing police budget; fortifying and modernizing police 
stations/outposts; raising special forces trained in counter insurgency and jungle warfare on the pattern 
of Greyhounds of Andhra Pradesh; strengthen ing Special Intelligence Branches; creating 
infrastructure for training facilities commensurate with their needs. The Ministry, ho wever, confessed 
that much more was needed to be done before the States were fully prepared to tackle the naxalites 
threat. 
4.5.10  The Ministry of Home Affairs further stated that the Ministry of Rural Development had 
decided to prepare a 3-year perspective plan for covering all eligible habitations under Pradhan Mantri 
Gram Sadak Yojana (PMGSY) and the State Governments had been requested to identify and 
prioritize unconnected habitations having population of 500 and above in plain areas and 250 and 
above in tribal areas, for preparing detailed project reports. The Ministry of Tribal Affairs also moved 
a proposal for providing 100 per cent Central assistance for Ashram Schools in naxalite affected 
districts, in place of the existing provision of 50 per cent Central assistance on 50: 50 matching basis. 

A similar proposal for 100 per cent Central assi stance for cost of construction of Hostels for 
Scheduled Tribes in naxalite affected districts had also been moved. 
4.5.11  When the Ministry was asked about the effectiveness of the multi pronged approach and 
strategy adopted to deal with the problem in the naxal-affected States, the Committee was unhappy 
with the vague response of the Ministry that the overall impact of the multi-pronged and holistic 
strategy could only be seen and observed over a period of time. In the same genre, the Ministry 
declared that as a result of the various initiatives already taken and the continuing regular reviews, the 
overall situation would progressively improve. 
4.5.12  In response to a query regarding obstacles being faced by the Government in evolving a 
comprehensive plan to tackle the problem at national level, the Ministry replied that some of the 
problems and difficulties being encountered, inter alia , relate to effective utilization of resources 
available under various schemes and their implementation on the ground; inadequacies in terms of the 
strength of the local police and related infrastructure; shortage/deficiencies of staff of various 
departments at the cutting-edge level in certain States; coordination between different States in actual 
anti-naxalite operations; critical gaps in infrastructure, which did not get covered under any specific 
schemes, etc. The Ministry added that all those issues were sought to be addressed in a focussed 
manner in the various initiatives that had been/were being taken by the Central Government in close 
coordination with the State Governments. In addition, it had also been observed, from time to time, 
that different State Governments adopted different approaches to deal with the naxalite problem. The 
Ministry, therefore, informed the Committee that efforts were being made in particular to see that 
there was uniformity in approach and policy among all the affected States in this regard. 
4.6  Strengthening of Police Force 
4.6.1 Trained to deal with criminals and law and order issues, the police are often found lacking 
preparedness and strength in dealing with such well-trained and well-equipp ed guerillas armies. A 
sense of helplessness reign among the police as they continue to suffer heavy casualties at the hands of 
the highly motivated and trained naxal cadres. The prevailing naxal problem demands a massive 
reorientation of the entire police force and their training in unconventional warfare. To fight in the 
jungles where naxal activists usually have bases, is very tough with poor communications, dense 
vegetation and little visibility. More than sophisticated weapons, it requires mental strength and 
confidence. 
4.6.2  The Ministry in its background note has mentioned the measures adopted by the Central 
Government in assisting the affected State, which inter alia include the following: 
z Deployment of CPMFs on long term basis for assisting the State Police; 
z 10 Battalions of Specialized Force trained and equipped for counter-insurgency and 
jungle-warfare operations, named as Commando Battalions for Resolute Action (CoBRA) 
are being raised as a part of the Central Reserve Police Force (CRPF); 
z 20 Counter Insurgency and Anti-Terrorist (CIAT) Schools, four per State, are being set up 
to impart specialized training to State police personnel in respect of counter insurgency, 
jungle warfare and terrorism in Assam, Bihar, Chhattisgarh, Orissa and Jharkhand; 
z 37 India reserve (IR) battalions have been sanctioned to 9 States of which 24 battalions 
have been raised; and 

z Under the Security Related Expenditure (SRE) scheme, assistan ce is provided for 
recurring expenditure relating to life insurance, training and operational needs of security 
forces, as also for naxalite s cadres who surrender in acco rdance with the surrender and 
rehabilitation policy of the concerned State Government, community policing; Security-
related infrastructure by village defense committees; and publicity material. During 2008-
09, Rs. 80 crore has been released to the States. 
4.6.3  During the presentation of the Ministry of Home Affairs before the Committee, in its sitting 
held on 16th September, 2009, the Home Secretary highlighting the problems relating to security 
forces conceded that with just ten personnel in a police station, one cannot control police station area. 
He observed that whereas Kashmir had seventy-seven battalions of CRPF, seventy battalions of BSF 
and five divisions of the Army and we were holding on; in all these five naxal-affected States, the total 
strength was not even one-tenth of what should be there. He also added that it was not possible to 
tackle the naxal problem, in one go, because for that the State needed something like two million 
policemen, to tackle the entire area. 
4.6.4  In regard to the initiatives taken to fill up vacancies in the State Police forces which stood up to 
30%, it was stated that the Central Government had been continuously urging the State Governments 
to fill up the vacancies, and this aspect was discussed in considerable detail with the State 
Governments at various levels, including meetings of the Prime Minister/Home Minister with the 
State Chief Ministers. As a result, it was observed that a number of naxalite affected States had 
initiated steps to speed up the process of filling vacancies. 
4.6.5  The Home Secretary, replying on the assistance being given to States on security front, stated 
that the States asked for a number of things. They were short of weapons and asked for weapons. They 
asked for more paramilitary forces which the Ministry did not have and State were told accordingly. 
He also stated that the States asked for night vision equipments to keep up vigilance and launch 
operations at night, mine-protected vehicles to escape the ID blast, communication equipment, 
helicopter support. He stated that the Ministry of Home Affairs would be able to provide only some of 
these items. The Central Government has posted some of the Commandant level paramilitary forces 
officers in LWE affected States and the States were  asked to send officers on deputation, as additional 
S.Ps. in the districts, so that there was better coordination between the paramilitary forces and the 
district authorities. 
4.7  Scheme for Special Infrastructure in Left Wing Extremism Affected States 
4.7.1 The Committee was given to understand that a new Centrally-sponsored scheme, Scheme for 
Special Infrastructure in Left Wing Extremism A ffected States with cent-percent funding by the 
Central Government and implementation by the concerned State Governments, was launched. Rs. 500 
crore was allocated for this Scheme over the Eleventh Plan period. Rs. 100 crore was provided for the 
year 2009-10 (Rs.30 crore at R.E.). For 2010-11, an outlay of Rs.100 crore has been provided within 
the overall allocation for the Eleventh Plan. The Scheme has following objectives: 
(i) to provide critical mobility to the police by upgrading existing roads/tracks in inaccessible 
areas; 
(ii) to provide secure camping ground and helipads at strategic locations in remote and 
interior areas; 

(iii) to provide secure police stations/outposts by strengthening those at risk of attack due to 
their dilapidated condition; 
(iv) to upgrade and strengthen a pproach roads to police stations/outposts where there is risk of 
attack by improvised explosive devices (IEDs) and landmines; and 
(v) to provide for critical needs, specific to the areas/districts where holistic anti-naxalite 
measures are being taken in a focussed manner. 
4.7.2  The Ministry stated that the benefits would be  in the arena of security . Security, in turn, would 
also provide an enabling environment for development and economic growth. 
4.8  Surrender Policy 
4.8.1 A proper surrender and rehabilitation mechanism is the most important component of dealing 
with insurgency, naxalism and any other extremist activities. It is necessary to get the surrendered, 
naxalites rehabilitated to bring them into mainstream of Indian society and check the growing 
influence of naxalites otherwise they could again turn to naxalism. It is also needed to save their life 
from the threat of the naxalite groups which they have left. 
4.8.2  In response to the query regarding ingredients of “Naxal Surrender Policy” formulated by the 
Government and the response of naxalites to the Government’s appeal to surrender and join the 
national mainstream, the Ministry informed as under:- 
“Different States have their own State-specific surrender policies in respect of naxalites, which 
are being implemented by the State Governments concerned. The Central Government 
reimburses the expenditure incurred by the State Governments at the rate of Rs.10,000/- per 
person surrendering without arms and Rs.20,000/- per person for surrendering with arms, 
based on specific proposals that may be received from the State Governments. State-wise 
information regarding the number of naxalites who have surrendered in the last three years has 
been provided below.”

4.9  Change in Government Perception 
4.9.1 The deliberations at the Chief Ministers’ Conference held in Delhi on December, 2007 witnessed 
a major departure in the approach of the Government of India towards the naxalite problem from the 
one taken at Hyderabad meet of the Chief Ministers, two years back. There, the Prime Minister had 
emphasized on a humane approach to the naxalite problem in spite of the more or less consensual 
approach of the Chief Ministers to deal with it as a law and order problem. The CMs conference held 
in December, 2007 in Delhi, he called for raising a specialized and dedicated force to tackle naxalism 
as it was a “virus” and the “single biggest” internal security challenge to the country. 
4.9.2  When the Ministry was asked about this shift in policy, it replied as under: 
“The naxalite situation and related developments are being reviewed and monitored on a 
continuous basis at various levels, inter alia with a view to identifying the specific measures and 
actions that may be required to be taken. The policy of the Government is to follow an 
integrated and holistic approach aimed at promoting socio-economic development in the 
affected areas, effective security measures and action against naxals cadres indulging in 
violence, coupled with efforts to encourage such elements to shun the path of violence and come 
into the mainstream. Raising a specialized and dedicated force to deal with the naxalite violence 
would be needed to be viewed as one part of the measures in the context of this holistic and 
multi-faceted strategy.” 
4.9.3  The Home Minister while replying a query in the Rajya Sabha on 15th July, 2009, regarding 
approach of the Government for tackling naxalism, made the following submission: 
“Our approach to facing the challenge of Left Wing Extremism is two-fold. First is the police 
action, followed by development; police action, in order to regain control over the territory, 
which is now dominated by the Left Wing Extremists. First, the State Government must be able 
to assert its control over the territory. Only when you have the control over the territory, you 
can really do development. Otherwise, the developmental activity in areas, which are controlled 
by the Left Wing Extremists, are usually demolis hed or destroyed by the extremists in a few 
days. You put up a telephone tower, it is blasted; you put up a road, it is mined; you put up a 
school building, it is demolished. There, the first thing is to regain control over the territory.” 

4.9.4  However, the Home Minister while making a statement in Rajya Sabha on 15th April, 2010 on 
the recent Dantewada massacre in which 76 secu rity personnel were killed, said the State 
Governments have the primary responsibility to fight the Maoist menace and the Centre was ready to 
assist them. 
4.10.1 It is public knowledge that the Maoists ha ve become an impediment to development 
projects in most backward regions and tribal dominated forest areas and are runni ng parallel 
administrations, siphoning money from Government funds and indulging in extortion from local 
people, contractors and others. The Maoists pr opagate that administrative and political 
institutions are inadequate; espouse local demands; take advantage of the prevalent 
dissatisfaction and feeling of injustice among ex ploited segments of the population and claim to 
form an alternative utopian Government. The Committee understands that by providing 
livelihood, land and proper wages, with good governance and proper utilization of funds 
allocated to the respective States for various  Government sponsored development schemes, 
people could be weaned away from naxalism and this menace could be contained. The 
Committee, therefore, is of the view that the a pproach to the naxalite problem needs a blend of 
firm handling with sensitive approach to the developmental aspects. Th e Government should 
also involve the political parties, people’s representatives, Panchayati Raj institutions and above 
all the masses, in all its initiatives to contain the menace. 
4.10.2  The Committee is of the view that capacity building is required to be done in the 
agriculture sector, bringing in modern technologies, so that the young people in the areas 
affected by LWE are able to take up agriculture for a gainful employment. 
4.10.3 The Committee views with concern that the you th, both educated and uneducated, in the 
LWE-affected areas do not get sufficient employment opportunities and they fall prey in the 
hands of the extremists. It is, therefore, necessary that urgent efforts are made to improve the 
skills of the youth so that  they may take up activities for gai nful employment in various sectors, 
including self-employment. Similarly, the Government should try to improve the employment 
opportunities for the youth in the affected ar eas. The Committee agrees with the view of the 
Expert Group of the Planning Co mmission that the extremists, by and large, take up issues, 
which are basically related to poverty, deprivation and alienation of the poor and the landless. 
4.10.4  It is an admitted fact that the implementati on of various schemes, particularly of the 
Ministry of Rural Development and the Ministry of Tribal Affairs,  is deficient and there is no 
proper administrative control and monitoring whic h obviously lead to dissatisfaction among the 
people and they fall easy prey to the extremists. Efforts, therefore, should be made in the 
direction of implementing the flagship program mes in a proper manner to ensure that the 
beneficiaries feel part of the mainstream of the society. 
4.10.5  The Government should identify the factors responsible for tardy implementation of the 
schemes and ensure that its benefits reach the target ed groups and they do not feel alienated. In 
this context, the Government should address the issues related to land acquisition, land-
alienation, resettlement and rehabilitation; issue pertaining to laws applic able to the scheduled 
and tribal areas, on priority. The Committee would also like to highlight the fact that in some 
States elections are not held regularly to the Panchayats and other loc al bodies, which results in 

lack of participation of peop les’ representatives in the im plementation of the flagship 
programmes. It is therefore, equally important that elections are held  regularly to the PRI 
Institutions so that public representatives are actively involved at th e grass root level in 
implementation of the schemes and projects which would directly help in bringing the people 
back to the mainstream. 
4.10.6 The areas affected by LWE are mostly in the central parts of India which has less density 
of population, more density of forest areas; le ss infrastructure facilities , including education, 
health and roads. It is, therefore, essential that  these infrastructure facilities are improved and 
developed at a much faster pace than has been projected and implemented so far. More and 
more roads are required to be built so that it  gives employment to jo bless people, including 
tribals while connecting those areas with the rest of the country. 
4.10.7  Education is the basic ingredient of human resource development. Lack of educational 
facilities in the naxal-affected areas is an im portant factor accountable for the spread of the 
movement. More and more schools with free boarding are required to be opened in the affected 
areas. Vacancies for the posts of teachers may be  filled up and to the exte nt possible, educated 
youth from those areas may be taken as teachers through a special drive. Technical and 
engineering institutions may also be opened in those areas. 
4.10.8 Lack of healthcare in the naxal-affected areas is another basic issue that leads to 
dissatisfaction among the people. Special packages  are required to be formulated for providing 
healthcare for the people in th e affected areas, both under the existing schemes as well as new 
schemes. New primary health centres and sub-centres  need to be opened and it should be made 
compulsory for the new doctors to serve in thos e areas by giving some incentives to them. 
Similarly, in some selected places, bigger hospit als are also required to be opened with all the 
facilities, including major specialities. Special e fforts are needed to be made for healthcare of 
women and children. The Government  should also explore streng thening ‘Anganwadis’ in all 
those areas with special packages. 
4.10.9  The Committee understands that most of the ha bitations in the affected areas are devoid 
of electricity, which makes them an easy have n for the extremists. Th erefore, the Government 
should start a new programme of electrifying a ll the habitations in the extremist-affected areas 
with specific targets fixed. Electricity is a basic requirement in the modern days and once this is 
available, people will be able to take up various activities for gainful employment and children 
will be able to devote their time for studies, etc. 
4.10.10 Drinking water is another basic requirement for the people. In most of the habitations in 
the extremist-affected areas, people do not have access to potable drinking water and even if it is 
available, people, particularly wo men, have to trek long distances  to obtain a pot of water. The 
Government should make use of  the existing schemes availabl e and provide drinking water 
facilities in all the habitations by the end of the Eleventh Five Year Plan. 
4.10.11  It is a fact that in naxal violence, people , from all sides, including the local people, 
extremists as well as the security forces lose th eir lives. Though the multi- pronged development 
strategy is required to be planne d and 
 implemented, it should al so be kept in one’s mind that 
violence also needs to be addre ssed. Every one knows that people in the affected areas do not 
support the movement by their heart but circu mstances, coercion a nd the under-development 

make them surrender to the movement. Therefore, while taking up developmental activities, the 
strategies, as per the area and location, will have  to be adopted to counter the violent activities. 
One particular strategy will not help for a ll the areas and States. There needs to be a 
coordination between the Central para-militar y forces and the State s ecurity organizations. 
While preparing strategy for such operations by security forces, sufficient training should be 
given before sending them to the field for facing the extremists. In fact, the local youth are the 
right persons, who know the topography of the areas. Therefore, local youth on a large scale 
should be involved through empl oyment in the police forces and CPMFs for operations within 
those areas to resist any attack from the extremis ts. Police force should be  modernized in terms 
of latest and modern weaponry, communication, mobility and infrastructure. They should be 
supplied more Mine Protected Vehicles and th e Central Para Military Forces should be 
deployed on long-term basis. 
4.10.12  The Committee also feels that  the Central Para Military Forces trained in guerilla 
warfare should be deployed in all the sensitive di stricts of the affected areas. There should be 
more Counter Insurgency and Jungle Warfare (CIJW) Colleges opened  on the lines of 
Vairengte in Mizoram and another training centr e at Kanker in Chhattis garh, for training of 
Police Personnel for counter-insurgency operations. The Co mmittee understands that the 
biggest limitation while conducting  counter-naxal operations, is th e lack of helicopter support 
from the Government. The G overnment should therefore pr ovide helicopter support for 
conducting operations and for reconnaisance. 
4.10.13 The Committee feels that the incidents of jail break and attack on police establishments 
are direct challenges to the authority of the State and if the jails a nd other law enforcement 
mechanisms are prone to naxalit e attack, then nothing can be treated as secure and safe. The 
Committee recommends that the Central Governme nt, in close and active coordination with 
naxal-affected State Government s should review and strengthen th e security of sensitive jails, 
especially where naxalites or activists are lodged, to prevent recurrence of such incidents in 
future. The Committee understands that the incide nts of attacks and jail breaks reflect the 
inadequacies of intelligence agencies. Therefore, intelligence network in the naxal-affected areas 
should therefore be strengthened. 
4.10.14 The Committee is of the considered  view that the justice deliv ery system particularly in 
the lower judiciary should be revamped to reve rse the dangerous trend of justice dispensation 
by the so called ‘peoples courts’. 
4.10.15 Money is the life-line of every extremist acti vity. Therefore, all the channels from which 
the naxalites get funding, should be blocked. There should be a proper mechanism to investigate 
and check the extortion rackets being run by the naxalites and to punish all those who are found 
guilty of funding naxalites. 
4.10.16  The Committee feels that the approach of the Government for dealing with the naxalites 
should be consistent, holistic, multifaceted and collective. There  can be no two opinions that 
naxalism has become the “single biggest” threat to the country’s security. Th
 e Committee is of 
the view that the fight against naxalism should be at the developmental,  political and law and 
order fronts. The Committee also takes note of the statement made by the Home Minister while 
replying a Question in the Raj ya Sabha on 15th July, 2009 in which he conceded that the 
Government underestimated the challenge of le ft wing extremism for years. The Committee, 

therefore, is of the considered vi ew that the Government should take this menace seriously and 
make every effort to contain it. 
4.10.17  The Committee agrees with the policy of the G overnment that talks with the naxalites 
can be held only when they surrender their arms and abjure violence. The Committee thinks 
that the Government should reque st all the naxal aff ected States to’ implement the “surrender-
cum-rehabilitation” scheme fo r the naxalites who want to give up violence and join the 
mainstream of the society and the Centre should  provide assistance to the State Governments 
for this scheme. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

CHAPTER- V 
UNION TERRITORIES WITHOUT LEGISLATURE 
5.1  The Demands of the Ministry are divided into two volumes - Volume-I (Demand Nos. 51- 55) 
and Volume -II (Demand Nos. 95 - 99). Volume-II is related to the Union Territories without 
legislature. The entire Budget of the Union Territo ries (without Legislature) on the Plan as well as 
Non-Plan side is reflected in the Demands for Grants of the Ministry of Home Affairs. 
5.1.1 Though provision for the Union Territories with out Legislature form part of the Demands of the 
Ministry of Home Affairs, it is the subject matter of the Ministries which are responsible for 
scrutinizing, sanctioning and implementing the schemes/projects falling under their purview within 
the Plan and Non-Plan ceilings indicated under the concerned Ministries in the respective Demands of 
the UTs (without legislature). 
5.1.2  The total allocation for the Ministry of Home Affairs for the year 2010-2011 is Rs. 42376.66 
crore, out of which, Rs. 5240.59 crore have been a llocated to the UTs without legislatures under the 
following five Demands: 
(i) Demand No. 95 - Andaman and Nicobar Islands; 
(ii) Demand No. 96 - Chandigarh; 
(iii) Demand No. 97 - Dadra and Nagar Haveli;  
(iv) Demand No. 98 - Daman and Diu; and  
(v) Demand No. 99 - Lakshadweep. 
5.1.3 The Committee in its sitting held on 5th and 12th April, 2010 held detailed interaction with the 
representatives of Ministry of Home Affairs, and Andaman and Nicobar Islands, Daman and Diu and 
Dadra and Nagar Haveli.

5.1.6 The Committee expresses its displeasure over th e fact that the amount of pension paid 
under the various social welfare sc hemes in the UTs is not uniform. The amount is also grossly 
inadequate keeping in view th e cost of living. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends 
that the Government may enhance the amount of pension under the welfare schemes at par with 
that of the NCT of Delhi, uniformly in all the UTs. 
Demand No. 95 - Andaman and Nicobar Islands 
5.2  The major sectors for which allocations are made under Demand No. 95 of Andaman and 
Nicobar Islands are (i) Agriculture and Allied Activiti es; (ii) Rural Development; (iii) Transport; (iv) 
Housing and Urban Development; (v) Energy; (vi) Education; (vii) Civil Aviation and others.

and R.E. 2010-2011 for various sectors in respect of the Andaman and Nicobar Islands are as under:

5.2.2 Replying to the Committee’s query regarding th e lower Plan allocation for the year 2010-11 as 
compared to RE 2009-10, the Chief Secretary, A ndaman & Nicobar Administration stated that for 
additional funds, the Administration would place before the Planning Commission enhanced 

allocation for some of these special projects. He stated that last year the Working Group discussions 
were not held and the plans were approved. He hoped that this year Working Group discussion would 
be held so that they could project the requirements. 
5.2.3 The Committee takes serious note that Plan allocation for the year 2010-11 has been 
reduced by 30% from RE 2009-10. This is bo und to affect several schemes. The Committee 
desires that the Ministry of Home Affairs may take up the issue with the Planning Commission 
for enhancement of the Plan allocation at the RE  stage so that no projects suffer for want of 
finance. 
5.2.4 The Committee notes that the budgetary provision fo r Tsunami Rehabilitation 
Programme has been reduced to Rs . 100 crore in BE 2010-11 in comparison to Rs. 647.77 crore 
in RE 2009-10. The Committee p resumes that major part of the rehabilitation programme must 
have been completed in Andaman & Nicobar Islands. In case the al location is not sufficient for 
the purpose, efforts be made fo r enhancing the provision at th e RE stage. The Committee feels 
that due to reduction in allocation for Tsuna mi Rehabilitation, the remaining part of the 
rehabilitation programme should not suffer. 
5.2.5 During the course of the presentation, the Chief Secretary had informed that in Pradhan Mantri 
Gram Sadak Yojana, not much progress had been ma de in the last few years because the approved 
local contractors were not able to meet the conditions. There has been cost overrun in projects under 
the Pradhan Mantri Gramin Sadak Yojana and revised cost estimates had to be prepared. 
5.2.6 The Committee is of the opinion that rura l roads are of immense importance for the 
development of villages particularly in the remote areas of A & N Islands. Now that the revised 
estimates have been prepared, efforts should be  made to compensate for the lost time, by 
making appropriate allocations. 
5.3  Medical facility for Central Government Employees and Other Middle Class People 
5.3.1 The Committee was informed that CGHS facility is not available to the Central Government 
employees working in Andaman & Nicobar Islands.  The Committee was also informed that for the 
retired Government employees and other middle class and low income group people, the problem is 
more acute as they are not covered by any health sc heme. For serious ailments they have to go to the 
mainland for speciality treatment and the reimbursement of the expenditure incurred thereon is not 
admissible. The Committee was also informed that the matter was taken up with the CGHS in the past 
but it was not acceded to. The Chief Secretary, Andaman & Nicobar Islands submitted that the 
administration was contemplating for a health insurance scheme and hoped that the same would be 
ready within the next one or two months. 
5.3.2 It is a sad state of affairs that no super sp eciality treatment/services are available in the 
Islands and the people have to travel to Tamil Nadu or Kolkata for treatment for which 
expenditure is not reimbursed. The Commi ttee does not understand why the Central 
Government has been indifferent to the woes of the people in general and its employees 
particularly the pensioners wh o are living in that Islands. The Committee recommends that 
Ministry of Home Affairs may take up this matter with the Ministry of Health & Family 
Welfare for appropriate decision at an early date. The Committee also recommends that the 
Government should also formulate suitable medical insurance scheme for all the persons living 
in the Islands. 

5.3.3 In this context, the Committee would like to highlight another area of affecting day to day 
lives of Islanders. In case of serious medical emergency for whic h a person has to be urgently 
evacuated to the nearby city in the main land for treatment, there appears to be no 
arrangements for air ambulance or dedicated he licopter service. The Committee is of the view 
that this is a critical area which the Ministry of Home Affairs needs to urgently look into so that 
the facility of air ambulance is  put in place and allocate f und to UT Administration for 
procurement of an air ambulance or dedicated helicopter. 
5.4  Connectivity of Andaman and Nicobar Islands 
5.4.1 The Committee took up the matter of connectivity of Andaman & Nicobar Islands with the 
mainland of the country. The Committee was given to understand that there was shipping subsidy but 
no air subsidy. The administration provides ninety per cent subsidy for ship journey for local people 
and seventy five per cent for other people. However,  ships were ageing. Regarding air fare, the Home 
Secretary informed the Committee th at not only there was no subsidy, the air lines were actually 
charging more than the normal fare also. The Comm ittee was also given to understand that by paying 
less amount than the air fare for Port Blair, people can visit some cities in the neighbouring South East 
Asia with attractive packages like free stay and sight seeing. 
5.4.2 The Committee is of the view that the Andaman & Nicobar Isl ands has a special place in 
our history of freedom struggle. It is a sort of pilgrimage for the countrymen where Cellular Jail 
is located. Many of our legendary freedom figh ters were lodged in Cellular Jail and served life 
sentence. Some of the freedom fighters went to the gallows also. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that appropriate subsidy should be gi ven on air fare, to a ttract tourists to the 
Islands. Special packages for students could also be considered for making them understand and 
appreciate the sacrifices our freedom fighters had made for our independence. The Committee 
further recommends that old ships may be abandoned and new ones  may be brought in at the 
earliest. If necessary, participation of private sector may be explored. 
5.5  General Recommendations 
5.5.1 The Committee is of the view that besides th e specific recommendations made above, the 
Government should also consider the following suggestions: 
(i) Enhancement of Students Stipend from Rs.300 per month to Rs. 1000 per month 
keeping in view that the last revision was made in the year 2002; 
(ii) Enhancement of College Students Stipe nd from Rs.650 per month to Rs. 1000 per 
month keeping in view that the last revision was made in year 2002; 
(iii) Students staying in hostels in the mainland colleges are getting full reimbursement 
of amount as stipend. However, st udents who are unable to get hostel 
accommodation have and to reside in priv ate accommodations, are getting Rs. 300 
per month only. Such students should also be paid the amount equal to the stipend 
being given to the students staying in hostels. 
(iv) Budgetary provision be made for residential  schools at each Tehsil from class IX to 
XII with science, arts, commerce streams,  for imparting quality education with 
adequate sanitation facilities; 

(v) Part-time teachers, Aaya, Sweeper, Mali, Peon, Instructor who have put in services 
for more than eighteen years are getting Rs. 2000 per month only. They should be 
paid minimum wages first. Their services should also be regularized; 
(vi) Five Vivekananda Kendr a Zila Parishad Schools are imparting good quality 
education in the Islands. These schools are finding it difficult to retain teachers due 
to hike in their salary after the implem entation of the Sixth Pay Commission and 
also enhancement of teachers’ salary under the Sarva Shiksha Abhiyan from Rs. 
9000 to 18000. As these schools are situated in remote backward areas, 
enhancement of fee from students is not desirable. To retain teachers in these 
schools, A&N Islands Administ ration should match the defi cit part of their salary 
from its funds so that teachers in thes e schools get decent salary. For this MoU 
executed by the Administration may be amended; 
(vii) As per the criteria fixed for quantit y, calorie and protein under Mid-day Meal 
Scheme, the ceiling of Rs. 6.60 is not sufficient for the children of the A&N Islands 
as the prices of essential commodities in the Islands is the highest in the country 
due to its remoteness. The ceiling needs to be increased to Rs. 10 - 15 in view of the 
prevailing market rates. 
(viii) Mid-day Meal Scheme may be extended fr om class VIII to XII in consonance with 
the facility extended in Lakshdweep, Delhi and Tamil Nadu; 
(ix) Hostels in Islands particularly in remote backward area s like Mayabunder Boys 
Hostel, are in a dilapidated condition. The entire Islands fall under Seismic Zone V 
and these hostels may crumble in case of  any earthquake, causing loss of lives. 
Therefore, hostels in Rangat, Diglipur , Kishorinagar are in dire need of 
repair/renovation. There are many school s having broken tin roofs, broken 
thatched walls with no pucca floor, dri nking water and toilet  facilities. Those 
schools should properly be constructed with all basic amenities. Some schools in 
the islands do not have class rooms, science labs, and staff quarters. These facilities 
must be provided immediately; 
(x) Since 2008, bicycles are being provided to Nicobari  ST students in Class VIII 
under Sarva Shikhsha Abhiyan. Laksha dweep, Tamil Na du, Karnataka, 
Puducherry are providing free  bicycles to all the students. A& N Islands is 
considered as mini India on account of having harmony among different castes and 
creeds. Students have to walk 10-12 kms to  come to main road for buses and 
students are not getting time ly reimbursement for their bus passes. Therefore, 
bicycles should be provided to all the students to avoid any discrimination between 
tribals and non-tribals. 
(xi) Old age pension of Rs. 500/- was last  revised in 2005-06. Goa and Delhi have 
revised the pension to Rs. 1000/- in the year 2008. Besides dhoti, Saree and four kg. 
rice per month is also given by Tami l Nadu Government. Old persons in A&N 
Islands should be given pension of Rs. 1000 /- per month. They should also be given 
two dhotis/Sarees in a year. 

(xii) Disability allowance of Rs. 500/- is p resently being given to handicapped persons in 
the A&N Islands. Handicapped persons in De lhi and Goa are getting allowance of 
Rs. 1000/ - per month since 2008. Tam il Nadu Government provides dhoti/saree 
and four kg. rice per month free of cost . Mizoram Government provides financial 
assistance for piggery @ Rs. 2000/-, poultry @ Rs. 1500/-, petty shop @ Rs. 1000/- 
per head per annum with sewing mach ines. Disabled persons in Andaman & 
Nicobar Islands should also be given allowance of Rs. 1000/- as well as assistance as 
being provided by Tamil Nadu and Mizoram Governments. 
(xiii) Destitute/single/abandone d/divorced women in Delhi are getting a pension of Rs. 
1000/- per month. No such scheme has b een extended to A&N Islands. Pension of 
Rs. 1000/- for destitute/single/abandoned/divorced women in A&N Islands should 
be given. Government of NCT Delhi provides  marriage assistance of Rs. 20000/- to 
orphan girls and daughters of  widows. This scheme shou ld also be extended to 
A&N Islands. 
(xiv) There are 1400 Anganwadi  Workers and Helpers. Workers are getting Rs. 1800- 
2000 while the helpers are getting Rs. 990/ - per month. These workers/Helpers are 
working since the last 15 to 20 year s. Puducherry and Ta mil Nadu Governments 
have granted regular pay scales to them. Tamil Nadu Government also pay HRA, 
City Allowance and medical reimbursement. Workers/Helpers should be given at 
least minimum wages till such time, regular pay-scale for them are granted. 
(xv) A large number of farmers in the bo th districts of A&N Islands have lost 
agriculture land due to erosion caused by rivers and canals. Therefore, erosion 
control measures must be initiated immedi ately and alternate land should be given 
to those farmers who have lost their agricultural land due to erosion. 
(xvi) Many old villages viz. Hanspuri, Shanti Nagar, Ganesh Nagar, Jagannath Dera, in 
the Islands solely depends on inland water transport due to lack of road 
connectivity. Till date, no jetty or dingi ghat has been provided for these villages. 
New jetties should be constructed for the benefit of the villages. 
(xvii) There are many crocodile infested riv ers, canal, and creeks which people have to 
cross to go to their villages. These villages viz. Ramnagar, Sitanagar, Radhanagar, 
Nischintpur, Kalighat are 40-50 years old. Funds should be provided for 
construction of bridges in these areas. 
(xviii) Yatri Niwas/Dharamshala run by Municipal Corporation was constructed 50 years 
ago. However, with the increase in popul ation, no additional rooms have been 
added in spite of foundation stone laid do wn by the then Union Minister of State 
for Home Affairs. Muni cipal Corporation should  be provided funds for 
construction of Dharamshala. 
(xix) There are many old villages viz . Soal Bay, Flat Bay, Kalsi, Shyam Kunj, etc. which 
have no road connectivity. Rural roads in  these villages should be constructed for 
providing road connectivity. 

(xx) Seven projects under PMGSY were sa nctioned in 2004. Some funds were 
released. However, roads are still to be constructed in North and Middle 
Andaman. Roads under PMGSY should be constructed at the earliest. 
(xxi) Police Personnel in the A&N Islands sh ould be granted Ration Money like their 
counterparts in Delhi Police and CRPF. 
(xxii) Chatham Saw Mill which was established during British period is in dire need of 
modernization. Modernization of this Mill is included in  the Plan every year but 
nothing is being done. Similarly, Betapur Saw Mill in middle Andaman also needs 
modernization. 
(xxiii) About 80,000 agitated educated yout h are jobless. Group ‘D’ Posts in various 
departments of A&N Islands Administrati on has been banned. The youth in the 
Islands does not have job opportunities as compared to their counterparts in the 
mainland. Therefore, special attention need s to be paid for employment of youth 
in the Islands. 
(xxiv) Agricultural land of a large numb er of farmers has submerged after Tsunami. 
One person of each of such  affected family should be  given Government Service. 
They should also be given alternate la nd. Free ration may be given till the 
allotment of alternate land is made. 
(xxv) According to BPL Census 2000, BPL families in Nicobar Tribal Area were 67%, 
South Andaman District 13%, North Andama n District 7%. Many families living 
in de-reserved blocks or jungles in Nort h and Middle Andamans have been left 
out in BPL list. The cut off score for iden tifying BPL families is discriminatory. 
As per Planning Commission there should be 28000 BPL cards whereas only 
17000 BPL/AY cards have been issued. Remaining 11000 BPL cards should be 
issued immediately. 
(xxvi) LADLI Scheme on the pattern of Delhi,  for promotion of sex ratio, institutional 
birth and education of girls should be implemented in the Islands. 
(xxvii) Workers and Helpers in Day Care Ce ntres are getting honorarium of Rs. 1500-
2000 per month since the year 2003. Th e honorarium should be increased 
immediately. 
(xxviii) The earthen bunds have not been constructed scientifically, by the Zila Parishad, 
South Andaman and the same are in the danger of collapse/wash out. These 
bunds should immediately be reinforce d otherwise the Andaman Trunk Road 
and some villages will be submerged in the sea waters.

5.7 Reasons for reduction in allocation for Energy Sector 
5.7.1 When enquired about the reasons for the reduction in allocation for Energy Sector in the BE 
2010-11 as compared to the provisions in BE 2009-10 and RE 2009-10, the Committee was informed 
that due to sharp increase in petroleum price and shortage of power supply throughout the Northern 
and Western Grids, the average power purchase cost went upto Rs.3.94 per unit during 2008-09 from 
the previous purchase cost of Rs.2.56 per unit. C onsidering this exorbitant increase in power purchase 
cost, a budget provision of Rs.1790.00 crore was kept in BE in 2009-10. But during 2009-10 due to 
easing out of petroleum price and availability of power coupled with additional allocation of power 
from the Ministry of Power, the po wer purchase cost came down from the level of Rs.3.94 per unit to 
Rs.2.93 per unit approximately. Therefore the budget for power purchase has been reduced from 
Rs.1790.00 crores to Rs.1053.00 crore at RE 2009-10 and Rs.1350.00 crore in BE 2010-11.

regard to revenue receipts from various sources in the UT.

5.7.4 The Committee was informed that no such data as per capita income is available with UT 
Administration as no such survey was conducted. 
5.7.5 The Committee is unable to understand why the data relating to per capita income of 
Dadra & Nagar Haveli is not being maintained  by the UT Administration. The Committee 
strongly recommends that a surv ey be conducted, at the earlie st, to ascertain the per capita 
income of the UT not only for assessing the eco nomic health of the UT but also for various 
comparisons required for economic planning in the UT. 
5.7.6 The Committee learnt that out of 531 sanctio ned posts of teachers only 151 are presently in 
position and there are 380 vacancies. However 79 Trained Graduate Teacher (TGT) and 57 Post 
Graduate Teacher (PGT) are working on adhoc/daily wages basis. The representatives of the UT 
Administration stated that since there was a ruling of the Hon’ble Supreme Court on the matter of 
regularization and the case of the teachers wo rking on adhoc/daily wages basis in DNH Education 
Department was not covered under the principles laid down by the Hon’ble Court, the UT 
Administration was facing difficulties in regularizing their services. It was also  stated that since most 
of them have become over-age as on date and they had contributed substantially in smooth functioning 
of the schools when the posts were not in existence, the UT Administration had decided to request the 
Union Government te approve regularization of adhoc/daily wages teachers who were recruited 
against the anticipated creation of vacancies by the Government of India. 
5.7.7 It was stated that in erstwhile recruitment rules of TGTs, the method of recruitment was 100% 
by promotion from feeder cadre i.e. Primary School Teachers. There were no eligible candidates 
available for promotion from feeder cadre and hence vacant posts of TGTs could not be filled in. 
5.7.8 Vacant posts of PGTs could not be filled up due  to non-availability of subject and medium wise 
eligible candidates. To cope-up with the increasing demand and to safeguard the interest of students, 
the Department of Education had engaged teachers on daily wages basis after following detailed 
procedure for regular appointment with the approval of the competent authority i.e. Administrator, 
Dadra & Nagar Haveli, Silvasa. For filling up of vacant posts of TGTs & PGTs, the department has 
already initiated the process and soon these posts will be filled. 
5.7.9 The Committee is of the view that adequate num ber of teachers in the UT is an essential 
pre-requisite to make the ‘Right to Education’ a reality. The interest of those who are working 
on daily wages should be protected. In this light, the Committee recommends that the UT 

Administration’s request to the Union Governme nt to approve regulariz ation of adhoc/daily 
wages teachers who were recruited against th e anticipated creation of vacancies by the 
Government of India and who fu lfill educational qualification as prescribed in the Recruitment 
Rules, as an one time measure, may be accepted. The Committee also recommends that 
expeditious action may be taken to fill up other vacancies of teachers. 
5.8  Pay of Home Guards in Dadra & Nagar Haveli vis-a-vis other UTs 
5.8.1 The Committee learnt that Home Guards are paid an allowance @ Rs. 90/- per day by the 
Administration of Dadra & Nagar Haveli since 2000 and Rs. 40/- per month as washing allowance. 
The enhancement of the allowances to Home Guards is stated to be under consideration in the 
Ministry of Home Affairs and Ministry of Finance. 
5.8.2 The Committee recommends that the allowanc es of Home Guards may be suitably 
enhanced, at least at par with the private security services. 
5.9  Availability of drinking water 
5.9.1 The Committee has been given to understand that the main source of providing drinking water 
in rural areas is through tube wells, etc., and there is acute water shortage during summers on account 
of depletion of the water table particularly in Dudhani, Mandoni, Randha and part of Amboli Patelad. 
The UT Administration is providing water th rough tankers in these areas. The Committee was 
informed that in order to provide a permanent solution to the drinking water problem particularly 
during summer, the UT Administration has initiated two major water projects each at Mandoni and 
Dudhani Patelad. 
5.9.2 The Committee recommends that the water proj ects at Mandoni and Dudhani Patelad be 
completed at the earliest as a permanent solution to the drinking wa ter problem especially 
during the summer season. 
5.10  Urban Water Supply Scheme 
5.10.1 The Committee was informed that the existin g water supply scheme for Silvasa was designed 
for 20000 persons in the year 1995 and as per the Census 2001 the population of Silvasa Municipal 
area increased to 50500. The existing scheme has been found inadequate to cater to present need of 
water for about 75000 souls. A water supply scheme has been designed with an estimated cost of Rs. 
26.63 crore by Silvasa Municipal Council. Ministry of Urban Development has conveyed the 
administrative approval for Rs. 26.63 crore. The proposal for obtaining expenditure sanction has 
already been submitted to the Ministry of Urban Development and was still awaited. The 
Administration has also initiated a proposal for Integrated Water Management Plan for the whole 
Union Territory. 
5.10.2  The Committee desires that the scheme prop osed by Silvasa Municipal Council may be 
cleared at the earliest by the Ministry of Ur ban Development. The Co mmittee hopes that ‘The 
Integrated Water Management Plan’ for the whole of the Union Territory would soon be put in 
place as a long term solution for the depleting water resources. 
5.11  Mid-day Meal Scheme 

5.11.1  On a query as to whether Mid Day Meal Scheme is being implemented in the UT, it was stated 
that the Mid-Day Meal Scheme as per the guidelines of the Government of India is being implemented 
in the UT of Dadra & Nagar Haveli. The students in  I to V standard are provided hot cooked meals 
containing 480 calories and 30 grams of protein each  day of school (100 grams rice, 20 grams dal, 
green vegetables) for a minimum 200 days and students from VI to VII are provided cooked meals 
containing 746 calories and 20 grams of protein ev eryday (150 grams rice, 20 grams dal and green 
vegetables). It was also stated that the the me nu of the Mid Day Meal Scheme is prepared in 
consultation with the Demonstrator, Food and Nutrition Department. 
5.11.2  The Committee, however, has be en given to understand that the quality of food provided 
is of inferior quality and has compromised on the health and hygiene of children. The 
Committee is also constrained to learn that  there were instances in  the recent past when 
children had fallen ill after taking such food. The Committee desires that an inquiry may be 
held into the irregularities in implementing the Scheme. The Administrati on should ensure that 
the Scheme is implemented in a proper manner and carefully monitored on regular basis. 
Demand No. 97 - Daman & Diu

Transport; (ii) Education;(iii) Housing and Urban Development; (iv) Energy; (v) Village & Small
