5.13  Goals/Targets 
5.13.1 On being enquired about any vision document being envisaged for integrated development of 
the UT, the Committee has been informed that the Administration of Daman & Diu has not yet 
compiled any such document. However, it has been decided to accept the overall vision of the 
Planning Commission for Eleventh F i v e  Y e a r  P l a n  P e r i o d  w i t h  s p e c i f i c  a t t e n t i o n  o n  H e a l t h ,  
Education, Water Supply, Energy, Transport, Tourism, Urban Development and Industry. 
5.13.2  The Committee recommends that a holistic Vision Statement regarding overall 
development of the UT be prepared comprising of  realistic goals achievable within a given time 
frame and quantifiable benchmarks prescribed so that progress made in the Plan period could 
be measured and the development policies/schemes adjusted according to the performance on 
the ground. 
5.14  Problems of Fishermen 

5.14.1 The main source of income of the populace of Diu is fishing and therefore they have to venture 
into the seas. It may happen that at times they may venture into the deep seas for their catch as it may 
not be profitable to fish within the territorial waters of our country. It was stated that in the deep seas 
the biggest problem faced by these fishermen is the menace of the Pakistani authorities rounding them 
up and detaining in their jails. Over the last six years or so about 560 of our fishermen have been 
holed up in their jails. It was only recently that about 100 of our fishermen were freed. However, the 
fishing boats/trawlers captured by the Pakistani authorities are damaged and not returned. 
5.14.2 The Committee has learnt that the subsidy pr ovided to the fishermen has been increased 
but the number of fishermen covered under it has been drastically reduced  which is quite 
surprising. This does not augur well for the we lfare of the fishing community. The Committee is 
of the opinion that the matter needs to be reviewed. 
5.14.3 The Committee is also of the considered view  that the Coast Guard should take up the 
issue of safeguarding the fishermen who venture into the deep sea and ensure their safe return. 
The Committee also recommends that the MHA may take up the matter with MEA, so that the 
fishermen languishing in the Pakistani jails are released at the earliest. 
5.15  Education 
5.15.1 The Committee has been informed that the following major projects are to be undertaken 
during the year 2010-2011: 
(i) Elementary Education 
 
z Sarva Shiksha Abhiyan 
 z  Development of infrastructural facilities in Primary and Middle Schools  
 z  Upgradation of Government Middle Schools as High Schools 
(ii) Secondary Education 
 z  Construction of Class Rooms for High Schools and Higher Secondary Schools 
 z  Upgradation of Government Middle Schools as High Schools 
 z  Upgradation of Government Higher Secondary Schools as Model Schools 
 z  Upgradation of Government High Schools and Higher Secondary Schools 
(iii) Higher Education 
 z  Modernization of College Library 
 z  Strengthening of Government College, Daman (Upgradation, Repair & Maintenance) 
5.15.2  In the light of remoteness of the UT, physica l proximity of schools and other educational 
institutions is essential so that  children get schooling closer to home and do not have to travel 
long distances for the same. The Committee is also  of the view that upgra dation of schools and 
syllabi should be done at regular intervals to meet the requirements of the local students. 
5.16  General Recommendations 

5.16.1  Besides the specific recommendations made in the previous paras, the Committee desires 
that the Government should also address the following problems of the UT of Daman & Diu: 
(i) The main sources of livelihood of D&D are Fisheries and Tourism. Some 
development activities like Distillery in Daman, have come up but the main 
economic activity is coastal tourism on wh ich a substantial part of the population 
survives. D&D is one of the most beautifu l Union Territories in the country with 
vast sandy beaches and non-polluted coas tal waters. The Committee recommends 
that the tourism potential of this Union T erritory harnessed fully , so that tourists 
visit in large numbers to the UT and the me ans of livelihood of its residents multiply 
with the income generated from the proceeds of tourism. Steps should also be taken 
for encouraging the opening of resorts and hotels in the UT. 
(ii) One of the main reasons affecting the economic development of D&D is the existing 
Coastal Regulation Zone Notification, 1991, which is very restrictive in nature. As 
57% of land is covered under Coastal Regulation Zone, this notification has affected 
the livelihood of the fishermen communities including construction of their dwelling 
units apart from affecting the developm ent of coastal tourism. The Committee 
understands that the Ministry of Environm ent and Forests is in the process of 
amending the Coastal Regulation Zone No tification, 1991 and trying to make a 
region specific notification on the lines of Prof. M.S. Swaminathan Committee 
Report. Necessary steps are required to be taken for removing the restrictions in the 
existing Coastal Regulation Zone for the promotion of tourism sector. 
(iii) The UT Administration had proposed construction of New bridges between Nani 
Daman and Moti Dama n and between Diu and mainla nd Gujarat. A few of the 
projects have been technically approved by the Ministry of Shipping, Road 
Transport and Highways in June 2009 but the same have not seen the light of the 
day. Their financial approval is pending in the MHA. The Committee recommends 
that the Ministry of Home Affairs should  expedite the approval so that the long 
pending demands of both Daman and Diu are taken care of at the earliest. 
(iv) The Committee was informed that the 51st Battalion of the India Reserve Battalion, 
with headquarters in the UT of Laksha dweep, has a very peculiar instance on the 
recruitment policy. The recruits to this Batta lion cater to the needs of the UTs of 
D&D, DNH and Lakshadweep. The recruitment to this Battalion is from these three 
UTs. However these recruits are posted for a total period of four years away from 
their native place causing a lot of hardsh ip, mainly due to the language problem. 
The Committee feels that the local recruits  should stay within their Territory which 
will help them to lead a better family life and thereby improve their operational 
efficiency. 
(v) The Committee has been apprised that th e discharge of effluents (mostly untreated) 
from the chemical units in the GIDC Estate  of Vapi into Daman Ganga River leads 
to contamination of the river and the po lluted water flows through Daman emitting 
a lot of foul smell. Most of the potable wa ter in Daman, which is ground water, gets 
contaminated due to seepage of the pollut ed water. Coastal Marine life and tourist 

spots are adversely affected. The Commi ttee feels that the problem needs to 
addressed urgently which if allowed to continue may lead to drop in revenue 
earning as the tourists have  already started moving away from the coastal areas of 
D&D. 
(vi) The Committee, notes that both Daman and Diu depend on the Gujarat 
Government for water supply, which at p resent is not sufficient. Therefore, the 
Committee recommends that additional allocation from Madhuban Dam for Daman 
and Narmada Canal for Diu has to be considered at the earliest. 
(vii) The UT of D & D does not produce elec tricity and are dependent on NTPC and the 
Western Grid for their requirement. A solu tion to the demand-supply gap has to be 
filled in at the earliest so that the existing industrial units are not forced to shift 
base. 
(viii) To improve health care , a Government or Governme nt sponsored Super Speciality 
Hospital is the need of the hour for Diu and should be considered on top priority. 
(ix) More incentives to industrial houses to  set up units in Dama n and Diu to help 
generate employment for the locals and al so to generate revenue for the UT, may 
also be considered. 
5.17  Clarifications sought in respect of UTs of Dadra & Nagar Haveli and Daman & Diu 
5.17.1  After the deliberations of the Committee held on 5 April 2010, the Secretariat had forwarded 
to the Ministry of Home Affairs two lists, each containing seven points for clarifications, in respect of 
UTs of Dadra & Nagar Haveli and Daman & Diu. The clarifications sought pertained to Industries and 
trading Units functioning from the two UTs, nature of incentives offered to those, exemptions of 
Customs, Central Excise Duty, Sales Tax/VAT/Service Tax and other levies granted to such units, 
volume of revenue collected from them, instances of tax and duty evasions detected by the UT 
Administration and the Central Government Authorities, etc., during the period since 2004-05. 
5.17.2  The Ministry of Home Affairs has sent a letter dated 19 April 2010 to the Secretariat for grant 
of one month’s time for submission of replies to the Points for Clarifications on the plea that the 
requisite information is voluminous and that the Department of Revenue and Ministry of Commerce 
would also need that much time to collect the information. 
5.17.3 The Committee considered the request of the Ministry in its sitting held on 22 April 2010. The 
Committee was not convinced with the reasons given by the Ministry for seeking one month’s 
extension to furnish the information, as it was to finalise its report on 26 April 2010. The Committee 
directed that the Ministry may be asked to submit information on atleast tax and duty evasion cases for 
the first quarter of 2010, positively by 23 April 2010. 
5.17.4  In pursuance of the direction of the Committee, the Ministry of Home Affairs vide  their O.M. 
dated 23 April 2010 forwarded replies to the Points  for Clarifications as received from the UTs of 
Dadra & Nagar Haveli and Daman and Diu, the Ministry of Commerce and Department of Revenue. 
5.17.5  The information as forwarded by  the Ministry of Home Affairs has been perused. While 
information in respect of tax and duty evasion ca ses and in respect of some other items in the 

two UTs in the first quarter of 2010 have been furnished as directed by the Committee, certain 
interesting aspects emerged there from. Initially th e Ministry of Home Affairs sought a month’s 
time to compile the information as it was voluminous in  nature and was to be compiled from 
different Ministries and agencies, but in replies to many of the questions, it has now been stated 
that the requisite information may be collected from the Mini stry of Finance (Customs and 
Excise Department) and the Ministry of Commerce. In respect of some other questions one 
month’s time has again been sought  for submission of replies on th e plea that the material to be 
collected would be voluminous. It has further been found that in respect of those questions 
where time has been sought to reply, the administ rations of the two UTs have already furnished 
voluminous information to the Committee. 
5.17.6  At the outset, the Committee would like to ob serve that as per established practice, 
whenever, it requires any informat ion, the concerned Ministry und er its jurisdiction is asked to 
supply the same irrespective of the fact that the informatio n may concern other Ministry 
Department or State Government. It is perhaps the first time, in the history of the Committee 
that the administrative Ministry  concerned is asking it to pr ocure the requisite information 
from other Ministries directly. In the same vein, the Ministry is also seeking extension of time 
for supply of material in respect  of certain other questions. While  information has already been 
furnished by UT Administrations, the Committee takes a serious note of the casual approach of 
the Ministry of Home Affairs and the Administrator in respo nding to the request of the 
Standing Committee. The Committee therefore direc ts the Ministry and the Administrator to 
supply the requisite information to it positively within fifteen days  from the presentation of the 
Report. 
5.17.7 In reply to the query of the Committee regard ing instances of evasion of taxes and duties, 
if any, by enterprises, including violations of the Goa, Daman & Diu Excise Duty Act and Rules, 
1964 detected year-wise since 2004-05 by the Da man and Diu Administrati on and others, cases 
registered etc., the UT administration has prov ided incomplete and contradictory information 
instead of facilitating the Committ ee to understand the ground realitie s, particularly relating to 
the level of alertness of the concerned Depart ments in ensuring collection of Government 
revenue commensurating with the business activities of the Units operating from the Union 
Territories of Daman & Diu and Dadra & Nagar Haveli. 
5.17.8  For instance, the volume of Excise Duty colle ction is stated to have “more than doubled” 
since 2004-05, from 24.24 crore to Rs. 57.90 crore, but the reply is silent about what this increase 
in collection of Excise Duty has been attr ibutable to. It was incumbent upon the UT 
Administration to explain as to whether the incr ease in Excise Duty collection every year was 
due to enhanced volume of production by the units, or revision of rates or for any other reasons, 
to enable the Committee to und erstand the ground situation. By  providing such incomplete 
information, the Committee has been denied that opportunity. 
5.17.9  More glaring in the reply is the attempt of  providing contradictor y information to the 
Committee. While, on the one hand, it  is stated in the reply that “no evasion of Excise Duty has 
been de
tected during the period in question” on the other hand it is stated “... recently during the 
meetings of the Joint Regional Intelligence sharing, apprehensions have been raised regarding 
probable evasion of the excise duty by the distilleri es in Daman” and that “. .. In the interest of the 

Government revenue, the Department has moved swif tly and only on the apprehensions of the 
excise duty evasion the show cause notices have been issued to the Distilleries of Daman as to why 
the excise duty of Rs. 300 crores (approximately total of all distilleries) should not be raised upon 
them for last five years.” It is further stated that, “...notices have been issu ed just to pre-empt any 
time barring of the demand in case the raising of demand found justified and also as apprehensions 
were raised”. 
5.17.10  The Committee, it appears, is being sought to be  kept in the dark as to the genesis of the 
“apprehension” leading to issuance of notices for recovery of ev aded Excise Duty for periods 
covering past five years. Whether the said Jo int Regional Intelligence has been exercising 
surveillance over the region encompassing Gujarat, Goa, Maha rashtra, besides the UTs of 
Daman & Diu and Dadra & Nagar Haveli, and had pi cked up clues leading to the conceiving of 
“apprehensions” about the alleged evasion of Excise Duty by distilleries operating in Daman? In 
that case, the genesis of the said “apprehension ” should have been explained for removing the 
confusion of the Committee. 
5.17.11  Moreover, it remains unclear as to how th e UT Administration could issue notices for 
recovery of Excise Duty to asses sees simply ba sed on “apprehension” that it had been evaded 
for the past five years. Does is not weaken the case and strengthen the alleged taxes/duty evaders 
to challenge the demands of the Department in the Court? 
5.17.12  It should be incumbent up on Government to ascertai n the facts of the above 
developments with particular reference to the que stions raised by the Co mmittee, as above, and 
enlighten the Committee about the ground realities of the matter. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

CHAPTER-VI 
CENSUS 2011 AND NATIONAL POPULATION REGISTER 
6.1  A population census is the total process of collecting, compiling, evaluating, analysing and 
publishing or otherwise disseminating demographic, economic and social data pertaining, at a 
specified time, to all persons in a country or in a well-delimited part of a country. The objective of the 
census is complete coverage and accurate enumeration of all the people regarding: who and how 
many; what do they do and how often; type of houses they live in; household amenities that they have 
or don’t have and, household assets they posses or don’t posses. The Committee was informed that the 
exercise has statutory backing of the Census Act 1948 and Census Rules 1990. 
6.1.1 The Office of Registrar General and Census Commissioner, India (O/o RG & CCI) is in-charge 
of planning, coordination and supervision of the decennial Housing and Population Census, 
tabulation/compilation and dissemination of Census results under the provisions of the Census Act, 
1948 and the Census (Amendment) Act, 1993. Besides, this office is responsible for overall 
implementation of the Registration of Births and Deaths Act, 1969 in the country and compilation of 
data of vital statistics on births and deaths. The O/o and CCI separately brings out estimates of fertility 
and mortality at the National and State levels through a well represented sample under the Sample 
Registration System (SRS). Since 2003, the O/o RG and CCI has also been functioning as National 
Registration Authority and Registrar General of Citizen Registration under the Citizenship 
(Amendment) Act, 2003. It was further stated that the O/o RG and CCI has now been assigned the 
additional task of conducting the Annual Health Survey (AHS) at the behest of Ministry of Health and 
Family Welfare to yield benchmarks of core vital and health indicators at the district level. 
6.2  Census 2011 
6.2.1 The Registrar General and Census Commissioner of India made a presentation on Census of 
India 2011 and Preparation of National Population Register before the Committee in its meeting held 
on 12
th April, 2010 highlighting the following points: 
z In 1872 the first Census was held in India. 
z Since then, Census in India held uninterruptedly. 
z The Census of India 2011 will be the 15th Census and the 7th after Independence.  
z Census 2011will mark a milestone as the National Population Register (NPR) will also be 
prepared. 
z Census is of immense utility as it is biggest source of data on: 
 --- Demography 
 --- Economic Activity 
 --- Literacy and Education 
 --- Housing and Household Amenities 
 --- Urbanization 
 --- Fertility and Mortality 

 --- Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes 
 --- Language, Religion and Migration 
 --- Primary data at village, town and ward level 
 --- Information for planning and formulation of policies for Central and State 
Governments 
z It is widely used by national and international agencies, scholars, business people, 
industrialists, and many more. 
z It is useful for Delimitation/Reservation of Constituencies 
Parliamentary/Assembly/Panchayats and other Local Bodies 
6.2.2 Explaining about the details of arrangements for Census 2011, the Registrar General and Census 
Commissioner stated that 25 lakh Census Officials are being employed for Census 2011; 12,000 
Metric Tonnes of Paper would be used; 64 crore Sc hedules are to be printed in 16 languages; and 81 
lakh Instruction Manuals are printed in 18 languages. 
6.2.3 The Committee was informed that the first phase of the Census 2011 will be held during April 
and September 2010 for collection of data on Houselisting and Housing Census and National 
Population Register. The second phase will be held during 9th and 28th February, 2011 for population 
enumeration. 
6.2.4 The Committee was informed that the following items of information are required for 
Houselisting and Housing Census: 
z Information relating to Buildings- Use, Quantity and Quality. 
z Amenities available to the Households. 
z Assets possessed by the Households. 
z 35 Columns relating to building material, use of houses, drinking water, availability and 
type of 
 latrines, electricity, possession of assets will be canvassed 
6.2.5 It was also stated that the following ar e the stages of the process of Houselisting : 
  z House to house canvassing for Houselisting Operations by Enumerators (45 days)  
  z Scanning of Schedules 
  z Processing of Images using ICR technology 
  z Automatic Data Capture 
  z Manual Processing of unrecognized images 
  z Finalization of Results 
6.2.6 When the Committee wanted to know whether Census will be conducted in the enclaves of 
Indo-Bangladesh border, the Ministry replied that all areas within the territory of India will be covered 
in Census 2011. The concerned Collectors and other Revenue officers have been given instructions to 
cover all areas which are under their jurisdiction without omission or duplication. 

6.3  Pilot Project on Multipurpose National Identity Card (MNIC) and NPR 
6.3.1 The Committee has been informed that the current scheme of preparation of the NPR has 
evolved over a period of time. A Group of Ministers (GoM), while reviewing the Kargil Review 
Committee (KRC) Report on Internal Security, recommended that all citizens should be given a Multi 
purpose National Identity Card (MNIC) and non-citi zens should be issued identity cards of a different 
colour and design. 
6.3.2 Accordingly, the Citizenship Act, 1955 wa s amended in 2003 and Section 14A was inserted, 
which provides that the Central Government may compulsorily register every citizen of India and 
issue National Identity Card to him. Simultaneously, Citizenship Rules 2003 have been formulated 
laying down the processes to be followed for giving effect to this intent. 
6.3.3 It was also stated that to understand the co mplexities involved alongwith technical specifications 
and technology required for national roll out, a pilot project had been completed on March 31, 2009 in 
selected areas of 12 Stat es and 1 Union Territory viz., Andhra Pradesh, Assam, Delhi, Goa, Gujarat, 
Jammu and Kashmir, Rajasthan, Tripura, Uttar Pradesh, Uttarakhahd, Tamil Nadu, West Bengal, and 
Puducherry. More than 12.50 lakh identity cards had been issued to the citiz ens of age 18 years and 
above in the pilot areas. As a result of the pilot project, processes for collection and verification of 
individual data as well as the technology for personalization of identity cards using an interoperable 
operating system have been established. 
6.3.4 The Committee was informed that the main finding of pilot project was that determination of 
citizenship is an involved and complicated matter as documents or relevant data to prove citizenship 
like birth certificate, land records, school records were not readily available, especially in rural areas. 
On the contrary, many migrants who had illegally entered the county had created such documents. 
Thus, the genuine citizens of the country were put to great hardship. The other experience was that the 
entire exercise of verification was extremely time  consuming and needed the utmost cooperation of 
the State Government officials which in many cas es were not readily forthcoming due to their 
engagement in various State Government priorities like elections, natural calamities, local issues, etc. 
Moreover, there was a constant inflow and outflow of persons across the country. Unless the data was 
frozen at a point of time, it would be difficult to carry out the process of verification in a scientific 
manner. 
6.3.5 Thereafter the Committee of Secretaries (CoS ) in October 2006 recommended for the creation 
of a National Population Register (NPR) of usual residents with a single reference date. An 
Empowered Group of Ministers (E-GoM), accepted  the recommendation of the CoS and further 
recommended that the preparation of NPR could be undertaken along with the Census of India 2011 
wherein house to house enumeration would be undertaken and the population count taken with 
reference to a single reference date. It is based on this recommendation of the E-GoM that the 
Ministry of Home Affairs has embarked upon the scheme of creation of NPR in the country. The 
estimated expenditure, methodology, processes and the items of data to be collected has been 
approved by the Union Cabinet. 
6.3.6 It was further added that the proposed NPR would be a register of usual residents, who are 
present in a particular area on a particular reference date. This is the first step towards creation of the 
National Register of lndian Citizens. It is from this master database that further verification may be 

conducted to determine citizens and non-citizens. It was further claimed that the decision to create a 
NPR is not in variance with the decision to issue iden tity cards to citizens and non-citizens but is the 
first step towards creation of such a database. 
6.3.7 The Ministry of Home Affairs further inform ed the Committee that the details required for the 
creation of NPR will be collected along with the fi rst phase of Census between April and September 
2010. The details collected will be entered into computers in the language of each State/UT and in 
English. Once this is done, the photograph, 10 fingerprints and if approved by the Government, the 
Iris information of all persons of age 15 years and above will be collected by arranging camps in each 
village and ward (in urban areas). Following this, the data will be printed out and published in the 
village and wards for invitation of objections. Objectio ns, if any will be enquired into and dealt with 
by designated authorities. After all claims and objections have been disposed, the list will be placed in 
the Gram Sabha and the Ward Committee for scrutiny of public representatives. Then the electronic 
NPR database of all usual residents who are of age 15 years and above would be sent to UIDAI for de-
duplication and generation of UID Number. Once th e UID Number and de-duplicated database is 
received back, Identity Cards is proposed to be issued to all usual residents. The NPR is a biometrics 
based identity database with a definite reference date. After this reference date, future entry into the 
NPR database would be based on strict verification exercise as per the laid down procedures. The 
NPR is the first step towards the creation of th e National Register of Indian Citizens (NRIC). The 
NRIC will later be carved out as a subset of the NPR. 
6.3.8 Regarding linking UID database with National Population Register (NPR) and National Register 
for Indian Citizens (NRIC) and the roadmap of the activities related thereto, the Ministry of Home 
Affairs stated that the UID Authority of India has been set up on 28th January, 2009 in the Planning 
Commission to initially collate the metadata from the existing databases. Under the new arrangement, 
the Registrar General and Census Commissioner India (RG & CCI) is one of the Registrars of the 
UIDAL 
6.3.9 The Ministry of Home Affairs has further stated  that the experience of the pilot project on issue 
of MNIC has shown that determination of citizenshi p is an involved and co mplicated matter and that 
creation of National Population Register (NPR) of all usual residents with a single reference date 
would be more feasible. 
6.3.10  Government of India have created a Unique Identification Authority of India (UIDAI) on 28 
January, 2009 in the Planning Commission with the objective of assigning a Unique Identity Number 
(UIN) to each resident in the country as and when she/he registers herself/himself for availing benefits 
and/or services from the Government. The NPR for the country is expected to be created by 2012-
2013. Once created, its database will be handed over to UIDAI for assigning the UID numbers. The 
office of RGI will de-duplicate the NPR database and assign UID Numbers. Later, the office of RGI 
would maintain and update the database. 
6.4  Utility of National Population Register (NPR) 
6.4.1 Explaining about the utility of NPR it was stated by the Ministry of Home Affairs that it would 
be useful in the following ways:  
  z It will create a comprehensive identity database in the country; 

  z It will help in better targeting of the benefits and services under the Government schemes/ 
   programmes; 
  z It will help in improving the planning process; and 
  z It will strengthen security of the country. 
6.4.2 The following items of information would be used in NPR : 
1 Name of Person 6 Sex 11 Present address of usual

   Status

   as Declared

Photograph, Finger Print and Iris (if approved) to be collected for Usual Residents aged 15 years and above

6.6  Allocation for the National Population Register (NPR) project in 2009-2010 
6.6.1 During 2009-2010, an amount of Rs. 162.70 crore was allocated under BE which was 
subsequently reduced to 117.70 crore at RE stage.  Out of this, Rs.56.42 crore was allocated for NPR 
(Coastal) and Rs. 28.28 crore was for NPR (Country) and Rs. 33 cror e was transferred to Department 
of Animal Husbandry and Dairying for implementing the scheme for issue of identity cards to 
fishermen under the coastal security scheme. 
6.7  Issues raised by the Committee 
6.7.1 After the presentation of RG & CCI, large number of questions and queries were put to the 
Home Secretary and the RG & CCI concerning the co nfidentiality of information collected during the 
Census operations, particularly the information to be filled in the Census of India 2011 ‘Houselisting 
and Housing Census Schedule’; issues concerning the rules and regulations empowering the 

Government to initiate the work of the National Population Register; the issue of ‘Nationality As 
Declared’ with reference to the National Population Register ‘Household Schedule’ and its 
implications on internal security. 
6.7.2 The Committee wanted to know the progress made in preparation of NPR for coastal regions 
keeping in view the 26/11 incident in Mumbai. The Ministry of Home Affairs replied that for 
strengthening the security along the coastline of the country, the creation of NPR in 3331 coastal 
villages in 9 Coastal States and 4 Union Territories has been taken up. As far as Andaman & Nicobar 
Islands is concerned, the towns are also being covered. Direct data collection using Laptops has been 
resorted to for collection of details about every i ndividual. Photographs and 10 fingerprints have been 
collected by organizing camps in the villages and towns. The field work of direct data collection is in 
progress and biographic details of more than 95 lakh persons and biometric capture of 39 lakh persons 
have been collected so far. The data collection in respect of Tamil Nadu , Puducherry, Karnataka, 
Andhra Pradesh, Orissa, Andaman & Nicobar Islands, Lakshadweep, has been completed. The 
collection of biometrics has been completed in Tamil Nadu, Puducherry and Andaman & Nicobar 
Islands. The data collection in Goa, Gujarat, Daman & Diu will be completed by 31.3.2010. There 
may be small spill-over in data collection in Maharashtra (operations started late due to election), 
West Bengal (in one district filed work started late due to cyclone Aila) and Kerala. 
6.7.3 The Committee was further informed that Government has also decided to issue identity (smart) 
cards to the usual residents of these villages w ho are of 18 years of age and above. This will 
commence in three months time. The Cabinet has approved the scheme of data collection and issue of 
identity (smart) cards to usual residents living in these coastal villages at an estimated cost of 
Rs.216.31 crore. 
6.7.4 The work of data collection as well as production, personalization and delivery of identity 
(smart) cards has been awarded to a consortium of Central Public Sector Undertakings, comprising 
Bharat Electronics Limited, (BEL), Electronics Co rporation of India Limited (ECIL) and Indian 
Telephone Industries Limited (ITIL). The scheme is being implemented by the Registrar General and 
Census Commissioner, India. 
6.7.5 As regards the recommendations of the Demographic Data Standards and Verification 
Procedure Committee and Biometric Standards Committee constituted by the UIDAI which have 
reportedly submitted their reports on 9th December, 2009 and 7th January, 2010 respectively, the 
Ministry of Home Affairs merely stated the factual position that the reports have been submitted and 
that the UIDAI is under the purview of the Planning Commission. 
6.7.6 When the Committee sought to know as to how the work of NPR has been started without 
amending the Citizenship Rules, 2003, the Committee was informed in writing that the scheme for 
creation of NPR was being undertaken under the Citizenship Act, 1955 and the Citizenship 
(Registration of Citizens and Issue of National Iden tity Cards) Rules 2003. Rule 3(4) of the aforesaid 
rules reads as follows:- 
"The Central Government may, by an order issued in this regard, decide a date by which the 
Population Register shall be prepared by collecting information relating to all persons who are 
usually residing within the jurisdiction of Local Registrar." 

6.7.7 The Committee was further informed that in view of the threat perception in coastal areas, the 
Government decided to create NPR in 3331 coastal villages and few towns of Andaman & Nicobar 
Islands on a priority basis. The Ministry of Law and Justice was consulted in the matter. It was 
contended that on the advice of Ministry of Law, the project was started by issuing Executive 
Instructions under the aforesaid Rule. On the same analogy, Orders of the Government have been 
obtained to create NPR for the entire country and the sa me has been notified. It was further stated that 
action was separately being taken in consultation with the State Governments for formulation of Rules 
and this will be notified in due course. 
6.7.8 The Committee, on scrutiny of the said provis ion, finds that the said provision only empowers 
the Central Government to decide a date by which the Population Register shall be prepared. It does 
not empower the Central Government to decide the modalities for execution of the Scheme including 
the formulation of the NPR Household Schedule which inter alia contains a clause on ‘Nationality As 
Declared.’ The Committee observes that the Govern ment decided to create NPR in 3331 costal 
villages and a few towns of the Andaman & Nicobar Islands, on the basis of executive instructions 
under Rule 3 (4) the Citizenship Rules 2003, on the advice on the Law Ministry and on the same 
analogy, orders of the Government have been ob tained to create NPR for the entire country and the 
same have been notified. The Committee further notes that action is separately being taken in 
consultation with the State Governments for formulation of rules and this would be notified in due 
course. In other words, the Ministry of Home Affairs has been empowered to carry on with the work of 
NPR in coastal areas in the first instance and also for the entire country subsequently, based on 
executive instructions, to be followed by formulation of rules to give post facto legitimacy to the 
various actions to be taken for creation of the NPR. 
6.7.9 As the Committee was not satisfied with the explanations given by the Ministry of Home 
Affairs relating to the creation of NPR for the co astal areas in the first instance and for the entire 
county subsequently, along with the Census operations, the Committee asked the Ministry to furnish 
copies of executive orders, notifications and the le gal opinion with regard to empowering the Central 
Government to undertake the exercise even before the Citizenship Rules, 2003 have been amended. 
The Committee also decided that the concerned files of the Home Ministry containing the 
deliberations of the Government and the decision of the Union Cabinet in that regard, with particular 
reference to inclusion of the ‘Nationality As Declared’ in the NPR Schedule, may be made available 
for perusal of the Chairman of the Committee. In pursuance of the direction of the Committee, the 
Ministry of Home Affairs, on 21 April, 2010, furnished copies of the requisite documents to its 
Chairman. The relevant files of the Home Minist ry were produced before the Chairman of the 
Committee by RG & CCI in person on that day which were perused by the former. 
6.8  The issue of ‘Nationality As Declared’ in the NPR Schedule 
6.8.1 The genesis of the current exercises for creation of National Population Register (NPR) can be 
traced back to the recommendation of the Group of Minister (GoM) constituted in 2000 to review the 
National Security System in the aftermath of the Kargil War in 1999, which suggested for compulsory 
registration of citizens and non-citizens living in India which would facilitate preparation of a 
National Register of Indian Citizens (NRIC). As corollary to the same GoM recommendation, the 
Citizenship Act, 1955 was amended by Parliament by inserting Section 14A envisaging “... the central 
Government may compulsorily register every citizen of India and issue national identity card to him”.  

While the RGI was designated as the National Registration Authority/Registrar General of Citizen 
Registration, the complete procedure for implementing the scheme was laid down in the Citizenship 
(Registration and Issue of National Identity Cards) Rules, 2003. 
6.8.2 The Committee of Secretaries (CoS) in October,  2006, while examining the proposal of national 
roll-out presented to it based on the outcome of a ‘P ilot Project’ exercise carried out in selected areas 
of 12 States and 1 UT encompassing a population of 30.96 lakhs, completed on 31.3.2009, had noted 
that determination of citizenship was an involved and complicated matter and that the ‘NPR was to be 
a Register of Residents and not Citizens. 
6.8.3 The Committee took note of the fact that there was a column in the NPR Schedule providing for 
declaration of nationality. As it was felt the sa me may create problem for the whole system, the 
Committee during the course of the presentation of RG & CCI suggested that this column may be 
deleted from the NPR schedule. On this, the Ministry in its written reply stated that the question on 
‘Nationality As Declared’ was appr oved by the Government after due deliberations and after getting 
the approval of the Union Cabinet. 
6.8.4 It was also stated that the concern that i llegal migrants would falsely declare themselves as 
Indian Nationals and this would confer upon them  citizenship was not well founded. The NPR, as 
declared by the Government was a register of usual residents. This includes citizens as well as non-
citizens who were present in the country on a particular reference date. 
6.8.5 It was also stated that a signed/authenticated declaration is obtained from the respondent that 
“all the information provided above is true to the best of my knowledge and belief’. It was also 
provided in the form itself that “providing any false information would attract penalties under the 
Citizenship Rules, 2003”. The Ministry also argued that the instruction Manual provides that the 
enumerator should read out the filled in entries to the respondent before taking signature/thumb 
impression, and thus, adequate safeguards had been incorporated. 
6.8.6 On being enquired whether updating of National Register of Citizens (NRC) 1951 and data 
collection of NPR in Assam were going on parallel, the Committee was informed that after consulting 
the Assam Government, the work for data collection for NPR in Assam had began on 1st April, 2010. 
On the other hand, the project of updating the National Register of Citizens (NRC) 1951 was being 
taken up in parallel. 
6.9.1 The Committee takes into account the detail ed arrangements made for Census 2011, 
formation of UIDAI for issuance of Unique Identi fication Number and compilation of National 
Population Register (NPR). The Committee unders tands that Census of the country is a 
cumbersome process. However, it is significant in view of the fact that all the policies, 
programmes and schemes of the government are fr amed on the basis of the conclusions drawn 
from the Census. 
6.9.2 The Committee observes that the first phase of the Census 2011 will be held during April 
and September 2010 for collection of data on Ho uselisting and Housing Census as well as for 
preparation of National Population Register. The Committee apprehends that it will be difficult 
and improper to carry out both exercises together for the following reasons: 
(i) The Census information is collected u nder the Census Act, 1948 which guarantees 
complete confidentiality of  the individual and household data collected. This 

confidentiality clause is known to be a ke y-stone that ensures accuracy and sanctity 
of the Census Data; and 
(ii) The National Population Register (NPR) information is to be collected under the 
Citizenship Act, 1955 read with the Citi zenship (Registration and Issue of National 
Identity Cards) Rules, 2003 and the data collected are not confidential and are meant 
to be used for identifying individuals and household. Thes e make the purpose, 
objectives and the process of the two exercise different and, hence, it is important to 
keep the two exercises separate in order to maintain sanctity of both. 
6.9.3 With regard to the confidentiality of the Ce nsus Data, the Committee apprehends that it 
would be difficult to hold back information contained in the Census Schedule in view of the RTI 
Act, 2005. It is quite likely that even if exem ption clause is invoked under RTI Act, 2005, the 
CIC, the Supreme Court or the High Courts may pass orders for disclosure of information. The 
Committee therefore recommends to Government to keep this aspect in mind. 
6.9.4 The Committee also took note of the statement made by the Ministry of Home Affairs that 
the creation of National Population Register would be a comprehensive identity database in the 
country for better targeting of the benefits and services under the Government 
schemes/programmes, improving planning and strengthening securi ty of the country. However, 
the Committee is of the considered view that the ‘Nationality As  Declared’ clause in the NPR 
Household Schedule has the potential to confer de facto Indian Citizenship to illegal migrants, 
despite the disclaimer attached  to the clause. The Committee is of the opinion that the 
Government should not act in haste. There is a need to have a rethinking on the NPR exercise. 
The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends th at the ‘Nationality’ clause should be 
removed as it has the potents in the long run which may pose a threat to the security and 
integrity of the country. Therefo re, it may be worthwhile cons idering suspension of the NPR 
exercise for the time being. The Committee fu rther recommends that th e work relating to NPR 
preparation should be de-linked from the Census operations.

residential buildings is an on-going one. It provid es for constructing houses, purchase of ready-built 
flats from DDA and other agencies. This scheme aims at providing rent-free accommodation to the 
non-gazzetted officers of Delhi Police.

7.5  Targets and Achievements 
7.5.1 During the Tenth Five Year Plan, it was proposed to construct about 13,000 residential units to 
increase the satisfaction level from the then existing 18.93% to 40%. To meet the target, Delhi Police 
purchased ready-built flats from DDA/GDA, apart from construction of residential quarters on the 
land allotted for Police Station/Police Posts. The Committee is, however, constrained to note that the 
actual housing satisfaction level of Delhi Police was 22.91 %. In total, 395 ready built flats were 
added and 365 new flats were constructed during the Tenth Plan. The targeted housing satisfaction 
level of 40% could not be achieved due to non availability of land/ready-built flats in Delhi as per the 
requirements. The Ministry of Home Affairs in its background note submitted that at the time of 
preparation of Tenth Five Year Plan, sanctioned strength of Delhi Police was 58,701. However, their 
existing strength was 64,645. It was argued that if the strength had not been increased, the satisfaction 
level would have been 25.25%. 
7.5.2 During the Eleventh Five Year Plan, De lhi Police again proposed  to increase housing 
satisfaction level to 40%. Delhi Police, during the last two years, had purchased some ready-built flats 
at Narela, Dwarka, Rohini and Jahangirpuri from DDA and at Kaushambi from GDA, in addition to 
the buildings being constructed by the PWD. The Committee was informed that during the Eleventh 
Plan, it was proposed to construct 10 police station buildings, 11 police post buildings and 577 staff 
quarters under the Plan Scheme. Upto September, 2009, construction of 5 police station buildings, 2 
police post buildings and 143 staff quarters was completed. It was also stated that by 31.3.2010, 3 
police station buildings, 1 police post building and 383 staff quarters would be completed.

commence shortly. Delhi Police had succeeded in ac quiring plots/land for PSs at IFC Ghazipur, IFC 
Pocket Narela and Badarpur during the year. During the year, Delhi Police had taken possession of 39 
quarters at Jaafarpur Kalan on 22.4.2009, and 35 st aff quarters (5 quarters at PS Timarpur and 30 
quarters at Todapur) but the same were not being allotted to Delhi Police personnel in view of 
forthcoming Commonwealth Games (CWG) -2010. 
7.5.6 It was further stat ed that a Project Monitoring Committee (PMC) under the Chairpersonship of 
Joint Secretary (UT), Ministry of Home Affairs had been set up to monitor the building projects of 
Delhi Police. PMC had fixed a target to complete 7 projects by 31.12.2009 and 8 projects by 
31.03.2010. Out of 7 projects (which were to be completed by 31.12.2009),03 projects had been 
completed and handed over to Delhi Police during the year 2009. Other projects were at the 
completion stage. 
7.6  Constraints 
7.6.1 The Committee was informed that one of the ma jor main reasons for the shortfall in the housing 
provision was the increase in the strength of Delhi Police. At the beginning of Tenth Plan i.e. on 
1.4.2002 the sanctioned strength of Delhi Police was 58,877 which has now increased to 83,740 i.e. an 

increase of 24,864 and the construction of houses has not kept pace’ with the increase in strength. The 
Second major reason was non allotment of land by  land. owning agencies, thirdly non-clearance of 
building plans by local bodies like DDA, MCD, NDM C, Delhi Fire Service and DUAC, etc. in time 
Fourthly, non completion of construction by PWD in time and last but not the least, non-allotment of 
ready-built flats by concerned agencies. 
7.7.1 The Committee is constrained to note that there is undue delay in allocating land for 
housing projects by the DDA. Being a Government organization, DDA should not function on 
purely commercial lines like private builders. The Committee recommends that DDA should 
make more concerted efforts to acquire land exclusively for police housing of Delhi Police so 
that the pressing need of the police personnel could be met. Keeping in mind the sensitive law 
and order situation in Delhi, proper functioning of Police Stations and keeping the morale of the 
police force high are of utmost importance. Therefore, making land available for Police Stations 
and Housing Projects, requires immediate attention of the authorities. The Committee therefore 
urges upon the DDA, MCD and other agencies to make land available for Housing projects and 
Police Stations in Delhi with least delay. 
7.7.2 To achieve 40% satisfaction level, Delhi Police needs a total of abou t 28,000 houses against 
about 15,000 houses available with it, thus , leaving a gap of about 13,000 quarters. The 
Committee is concerned that by 201 2-13 only about 7,000 flats are likely to be added, including, 
the projects under PPP model. This is grossly in adequate. Considering the rate at which the 
quarters are being constructed and that additional land is not available with Delhi Police, the 
target can only be achieved in a reasonable period by outright purchase of ready built flats from 
DDA. The Committee is of the view that the Ministry of Home Affairs should take up the matter 
of ready built flats for Delhi Police with DDA and the DDA on its part should also make 
provision for 500-550 flats every year for Delhi Police. 
7.7.3 The Committee feels that on the one hand Delh i Police faces scarcity of housing for its 
personnel and on the other the fu nds allocated, particularly non- plan allocation, are not spent 
fully. During 2006-07 only a fraction was spent where as in 2007-08 and 2008-09, more than 10 
per-cent could not be spent. For achieving physi cal targets, achieving financial targets is 
necessary. Delhi Police should take necessary steps to achieve financial targets. 
7.7.4 The Committee is of the view that police build ings should be decl ared as operational 
buildings as has been done in the case of Railways and P & T so that construction could start 
straightaway. The Committee is also of the view that the average time  taken for declaring 
buildings as operational should also be brought down so as to expedite the process. 
7.7.5 The Committee feels that for achieving 40 per cen t satisfaction level, present allocations - 
both Plan and Non-Plan - are not su fficient. Government should therefore  assess the total 
requirement of funds. Allocations should be increased to enable Delhi Police to achieve desired 
satisfaction within the next five years. 
7.7.6 The Committee does not understand why Delhi Police makes such a target which cannot 
be achieved. During tenth plan 40% satisfaction level was proposed and only 22.91 % was 
achieved. Again 40% is being proposed for Eleven th Plan. This is not a right approach. Delhi 
Police should try to keep realistic targets only. 

7.7.7 The Committee desires that the pitfalls of th e Tenth Plan have to be avoided and the 
physical targets fixed for the eleventh Five Ye ar Plan should be achieved. Clearance of 
proposals and clearance for lands may be taken up at the highest level so that these areas do not 
act as impediments for the achievement of targets. 
7.7.8 In the non-plan scheme also, things are not moving in the right direction. Out of 17 PS 
buildings, only 3 were ex pected to be ready by 31.3.2010 and out of 1,481 staff quarters, 137 
were expected  to be ready by 31.3.2010. This needs to be looked into and steps may be taken for 
timely completion of the buildings and quarters. 
7.7.9 It is a welcome step that about 5,202 quarters are proposed to be constructed under Public 
Private Partnership (PPP) model at Dheerpur and Police Headquarters are proposed to be 
constructed at Parliament Street . As mentioned in the Outcome Budget, the financial mode for 
the projects may be finalized quickly. All other st eps may also be taken to ensure that both the 
projects are completed by 2013. 
7.7.10  All the projects, which are under way in both Plan and Non-Plan Schemes, may be 
completed as per the targets and there should not be any cost and time over-runs. 
7.7.11  The Committee was given to understand that on e of the reasons for the shortfall in the 
housing project was non-completion of constr uction by PWD in time. The Committee takes 
strong exception to this. If PWD does not impr ove its performance, Delhi Police may go in for 
construction by other public sector undertakings. 
7.7.12  The Committee appreciates that a Projec t Monitoring Committee (PMC) under the 
Chairmanship of Joint Secretary (UT), Ministry of Home Affairs has been set up to monitor the 
building plans of Delhi Police. The Committee de sires that this Committee should strictly 
monitor the implementation on a regular basis and ensure that the projects are completed in 
time. 
7.8  HOUSING SCHEME FOR CENTRAL POLICE FORCES 
7.8.1 Central Police Forces have acquired a pivotal role, both in border guarding and in fulfilling 
Central Government’s constitutional responsibility of assisting the States in controlling situations and 
incidents of internal disturbances. A growing recognition of their crucial role and dependence of the 
State Governments on their assistance has translated into a substantial increase in the force strength. 
The Committee is constrained to note that while the absolute strength, in terms of manpower, has gone 
up, the physical infrastructure has not grown in a commensurate manner. Inadequate infrastructure, 
especially family accommodation, has been found to be the major reason for high stress and 
dissatisfaction levels amongst the forces personnel. The Ministry of Home Affairs informed the 
Committee that the Bureau of Police & Research Development (BPR&D), had undertaken an in-depth 
study, which highlighted several cases of suicide and homicide among the forces personnel, due to 
inadequate time spent by them with their families. According to BPR&D findings, separation from 
family, shortage of family accommoda tion, lack of basic facilities at the place of posting and poor 
living conditions, were the predominant factors causing stress among Central Police Forces. The 
Ministry admitted that the finding of BPR&D reinforced the views expressed by the various 

Commissions/Committees that studied the functioning of police personnel and underlined the need for 
suitable and adequate Housing Scheme for Central Police Forces. 
7.8.2 The Forces covered under the Scheme, are Assam Rifles, Border Security Force, Central 
Industrial Security Force, Central Reserve Police Fo rce, Indo-Tibet Border Police, Sashastra Seema 
Bal and National Security Guards. 
7.9  Financial Allocations and Physical Targets 
7.9.1 The Ministry of Home Affairs stated that Housing for CPFs was included as a Plan Scheme 
during the Eighth Five Year Plan (1993-98). A sum of Rs.119.65 crore was incurred against an outlay 
of Rs.130 crore. In the Ninth Five Year Plan, a su m of Rs.316.28 crore was incurred against an outlay 
of Rs.340 crore. For the Tenth Five Year Plan, a sum of Rs.1037.50 crore wa s agreed under the Plan 
Scheme of Police Housing. Simultaneously, allocation under Non-Plan Head was also increased. A 
total amount of Rs.1742.97 crore was spent during the Tenth Plan and non-Plan Heads together. The 
Ministry also stated that in 2007-08, 2008-09 and during the financial year 2009-10, allocation under 
Police Housing Scheme for CPFs had been Rs.425.10 crore, Rs.527.18 crore and Rs.501.99 crore for 
both Plan and non-Plan Heads, respectively. From th e presentation made by the Home Secretary on 
Demands for Grants for 2010-2011, the Committee learnt that during the years 2008-09, and 2009-10, 
the allocations made for the Scheme under plan head were not fully utilised. During 2008-09, Rs. 250 
crore were allocated and only Rs. 157.77 crore were spent. Similarly, during 2009-10, out of Rs. 270 
crore allocated, only Rs. 205.23 crore were spent. 
7.9.2 The Ministry, explaining about the satisfaction level in various forces, stated that the Group of 
Ministers on reforming the National Security System, recommended in 2001, enhancement of housing 
satisfaction level from 14 per cent to 25 per cent. The Committee is, however, unhappy to learn that 
the housing satisfaction level, as on 01.04.2009, was 15.39 per cent. 
7.9.3 The Committee was given to understand that a proposal to provide a special grant for achieving 
the stipulated satisfaction level for housing (25 per cent), in a time-bound manner, was considered by 
a Working Group constituted by Planning Commission in 2006 under the Chairmanship of Home 
Secretary. The objectives of the Working Group were to assess the magnitude of housing and office 
and field infrastructure required for the CPFs in the Eleventh Five Year Plan period. The Group was 
also tasked with the responsibility of reviewing the implementation of housing construction activities 
in the Tenth Plan and suggested polices and progr ammes of improving the housing situation. The 
Group also studied the capacity of CPWD in executing works in a time-bound manner and assessed 
that during the Eleventh Plan, if the housing level were to be taken to 21.5 per cent, then an additional 
resource of Rs. 6,583.10 crore was required. The Working Group recommended that the requirement 
of funds for housing and office infrastructure of CPFs may be met from the Plan funds. In case 
Planning Commission was unable to meet the entire requirement within the ov erall Plan outlay of 
Government of India (both five yearly and annually), then it would be essential to supplement the 
requirement not covered by Planning Commission, through adequate allocation of funds by Ministry 
of Finance on the ‘Non-Plan’ side so that the deficiency was fully met in a time-bound manner. The 
Committee has also learnt that the Planning Commission had approved an outlay of Rs.2500 crore, 
under the Eleventh Plan period, out of the projected requirement of Rs.6584.10 crore recommended by 
the Working Group for reaching a satisfaction level of 21.5 per cent. 

7.9.4 The Ministry in its written reply submitted to the Committee stated that the allocation made 
under BE (Plan) for 2010-2011 was Rs. 297.40 crore and for BE (Non-Plan) it was 232.38 crore. The 
Ministry also stated that it had proposed to launch a mega housing project for CPFs to address 
problems of accommodation for the forces personnel on Public Private Partnership basis. The Ministry 
contended that due to massive housing shortage the funds were inadequate and it would move for 
additional funds at RE stage. 
7.10  Force Strengths and Satisfaction Level 
7.10.1 The force-wise information relating to satisfaction level along with details like sanctioned 
strength, dwelling units authorized, deficiency, etc. as available on 1.4.2009 is as below:

7.11  Evaluation of the Housing Scheme 
7.11.1  The followings are the main findings/recommendations of Bureau of Police Research and 
Development (BPR&D), which conducted evaluation of the Scheme and submitted its report in 
November, 2008: 
(i) With the overall increase in the satisfaction of housing, all the CPFs personnel have 
benefited from investment in this scheme. The scheme is beneficial and should be 
continued; 
(ii) Financial targets during Tenth Plan have been achieved. However, CPFs need more funds 
and enhanced capacity for utilization of funds; 

(iii) To meet physical targets, there is a need fo r review of financial powers of DIGs of CPFs; 
involving agencies other than CPWD and simplifying the procedure to award contracts; 
(iv) To liquidate shortfall in housing, CPFs require increased funding and infrastructure to 
absorb the same within definite time-frame; 
(v) Engineering Wing of CPFs needs to be strengthened for facilitating higher pace of 
construction; and 
(vi) Services of technical expe rts hired through a proper mechanism are required by the CPFs. 
7.11.2  The BPR&D, in the evaluation study, had done an analysis of the projects which were 
implemented in the Tenth Plan. According to this study, 260 projects were implemented by the CPWD 
in which 30 per cent cases had cost overruns and in 90 per cent time overruns. The analysis had also 
been carried out for other agencies that had implemented projects in the Tenth Plan. For example, 
NBCC had implemented 153 projects, and all of them in the North East, the cost overrun was 1.3 per 
cent in a number of projects and the time overrun was 15 per cent of the projects. 
7.11.3  Elaborating on the time taken for clearance of the scheme, the representatives of the Ministry 
of Home Affairs stated that BPR&D found that for sending requisition of construction agency and 
submission of preliminary estimates by the constr uction agency, the time taken was between three to 
six months. For granting administrative approval and sanction of expenditure, the time taken was 
between three to six months. For financial sanction, preparation of detailed estimates and drawings, 
and technical sanction by the technical authority, the time taken was about six months and then one 
month for preparation of tender document, two months for publicity of tender, three months for 
evaluation of tender bids and award of work. 
7.12  Expansion of Forces 
7.12.1 From the background note and the oral evidence tendered before the Committee by the 
representatives of the Ministries of Home Affairs, Urban Development and other Ministries/agencies 
involved, the Committee learnt that many constraints are being faced in achieving the satisfaction 
level. One of them being the rapid expansion of th e Forces. The Joint Secretary, Ministry of Home 
Affairs, stated that given the number of houses, which should have been provided, the total 
authorization of dwelling units would come to 2,34,582. But against them, the number of dwelling 
units which were available, as on 1.4.2009, was 1,19,948, at a satisfaction level of 15.39 per cent. 
According to him, although there was a satisfaction level of 15.39 per cent for the total force, but the 
satisfaction level as per the authorized level was 51.13 per cent which left a gap of 1,14,634 dwelling 
units. According to him, if the new battalions, which had been approved by the Government and were 
likely to be raised by 2013-14 were also taken into account, forty thousand personnel would be further 
added, which would mean that between 2001 to 2013-14, as per the existing authorization, 208 
battalions involving about 2.2 lakh personnel would be added to the security apparatus in the country. 
He also felt that the fund allocation for housing had not kept pace with the requirements of this 
growing force. 
7.13  Capacity Utilization 
7.13.1  To create more than 1.15 lakh houses, a lot of resources are required. However, the allocation 
which has been made is not commensurate with th e requirements or to meet the deficiency. The 
Committee was also informed that, besides the availability of resources, the other limitation that the 

Government had faced was regarding the capacity utilization of funds due to the impediment 
prevailing before 2005 that only CPWD could be engaged for executing these works. In 2005, an 
amendment in the GFR was made which permitted the engagement of other public works 
organisations. The main constraint faced in the creation of housing facilities for CPFs had been the 
slow pace of execution of works by the CPWD. It was further added that even after the amendment in 
the GFR in 2005, for engaging any public works organisation, there was a process of consultation with 
the Ministry of Urban Development. 
7.13.2  When the Committee wanted to know th e constraints faced by the CPWD, the Additional 
Director-General, CPWD stated that the main cons traint was that the CPWD had been awarded the 
jobs in remote areas, such as the North-Eastern areas, Bihar, Jharkhand, Orissa, Jammu and Kashmir 
and Uttar Pradesh. The staff was reluctant to go to these remote areas. The CPWD tried to ensure that 
the contracts were awarded within three to six months until and unless there was a specific reason, for 
example, land acquisition, or a new design was being developed which would take some time. 
7.13.3  Otherwise three to six months was the normal time which CPWD would take to award the 
work. 
7.13.4  Besides, the above mentioned constraints, the following reasons were also attributed by the 
Ministry of Home Affairs for delay in completion of works by the CPWD: 
(i) Delay in obtaining the approval from local bodies, wherever applicable. 
(ii) Delay in preparation of detailed estimates after receipt of sanction. 
(iii) Delay in preparation of working dr awings by Architectural Wing of CPWD. 
(iv) Splitting of high value sanction into smaller works for calling of tenders thereby involving 
many contractors in completion of a project. 
(v) Delay in processing/finalization of tenders. 
(vi) Lack of proper monitoring/s upervision of projects by CPWD. 
(vii) Lack of coordination between civil, electrical and Architectural Wing of CPWD. 
7.14  Local Problems 
7.14.1 The Committee was informed that some areas had problems of law and order, problems of 
terrain and less time available in the year due to natural conditions because of which the construction 
activities were hampered. There had been instances where the construction works were allotted, but 
the contractors were not able to take up the job because of some local problems. 
7.15  Land Acquisition 
7.15.1  Another difficulty that the CPFs have faced is land acquisition. The Committee was informed 
that in various States where these facilities had to be created, the responsibility for acquiring land lies 
with the State Governments. This is an area in which a lot of time is taken for completing the 
acquisition process. 
7.16  Initiatives taken by the Government 
7.16.1 The representatives of the Ministry of Home  Affairs informed the Committee that the Ministry 
had taken several initiatives for improving the procedures for expeditious implementation of the 

housing scheme. One of the initiatives was that the sanctioning powers of the Directors-General of the 
Police Forces had been enhanced, for major works, from Rs.3 crore to Rs.5 crore and for minor works 
from Rs.10 lakh to Rs.15 lakh and from Rs.20 lakh to Rs.30 lakh for residential and non-residential 
buildings. Similarly, for departmental works, which could be carried out on their own, for those 
Forces which had their Engineering Wing, the financial powers had been raised from Rs.40 lakh to 
Rs.60 lakh for major works and from Rs.5 lakh to Rs.10 lakh for minor works. It was also stated that 
comprehensive guidelines had been issued for formulation and processing of the works proposals 
including the procedure for land acquisition. 
7.16.2  The Committee was also informed that a Memorandum of Understanding had been entered 
into with the CPWD for monitoring the progress of wo rks. In this MoU, the responsibilities of the 
forces and the CPWD had been clear ly mentioned. Various checklists had been provided so that the 
project preparation could be more  methodical. Similar MoU had been finalised with the NBCC. The 
Ministry was undertaking regular monitoring and the Forces would also regularly review the 
implementation of the schemes. Regular coordination meetings were to be held with the CPWD and 
other implementing agencies. The representatives of the Ministry also stated that one of the initiatives, 
which the Ministry had recently taken, was to explore the possibility of a public-private partnership 
for creating the housing infrastructure. In this init iative, the objective was to create about one lakh 
houses for the CPF personnel. For this, the Ministry was in active dialogue with the Ministry of 
Finance, which had engaged the Asian Development Bank for carrying out a technical consultancy for 
preparing a feasibility report. It was also stated th at the Ministry was expecting that the feasibility 
report would be completed soon, after which the contours of the scheme would clearly emerge. It was 
further elaborated that the Pub lic-Private Partnership (PPP) Model would remove the burden of 
execution on the public works organizations; nor would it impose any immediate liability on the 
financial resources. The Ministry was hoping for the model to evolve and see what could be the 
options which would be suggested by the feasibility study to take advantage of some private 
investment in creating this infrastructure which would be paid as a deferred payment in due course of 
time. It was added that the concept of PPP model was one of the measures, which Government hoped 
to be able to adopt to overcome the capacity and the utilization of funds problems. 
7.16.3 It was also stated that in order to remove hurdles in coordination between various agencies, the 
following steps had been taken by the Ministry: 
(i) The progress of major works is reviewed on a monthly basis at the Ministry level and 
quarterly reviews are also conducted by the Committee of Additional Secretaries. 
(ii) The CPFs are having periodic coordination meetings with execution agencies. 
(iii) A system of fast track clearance of infrast ructure related proposals has been put in place. 
(iv) Formal monitoring mechanism has now been instituted in each CPF. 
(v) A number of cases have been approved under Rule 126 of the GFR (2005) permitting 
engagement of Public Works Organizations other than CPWD for executing some major 
projects. Earlier general relaxation for engaging Central Government construction 
agencies existed only in the case of remote areas like the North-East. 
7.17.1 The Committee observes that just a little more than 15% of the Force personnel entitled 
to family accommodation, are able to avail the facility. Force personnel, who remain deployed at 

international borders as well as in violence infested regions, are engaged in grappling with 
militancy and insurgency throughout the year. They do not even have the mental comfort of 
their families having proper accommodation, medi cal facilities etc. while they are away from 
home. 
7.17.2  The Committee taking note of the findings of the BPR&D study feels that the Scheme is 
beneficial and recommends that it should be continued. 
7.17.3  The Committee takes a serious note of the fact that the deficiency of the housing units as 
on 1.4.1009 was 114634. If the new battalions, which had been approved by the Government and 
were likely to be raised by 201 3-14 were also taken into ac count, forty thousand personnel 
would be further added. The allocations have been made as per the current requirements only. 
As this rate, the gap would keep on increasing and it would be a never ending phenomenon. The 
Committee agrees that the allocations have not kept pace with the increase in number of 
personnel in various Central Police Forces and this situation is likely to go from bad to worse. 
The Committee therefore recommends that the Government should seriously assess the financial 
requirements for the housing of the CPF personne l. The Ministry informed the Committee that 
due to massive housing shortage, the funds were inadequate and they would move for additional 
funds at RE stage. The Committee suggests that a dditional funds may be sought at RE 2010-11 
stage. 
7.17.4  The Committee recommends that to supplemen t the funds requiremen t, not covered by 
the Planning Commission, Ministry  of Finance may be allowed to make adequate allocation 
from the ‘Non-Plan’ side also so as to enable the forces to meet the housing requirements. 
7.17.5  The CPFs are facing the problem of land ac quisition for construction of houses. The 
responsibility of acquiring land lies with the Stat e Governments and a lot of time is taken for 
completing the acquisition process. This area needs to be addressed. The Committee desires that 
the Home Secretary may address the Chief Secretar ies of all the State Governments about the 
necessity of early clearances for the land acquisition. 
7.17.6 The construction of houses for CPFs also suffers due to problems of capacity utilization of 
the implementing agencies. The Committee ther efore suggests that besides CPWD, more and 
more public sector agencies may be considered for executing the projects. 
7.17.7  The Ministry proposed to launch a mega hous ing project for CPFs to address problems 
of accommodation for the forces personnel on P ublic Private Partnership basis. The Committee 
desires that an early decision may be taken in this regard. 
7.17.8  The Committee appreciates the amendment made in Rule 126 of GFR allowing the 
involvement of other Public Works Organisati ons as the same would help in avoiding 
unnecessary delays and other regulatory bottlen ecks in completion of housing projects for 
Central Police Forces in time. However, the purpo se will be defeated if there are inordinate 
delays in giving nod/concurrence. The Committee, therefore recommends that a reasonable time 
limit should be imposed in giving clearance by Ministry of Urban Development/CPWD. The 
Committee also recommends that Engineering Wing s of CPFs be further strengthened so that 
they can be entrusted more project works for spee ding construction of residential as well as 
other type of accommodation. 

7.17.9  A well approach coordinated is needed amon gst various agencies so that the regulatory 
and logistic requirements are me t well before the projects are given a go-ahead and that the 
projects do not get stuck at the implementation stage due to pr ocedural hurdles. Clearances 
from local bodies through an institutionalized mechanism, with all-round support from the 
clearance agencies should be done on fast track basis. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

OBSERVATIONS/CONCLUSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS OF THE  
COMMITTEE –– AT A GLANCE 
MODERNISATION OF STATE POLICE FORCE (MPF) 
  The Committee is unhappy to note that there is no Management Information System 
(MIS) for proper monitoring and concurrent evaluation of the Scheme of Modernisation of 
Police Forces. Now that the CMC has offered to de velop the software on the lines of MISs done 
for the other centrally sponsored programmes of the Government of  India, the Ministry should 
take immediate steps to firm up the project so that a scientific monitoring mechanism of the 
scheme is in place at the earliest.   
  The Committee is of the consid ered view that the Scheme is of utmost importance so far as 
the maintenance of law and order in the Stat es is concerned. Therefore, the earmarked 
provision for the Scheme must be utilized fully  and optimally. In this connection, active 
participation of State Governme nts is necessary. The Committee, therefore, impresses upon the 
State Governments that Utilisation Certificates may be submitted in time so that they could 
avail of the funds under the scheme. The Committee takes a serious view of  the fact that even 
some of the States affected by left wing extremism are not properly availing the Scheme. Such 
States have not submitted the UCs for 2007-08 which would obviously deprive them in getting 
their full share of funds in future. The Committee fi nds it disturbing that on one side, the States 
are reeling under the impact of left wing extrem ism, as shown by the recent example of the 
Dantewada incident on 6.4.2010, in which a la rge number of security personnel were butchered 
by the ultras. On the other hand, the affected Stat es display lack of interest in availing of the 
Scheme. The Committee desires that the Minister of Home Affairs should discuss the matter 
with all the concerned Chief Ministers. (Para 3.1.12) 
  The Committee appreciates introduction of  the system of Concurrent Audit to monitor 
timely and appropriate use of the funds sanc tioned and released under the MPF Scheme. The 
Committee recommends that the Ministry of Home Affairs should apprise it of the highlights as 
well as the corrective steps contem plated in the light of the Action Taken Reports received from 
the State Governments.  (Para 3.1.13) 
  The Committee hopes that the report on im pact assessment of the Scheme might have 
been submitted by now, as inte nded by the Ministry. If so, the Ministry should prepare action 
plan on the findings.   (Para 3.1.14) 
  The Committee, while appreciating the layi ng of more focus on the components of 
operational buildings such as po lice stations, police outposts and police lines as a part of 
effective budgeting for Annual Acti on Plan of States under the Modernization of Police Forces, 
feels that earmarking Rs.2.00 crore per district for naxal affected Districts, is meagre. Keeping 
in view the use of latest weapons by the outlaws and their stra tegy to attack police 
establishments, the allocation and facilities to police establishments and personnel should be 
appropriately increased. However,  this should not be done across the board and should be 
linked to utilization.                                                                                                        (Para 3.1.15) 
  The Committee feels that in addition to  providing the State and Centre Police Forces 
modern arms, equipment, surveillance devices, better communications facilities, etc., it is 

equally important to provide them  basic and specialized training . The Government should also 
not only train them for better interaction and communication skills with the public; but also 
raise their discipline, attitude, behaviour,  responsibility and accountability quotient’ and 
motivation and morale levels. (Para 3.1.16) 
CIVIL DEFENCE 
  The Committee has made detailed recommendations in its On e Hundred and Thirty-sixth 
Report on the Revamping and Revitalization of Civil Defence in the country and it intends to 
present an Action Taken Report thereon, whic h would contain detailed observations and 
recommendations in the light of action taken by  the Government. Sin ce the Government is 
revamping the Civil Defence set up, fund requi rements may be assessed properly. Regular 
monitoring on the revamping ma y be made. Enrolling young and active volunteers should be 
made a regular feature. The total volunteer force should also be trained in disaster 
management. The Committee urges upon the Gov ernment to create awareness amongst all 
citizens for the imperative need to discharge their fundamental duties, to uphold and protect the 
sovereignty, unity and integrity of the country and to defend the country  and render national 
service when called upon to do so. The Committee also urges upon the Government to prepare 
detailed plans to provide training  to all able-bodied citizens so  that they can perform their 
fundamental duties.                                                                                                           (Para 3.2.7) 
NATIONAL DISASTER MANAGEMENT 
  The Committee understands the need for st rengthening mitigatio n, preparedness and 
response measures for effective and efficient management of natural as well as manmade 
disasters keeping in view vulner able geo-climatic conditions, of the country such as 58.6% 
earthquake prone landmass, 12% area floods pron e and 68% cultivable area drought prone. 
The Committee, however, observes  that most of the new program mes/augmentation of ongoing 
programmes are at the rudimentary stages. It would take considerable time for the new 
programmes to take off. The Comm ittee is constrained to note that even though the Act was 
passed in 2005, there is no perceptible improvement in the di saster management preparedness 
in the country. The 26/11 terrorist attack in Mumbai is a glaring example when it took a lot of 
time for the NSG commandoes to reach the spot . There are several inst ances of handling of 
major fires, floods/droughts and major accidents, includi ng recently radioactive leakages 
(Cobalt 60), which should have been tackled with great promptness and effectively. The 
Committee therefore, urges upon the Ministry of Home Affairs and NDMA to take this task as a 
major challenge so that tangible results are vi sible for the countrymen. National Institute of 
Disaster Management is functioning from rente d premises. Land/Buildings for three battalions 
of NDRF are yet to be acquired. Though Phase I of National Emergency Communication Plan 
has been completed, the Committee apprehends that the provision of a meagre amount of Rs. 10 
crore is not sufficient in view of the vastness of the country and its vu lnerability. Therefore, 
Fund requirements need to be re-assessed. Th e Committee also recommends that 124 posts
