sanctioned for the NDMA may be filled up at the ea rliest. There should not be any delay in this 
regard. (Para 3.3.4) 
  The Committee in its 115th Report on the Disaster Management Bill 2005 dealt with in 
detail about the mechanism to be  put in place for tackling the natural disasters like earthquake, 

cyclones, floods. The Committee understands that there should be requisite mechanism for 
drawing up and monitoring impl ementation of the disaster management plans, ensuring 
measures for preventing and mi tigating disaster. The NDMA may also chalk out a plan for 
giving training to Gov ernment officials, on disaster manage ment. Training on this should be 
made compulsory for all new recruits. 
(Para 3.3.5) 
HOME GUARDS 
  The Committee appreciates the role played by the Home Guards in assisting police in 
maintaining law and order and in various othe r security related duties including disaster 
management. The Committee agrees with the suggestions made by 2 nd ARC. In the present day 
scenario, disaster management a nd para medical traini ng are very important and these should 
be made an integral part of the training of  Home Guards. The Committee is however given to 
understand that the picture is not so rosy as it is being projected. Home Guard Jawans do not 
receive the needed moral support, proper leadership and financial incentives. They are always 
under the feeling of job insecurity and are inducted mostly on part-time basis. The Committee is 
unhappy to note that the States are not submitting their claims on time and therefore allocation 
is getting reduced and so is the expenditure whic h also gets reduced reluctantly. It is an 
unfortunate situation where States are not properly making use of the scheme and the funds. 
The Centre should write to all the States to implement the scheme in lett er and spirit and make 
their claims on a regular basis. The Committee recommends that the Union Home Minister may 
take up this issue with all the State Governme nts impressing upon them the need to make 
optimum use of the scheme, submit claims regular ly so that adequate f unds could be allocated 
and released. A review of the role and responsib ilities of Home Guards may be undertaken to 
revitalize their role and consequent augmentation of the resources. 
(Para 3.4.8) 
FIRE SERVICES 
  The Committee having taken note of the major fire accidents with alarming regularity in 
the country, causing heavy causalit ies, particularly in commercial buildings in Bangalore and 
the Stephen’s Court Building in Ko lkata, wishes to highlight the weaknesses in the fire fighting 
mechanism of the country. The Committee notes wi th dismay that lessons have not been learnt 
from the heart-rendering incidents of Dabw ali (1995) Kumbakonam  (2004), and Uphaar 
Cinema, (Delhi) (1997) tragedies. The National  Building Code (NBC) published in 1970 and 
updated in 2005 does prescribe th e minimum fire safety and rescue measures to be provided in 
the buildings but what is missing the actual en forcement mechanism. Transparency in giving 
clearance to construction of structures must be in place and public ac cess to building safety 
data, a practice common in other parts of the world, ought to adopted in our country. The 
Committee, therefore, recomme nds that Government must undertake required steps for 
strengthening of fire fighting mechanism. The old and the new structures should comply with 
the mandated rules and the State Governments must be persuaded to ensure enforcement and 
compliance of the fire fighting measures and safety code. The Committee also recommends that 
a complete overhauling of fire services needs on an emergent basis be done. The Committee 

takes a serious note of the fact that the allocation of Rs. 38 crore made at BE 2009-10 was 
reduced to Rs. 14 crore at RE. This needs to be seriously looked into. (Para 3.5.6) 
  The Committee desires that reassessment of  the fire and emergency preparedness for the 
Commonwealth Games in Delhi may be done as  any untoward incident during the Games may 
bring bad name to the nation. (Para 3.5.7) 
MODERNIZATION OF PRISON ADMINISTRATION 
  The Committee in its One Hundred and Fo rty Second Report on Implementation of 
Central Scheme of Modernisation of Prison Admi nistration, made a detailed study of the same 
and made several recommendations for prison infrastructure a nd correctional administration. 
The Ministry of Home Affairs in its Action Taken Notes stated that the Committee’s 
recommendations, had been forwarded to the St ate/UT Governments for due compliance and 
the experience gained during the first phase of th e implementation of the scheme would be duly 
utilized in its second phase. The Committee fo und the Action Taken Notes of the Ministry 
evasive and incomplete and asked the Ministry to  furnish concrete Acti on Taken Notes on its 
recommendations after due consultation with the States/UT Governments. While proper Action 
Taken Notes of the Ministry are still awaited,  the Committee is surpri sed to note that the 
Government has decided to defer the second phase of the scheme while the jails in the country 
needs immediate modernization. Howeve r, the Committee would make detailed 
recommendations on the subject in  its Action Taken Report (ATR) after receiving proper 
Action Taken Notes from the Mi nistry of Home Affairs in the matter. In the meanwhile, the 
Committee would like the Government to reconside r the decision to defer the second phase of 
the scheme.    (Para 3.6.4) 
DISPLACED PERSONS FROM SRI LANKA 
  The Committee takes note of the fact that  a large number of Sri Lankan Tamils have 
crossed over to India during the internal disturba nces in that country and the Government of 
India is providing relief to them on humanitarian grounds. The Committee however desires that 
no Sri Lankan Tamil who has cro ssed over to India should starve even if India was not a 
signatory to the 1951 Convention, and its 1967 Protocol. (Para 3.7.9) 
DISPLACED PERSONS FROM AFGHANISTAN 
  The Committee takes note of the fact that a large number of displaced persons from 
Afghanistan have crossed over to India due to in ternal disturbances in that country and that 
Government is providing relief to them on humanitarian grounds. (Para 3.8.5) 
COASTAL SECURITY 
  The Committee wishes to highlight the impera tive need for a robust coastal security in the 
backdrop of the modalities of the 26/11 attack. The Committee recommends that the 
Government should make all efforts to ensu re that comprehensive and fool-proof coastal 
security is put in place so that another 26/11 type attack may not take place. (Para 3.9.2) 
CRIME AND CRIMINAL TRACKING AND NETWORKING SYSTEM (CCTNS) 

  The Committee appreciates the adoption of pr inciples of e-governan ce and creation of a 
nationwide networked infrastruc ture of IT -enabled state-o f-the-art tracking system of 
“investigation of crime and detection of criminals” in real time, which is a critical requirement 
in the context of the present day internal security  scenario. The Committee believes that the 
project would enable a comprehensive and integr ated system for enhancing the efficiency and 
effectiveness of policing at the police station level and arresting the increasing criminal 
activities. The Committee recommends that the a llocated funds for the purpose should be 
optimally utilized for timely completion of the project.   
SECURITY FOR COMMONWEALTH GAMES 2010 
  Organization of a sporting event like the Commonwealth Games is a major challenge for 
any country not only from the point of view of investment in infrastructure but also from the 
security aspect, given the global security scenario. The Committee urges upon the Central 
Government, Government of NCT, Delhi and all ag encies concerned to ensure that there should 
not be any lapse in security arrangements for want of resources. Government may therefore 
provide funds to put in place a fool proof security system. (Para 3.11.6) 
  The Committee takes note of the fact that  an Empowered Security Committee (ESC) has 
been constituted to co-ordinate, oversee a nd approve the security arrangement for the 
Commonwealth Games 2010. In this regard, the Committee would like to recommend that co-
ordination and monitoring need to  be done at every level right upto the lowest level of security 
deployment. Intelligence agencies should also be actively involved in co-ordination. There should 
not be any time gap in the dissemination and pe rcolation of intelligence inputs. The Committee 
also recommends that the security personnel may be sensitized to be people-friendly. (Para 3.11.7) 
  The Committee expresses its displeasure ov er the under-utilization of funds meant for 
modernization of Traffic and Communication Ne twork for which Rs. 40 crore was allocated in 
BE 2009-10 but only an amount of Rs. 10.63 crore could be utilized, which indicates serious 
lacunae in the implementation aspects of the project. It is a matter of great concern that the 
project was not initially timed to be comp leted before the Commonwealth Games 2010. The 
Committee is perturbed to note that the remaini ng amount has been appropriated for other 
purpose namely, ‘Delhi Police Building Programme’. (Para 3.11.8) 
NATIONAL INVESTIGATION AGENCY (NIA) 
  The Committee appreciates the constitution of the National Investigation Agency (NIA) 
and hopes that necessary steps would be taken for ma king available the required infrastructure 
so that it can discharge its mandate efficiently. The Commi ttee recommends that the vacant 
posts in NIA should be filled up at the earliest. The Committee also recommends that permanent 
office accommodation should be provided to NIA within the shortest possible time. (Para 3.12.4) 
BUREAU OF POLICE RESEARCH AND DEVELOPMENT (BPR&D) 
  BPR&D is the premier police research organization which has the pivotal responsibility of 
monitoring the training needs and quality of training in States. However, the Committee 
expresses its displeasure over th e fact that out of Rs. 20 crore allocation, only Rs. 2.25 crore 
could be spent during the year 2009-10. Though th e Ministry has tried to  explain the reasons, 
the Committee is not convinced with those ex planations. The Committee therefore strongly 

recommends that the “Training Intervention” scheme be implemented without further 
procrastination. The Committee also recomme nds that the projects relating to BPR&D 
headquarters, Central Detective Training Schools at Gandhinagar and Lucknow and Central 
Academy for Police Training (CAPT), Bhopal shou ld be completed as per the current targets. 
Being the premier police research organization, BPR&D needs more staff. The additional posts 
recently sanctioned should be filled up without delay. 
(Para 3.13.7) 
REDRESSAL OF GRIEVANCES OF CISF DIRECT RECRUIT OFFICERS 
  The Committee, while noting the steps taken by the Government in regard to the redressal 
of grievances of Group ‘A’ CISF Officers , expresses its concern that despite the 
recommendations made in 54th, 59th, 83rd Reports of this Committee, the Nikhil Kumar 
Committee and Bhandari Committee recommendation s and the few steps taken by MHA, the 
grievances of Group ‘A’ officers of CISF remai n unresolved. There has been a severe attrition 
amongst these officers and the rest are totally demoralized. The very fact that many of them 
have been petitioning the auth orities for the redressal of thei r grievances is a pointer for 
immediate action on the part of the Government. 
(Para 3.14.3) 
  The Committee urges upon the Government to implement the scheme at the earliest so 
that the outstanding grievances of the Direct Recruit Officers could be addressed substantially. 
(Para 3.14.7) 
ALLOCATION OF BADP AND HADP 
  The Committee is of the view  that Government should finall y arrive at a decision without 
further delay, with regard to the allocation of BADP and HADP which has remained unresolved 
between Ministry of Home Affa irs and Ministry of DoNER, fo r better implementation of the 
schemes. 
(Para 3.15.3) 
PROCUREMENT OF BULLET PROOF JACKETS 
  The Committee is of the considered view th at the level of body armour provided to the 
Central para-military forces should be raised to level IV category. The Ministry should make 
concerted efforts to ensure that the next proc urement takes place on time as per level IV 
category, so that those could be used by our fo rces in combat operation s. It should also be 
ensured that the armour is made available to all the personnel in the combat zone. (Para 3.16.5) 
TRAINING OF POLICE PERSONNEL 
  The current security scenario  of the country being what it is, the urgency to have Quick 
Response Teams and Commandos in State Police and Central Police Organisations needs hardly 
to be emphasized. In fact during the meetin g of the Committee held on 23 March, 2010, the 
Home Secretary informed that the suggestion that all forces should have Quick Response Teams 
and Commandos right across the States, has been accepted. (Para 3.17.8) 
  The total strength of the po lice force in all the States and UTs as on 30th September, 2009 
stood at 15,04,153 which was projected to go up to 15,81,439 by 31st March,2010. The total 
strength of the CPMFs as on 1st April, 2009, was 7,79,552*. As against this massive force, the 
infrastructure in terms of four training institut es offering commando tr aining with only 4270 

slots available during the last five years, as info rmed by the Ministry of Home Affairs, present a 
dismal picture of the preparedness of the police forces to fight terrorist, insurgents and left wing 
extremists, who have no dearth of latest arms and weapons, who are highly trained in guerrilla 
warfare and highly politically and ideologically motivated. (Para 3.17.9) 
  The Committee believes that in addition to the information furnished by the Ministry of 
Home Affairs to it, there would be more specialized  training centres in some States, particularly 
the Naxal affected States, to impart commando training to th e forces, but their numbers would 
be miniscule as compared to the need of training of 8.5 % of the total strength of the State Police 
Forces. While surfing the net, the Committee came  across the information relating to a training 
centres for jungle warfare located in Kank er (Chhattisgarh) and another training centre 
Chandaka (Orissa) and yet anoth er recently set up by Government of Maharashtra to train the 
recently raised Force One battalion of Maharashtra Police.   (Para 3.17.10) 
  The Committee also learnt that the Centra l Government proposed to set up an Advanced 
Training Centre for CRPF Commandos in Uttar Pr adesh. The Government of Bihar is also 
reported to have plan to set up four institutes to impart training in counter insurgency and 
jungle warfare.  
(Para 3.17.11) 
  Be that as it may, the Committee, therefore, with all the emphasis at its command, urges 
upon the Ministry of Home Affairs to take this aspect of co mmando training seriously and 
reformulate its Policy on Education and Training of Central Police Forces with a view to 
ensuring that adequate number of police personnel, especially  trained in commando, counter-
insurgency, anti-terrorist and guerrilla warfare, are available to counter the menace of 
terrorism, insurgency and left wing extremism in all their hues. Besides, the inadequacies in 
training infrastructure, the Committee has been given to understand that there is shortage of 
trainers and the Government shou ld look into this aspect by  recruiting sufficient number of 
trainers and also updation of the Training of Trainers programme. Th e Central Government 
may also proactively assists the State Governme nt particularly those affected by cross-border 
terrorism, insurgency and left wing extremism, in  setting up Training Centres for training their 
police personnel in guerilla a nd jungle warfare and help them  set up special units like the 
Greyhound of Andhra Pradesh Police and COBRA of CRPF. (Para 3.17.12) 
NAXALISM 
  It is public knowledge that the Maoists have become an impediment to development 
projects in most backward regions and tribal dominated forest areas and are runni ng parallel 
administrations, siphoning money from Government funds and indulging in extortion from local 
people, contractors and others. The Maoists pr opagate that administrative and political 
institutions are inadequate; espouse local demands; take advantage of the prevalent 
dissatisfaction and feeling of injustice among ex ploited segments of the population and claim to 
form an alternative utopian Government. The Committee understands that by providing 
livelihood, land and proper wages, with good governance and proper utilization of funds 
allocated to the respective States for various  Government sponsored development schemes, 
people could be weaned away from naxalism and this menace could be contained. The 
Committee, therefore, is of the view that the a pproach to the naxalite problem needs a blend of 

firm handling with sensitive approach to the developmental aspects. Th e Government should 
also involve the political parties, people’s representatives, Panchayati Raj institutions and above 
all the masses, in all its initiatives to contain the menace. (Para 4.10.1) 
  The Committee is of the view that capaci ty building is required to be done in the 
agriculture sector, bringing in modern technologies, so that the young people in the areas 
affected by LWE are able to take up agriculture for a gainful employment. (Para 4.10.2) 
  The Committee views with concern that th e youth, both educated and uneducated, in the 
LWE-affected areas do not get sufficient employment opportunities and they fall prey in the 
hands of the extremists. It is, therefore, necessary that urgent efforts are made to improve the 
skills of the youth so that  they may take up activities for gai nful employment in various sectors, 
including self-employment. Similarly, the Government should try to improve the employment 
opportunities for the youth in the affected ar eas. The Committee agrees with the view of the 
Expert Group of the Planning Co mmission that the extremists, by and large, take up issues, 
which are basically related to poverty, deprivation and alienation of the poor and the landless.(Para 4.10.3) 
  It is an admitted fact that the implementa tion of various schemes, particularly of the 
Ministry of Rural Development and the Ministry of Tribal Affairs,  is deficient and there is no 
proper administrative control and monitoring whic h obviously lead to dissatisfaction among the 
people and they fall easy prey to the extremists. Efforts, therefore, should be made in the 
direction of implementing the flagship program mes in a proper manner to ensure that the 
beneficiaries feel part of the mainstream of the society. (Para 4.10.4) 
  The Government should iden tify the factors responsible for tardy implementation of the 
schemes and ensure that its benefits reach the target ed groups and they do not feel alienated. In 
this context, the Government should address the issues related to land acquisition, land-
alienation, resettlement and rehabilitation; issues pe rtaining to laws applicable to the scheduled 
and Tribal Areas, on priority. The Committee would also like to highlight the fact that in some 
States elections are not held regularly to the Panchayats and other local bodies, which results in 
lack of participation of peop les’ representatives in the im plementation of the flagship 
programmes. It is therefore, equally important that elections are held  regularly to the PRI 
Institutions so that public representatives are actively involved at th e grass root level in 
implementation of the schemes and projects which would directly help in bringing the people 
back to the mainstream. (Para 4.10.5) 
  The areas affected by LWE a re mostly in the central parts of India which has less density 
of population, more density of forest areas; le ss infrastructure facilities , including education, 
health and roads. It is, therefore, essential that  these infrastructure facilities are improved and 
developed at a much faster pace than has been projected and implemented so far. More and 
more roads are required to be built so that it  gives employment to jo bless people, including 
tribals while connecting those areas with the rest of the country. (Para 4.10.6) 
  Education is the basic ingredient of huma n resource development. Lack of educational 
facilities in the naxal-affected areas is an im porta
 nt factor accountable for the spread of the 
movement. More and more schools with free boarding are required to be opened in the affected 
areas. Vacancies for the posts of teachers may be  filled up and to the exte nt possible, educated 

youth from those areas may be taken as teachers through a special drive. Technical and 
engineering institutions may also be opened in those areas. (Para 4.10.7) 
  Lack of healthcare in the naxal-affected  areas is another basic issue that leads to 
dissatisfaction among the people. Special packages  are required to be formulated for providing 
health care for the people in the affected area s, both under the existing schemes as well as new 
schemes. New primary health centres and sub-centres  need to be opened and it should be made 
compulsory for the new doctors to serve in thos e areas by giving some incentives to them. 
Similarly, in some selected places, bigger hospit als are also I required to be opened with all the 
facilities, including major specialit ies. Special efforts are needed to  be made for health care of 
women and children. The Government  should also explore streng thening ‘Anganwadis’ in all 
those areas with special packages. 
(Para 4.10.8) 
  The Committee understands that most of the habitations in the affected areas are devoid 
of electricity, which makes them an easy have n for the extremists. Th erefore, the Government 
should start a new programme of electrifying a ll the habitations in the extremist-affected areas 
with specific targets fixed. Electricity is a basic requirement in the modern days and once this is 
available, people will be able to take up various activities for gainful employment and children 
will be able to devote their time for studies, etc. (Para 4.10.9) 
  Drinking water is another basic requirement for the people. In most of the habitations in 
the extremist-affected areas, people do not have access to potable drinking water and even if it is 
available, people, particularly wo men, have to trek long distances  to obtain a pot of water. The 
Government should make use of  the existing schemes availabl e and provide drinking water 
facilities in all the habitations by the end of the Eleventh Five-year Plan. (Para 4.10.10) 
  It is a fact that in naxal violence, peop le, from all sides, incl uding the local people, 
extremists as well as the security forces lose their lives. Though the multi-pronged development 
strategy is required to be planne d and implemented, it should al so be kept in one’s mind that 
violence also needs to be addre ssed. Every one knows that people in the affected areas do not 
support the movement by their heart but circu mstances, coercion a nd the under-development 
make them surrender to the movement. Therefore, while taking up developmental activities, the 
strategies, as per the area and location, will have  to be adopted to counter the violent activities. 
One particular strategy will not help for a ll the areas and States. There needs to be a 
coordination between the Central paramilitary fo rces and the State security organizations. 
While preparing strategy for such operations by security forces, sufficient training should be 
given before sending them to the field for facing the extremists. In fact, the local youth are the 
right persons, who know the topography of the areas. Therefore, local youth on a large scale 
should be involved through empl oyment in the police forces and CPMFs for operations within 
those areas to resist any attack from the extremis ts. Police force should be  modernized in terms 
of latest and modern weaponry, communication, mobility and infrastructure. They should be 
supplied more Mine Protected Vehicles and th e Central Para Military Forces should be 
deployed on long-term basis. (Para 4.10.11) 
  The Committee also feels that the Central Pa
 ra Military Forces trained in guerilla. 
warfare should be deployed in all the sensitive di stricts of the affected areas. There should be 

more Counter Insurgency and Jungle Warfare (CIJW) Colleges opened  on the lines of 
Vairengte in Mizoram and another training centr e at Kanker in Chhattis garh, for training of 
Police Personnel for counter-insurgency operations. The Co mmittee understands that the 
biggest limitation while conducting  counter-naxal operations, is th e lack of helicopter support 
from the Government. The G overnment should therefore pr ovide helicopter support for 
conducting operations and for reconnaisance. 
(Para 4.10.12) 
  The Committee feels that the incidents of ja il break and attack on police establishments 
are direct challenges to the authority of the State and if the jails a nd other law enforcement 
mechanisms are prone to naxalit e attack, then nothing can be treated as secure and safe. The 
Committee recommends that the Central Governme nt, in close and active coordination with 
naxal-affected State Government s should review and strengthen th e security of sensitive jails, 
especially where naxalites or activists are lodged, to prevent recurrence of such incidents in 
future. The Committee understands that the incide nts of attacks and jail breaks reflect the 
inadequacies of intelligence agencies. Therefore, intelligence network in the naxal-affected areas 
should therefore be strengthened. 
(Para 4.10.13) 
  The Committee is of the consid ered view that the justice deli very system particularly in 
the lower judiciary should be revamped to reve rse the dangerous trend of justice dispensation 
by the so called ‘peoples courts’. (Para 4.10.14) 
  Money is the life-line of every extremist ac tivity. Therefore, all the channels from which 
the naxalites get funding, should be blocked. There should be a proper mechanism to investigate 
and check the extortion rackets being run by the naxalites and to punish all those who are found 
guilty of funding naxalites.  (Para 4.10.15) 
  The Committee feels that the approach of th e Government for dealing with the naxalites 
should be consistent, holistic, multifaceted and collective. There  can be no two opinions that 
naxalism has become the “single biggest” threat to the country’s security. The Committee is of 
the view that the fight against naxalism should be at the developmental,  political and law and 
order fronts. The Committee also takes note of the statement made by the Home Minister while 
replying a Question in the Raj ya Sabha on 15th July, 2009 in which he conceded that the 
Government underestimated the challenge of le ft wing extremism for years. The Committee, 
therefore, is of the considered vi ew that the Government should take this menace seriously and 
make every effort to contain it. 
(Para 4.10.16) 
  The Committee agrees with the policy of the Government that talks with the naxalites can 
be held only when they surre nder their arms and abjure violen ce. The Committee thinks that 
the government should request a ll the naxal affected States to  implement the “surrender-cum-
rehabilitation” scheme for the naxalites who want to give up violence and join the mainstream of 
the society and the Centre should provide assistance to the State Governments for this scheme. 
(Para 4.10.17) 
UNION TERRITORIES WITHOUT LEGISLATURE 

  The Committee expresses its displeasure over  the fact that the amount of pension paid 
under the various social welfare sc hemes in the UTs is not uniform. The amount is also grossly 
inadequate keeping in view th e cost of living. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends 
that the Government may enhance the amount of pension under the welfare schemes at par with 
that of the NCT of Delhi, uniformly in all the UTs. (Para 5.1.6) 
  The Committee takes serious note that Plan  allocation for the year 2010-11 has been 
reduced by 30% from RE 2009-10. This is bo und to affect several schemes. The Committee 
desires that the Ministry of Home Affairs may take up the issue with the Planning Commission 
for enhancement of the Plan allocation at the RE  stage so that no projects suffer for want of 
finance. (Para 5.2.3) 
  The Committee notes that the budgetary provision for Tsunami Rehabilitation 
Programme has been reduced to Rs . 100 crore in BE 2010-11 in comparison to Rs. 647.77 crore 
in RE 2009-10. The Committee p resumes that major part of the rehabilitation programme must 
have been completed in Andaman & Nicobar Islands. In case the al location is not sufficient for 
the purpose, efforts be made fo r enhancing the provision at th e RE stage. The Committee feels 
that due to reduction in allocation for Tsuna mi Rehabilitation, the remaining part of the 
rehabilitation programme should not suffer.   
  The Committee is of the opinion that ru ral roads are of immense importance for the 
development of villages particularly in the remote areas of A & N Islands. Now that the revised 
estimates have been prepared, efforts should be  made to compensate for the lost time, by 
making appropriate allocations.   
MEDICAL FACILITY FOR CENTRAL GOVERNMENT EMPLOYEES AND OTHER 
MIDDLE CLASS PEOPLE 
  It is a sad state of affairs that no super speciality treatment/servic es are available in the 
Islands and the people have to travel to Tamil Nadu or Kolkata for treatment for which 
expenditure is not reimbursed. The Commi ttee does not understand why the Central 
Government has been indifferent to the woes of the people in general and its employees 
particularly the pensioners wh o are living in that Islands. The Committee recommends that 
Ministry of Home Affairs may take up this ma tter with the Ministry of Health & Family 
Welfare for appropriate decision at an early date. The Committee also recommends that the 
Government should also formulate suitable medical insurance scheme for all the persons living 
in the Islands. (Para 5.3.2) 
  In this context, the Committee would like to  highlight another area  affecting day to day 
lives of Islanders. In case of serious medical emergency for whic h a person has to be urgently 
evacuated to the nearby city in the main land for treatment, there appears to be no 
arrangements for air ambulance or dedicated he licopter service. The Committee is of the view 
that this is a critical area which the Ministry of Home Affairs needs to urgently look into so that 
the facility of air ambulance is  put in place and allocate f und to UT Administration for 
procurement of an air ambulance or dedicated helicopter. (Para 5.3.3) 
CONNECTIVITY OF ANDAMAN AND NICOBAR ISLANDS 

  The Committee is of the view  that the Andaman & Nicobar Isl ands has a special place in 
our history of freedom struggle. It is a sort of pilgrimage for the countrymen where Cellular Jail 
is located. Many of our legendary freedom figh ters were lodged in Cellular Jail and served life 
sentence. Some of the freedom fighters went to the gallows also. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that appropriate subsidy should be gi ven on air fare, to a ttract tourists to the 
Islands. Special packages for students could also be considered for making them understand and 
appreciate the sacrifices our freedom fighters had made for our independence. The Committee 
further recommends that old ships may be abandoned and new ones  may be brought in at the 
earliest. If necessary, participation of private sector may be explored. (Para 5.4.2) 
GENERAL RECOMMENDATIONS 
  The Committee is of the view that besides the specific recommendations made above, the 
Government should also consider the following suggestions: 
(i) Enhancement of Students Stipend from Rs.300 per month to Rs. 1000 per month 
keeping in view that the last revision was made in the year 2002; 
(ii) Enhancement of College Students Stipe nd from Rs.650 per mo nth to Rs. 1000 per 
month keeping in view that the last revision was made in year 2002; 
(iii) Students staying in hostels in the main land colleges are getting full reimbursement 
of amount as stipend. However, st udents who are unable to get hostel 
accommodation have and to reside in priv ate accommodations, are getting Rs. 300 
per month only. Such students should also be paid the amount equal to the stipend 
being given to the students staying in hostels. 
(iv) Budgetary provision be made for residential schools at each Tehsil from class IX to 
XII with science, arts, commerce stream s, for imparting quality education with 
adequate sanitation facilities; 
(v) Part-time teachers, Aaya, Sweeper, Mali, Peon, Instructor who have put in services 
for more than eighteen years are getting Rs . 2000 per month only. They should be 
paid minimum wages first. Their services should also be regularized; 
(vi) Five Vivekananda Kendra Zila Parishad Schools are imparting good quality 
education in the Islands. These schools are fi nding it difficult to retain teachers due 
to hike in their salary after the implem entation of the Sixth Pay Commission and 
also enhancement of teachers’ salary under the Sarva Shiksha Abhiyan from Rs. 
9000 to 18000. As these schools are si tuated in remote backward areas, 
enhancement of fee from students is not desirable. To retain teachers in these 
schools, A&N Islands Administ ration should match the defi cit part of their salary 
from its funds so that teachers in thes e schools get decent salary. For this MoD 
executed by the Administration may be amended; 
(vii) As per the criteria fixed for quantit y, calorie and protein under Mid-day Meal 
Scheme, the ceiling of Rs. 6.60 is not suffi cient for the children of the A&N Islands 
as the prices of essential commodities in the Islands is the highest in the country due 

to its remoteness. The ceiling needs to be  increased to Rs. 10 - 15 in view of the 
prevailing market rates. 
(viii) Mid-day Meal Scheme may be extended fr om class VIII to XII in consonance with 
the facility extended in Lakshadweep, Delhi and Tamil Nadu; 
(ix) Hostels in Islands part icularly in remote backward  areas like Mayabunder Boys 
Hostel, are in a dilapidated condition. The entire Islands fall under Seismic Zone V 
and these hostels may crumble in case of  any earthquake, causing loss of lives. 
Therefore, hostels in Rangat, Diglipur, Kishorinagar are in dire need of 
repair/renovation. There are many school s having broken tin roofs, broken 
thatched walls with no pucca floor, dr inking water and toilet facilities. Those 
schools should properly be constructed with all basic amenities. Some schools in the 
islands do not have class rooms, scien ce labs and staff quarters. These facilities 
must be provided immediately; 
(x) Since 2008, bicycles are be ing provided to Nicobari ST students in Class VIII under 
Sarva Shikhsha Abhiyan. Lakshadweep, Ta mil Nadu, Karnataka, Puducherry are 
providing free bicycles to all the students.  A& N Islands is considered as mini India 
on account of having harmony among diff erent castes and creeds. Students have to 
walk 10-12 kms to come to main road for buses and students are not getting timely 
reimbursement for their bus passes. Therefor e, bicycles should be provided to all 
the students to avoid any discrimination between tribals and non-tribals. 
(xi) Old age pension of Rs. 500/- was last revised in 2005-06. Goa and Delhi have revised 
the pension to Rs. 1000/- in the year 2008. Besides dhoti, Saree and four kg. rice per 
month is also given by Tamil Nadu G overnment. Old persons in A&N Islands 
should be given pension of Rs. 1000/- p er month. They should also be given two 
dhotis/Sarees in a year. 
(xii) Disability allowance of Rs. 500/- is p resently being given to handicapped persons in 
the A&N Islands. Handicapped persons in De lhi and Goa are getting allowance of 
Rs. 1000/- per month since 2008. Tamil Nadu Government provides dhoti/saree and 
four kg. rice per month free of cost. Mi zoram Government provides financial 
assistance for piggery @ Rs. 2000/-, poultry @ Rs. 1500/-, petty shop @ Rs. 1000/- 
per head per annum with sewing mach ines. Disabled persons in Andaman & 
Nicobar Islands should also be given allowance of Rs. 1000/- as well as assistance as 
being provided by Tamil Nadu and Mizoram Governments. 
(xiii) Destitute/single/abandone d/divorced women in Delhi are getting a pension of Rs. 
1000/- per month. No such scheme has been extended to A&N Islands. Pension of 
Rs. 1000/- for destitute/single/abandoned/divorced women in A&N Islands should 
be given. Government of NCT Delhi provides  marriage assistance of Rs. 20000/- to 
orphan girls and daughters of  widows. This scheme shou ld also be extended to 
A&N Islands. 
(xiv) There are 1400 Anganwadi Workers and Helpers. Workers are getting Rs. 1800- 
2000 while the helpers are getting Rs. 990/ - per month. These workers/Helpers are 

working since the last 15 to 20 years.  Puducherry and Tami l Nadu Governments 
have granted regular pay scales to them. Tamil Nadu Government also pay HRA, 
City Allowance and medical reimbursement. Workers/Help ers should be given at 
least minimum wages till such time, regular pay-scale for them are granted. 
(xv) A large number of farmers in the bo th districts of A&N Islands have lost 
agriculture land due to erosion caused by  rivers and canals. Therefore, erosion 
control measures must be initiated immedi ately and alternate land should be given 
to those farmers who have lost their agricultural land due to erosion. 
(xvi) Many old villages viz. Hanspuri, Shanti Nagar, Ganesh Nagar, Jagannath Dera, in 
the Islands solely depends on inland water transport due to lack of road 
connectivity. Till date, no jetty or dingi ghat has been provided for these villages. 
New jetties should be constructed for the benefit of the villages. 
(xvii) There are many crocodile infested riv ers, canal, and creeks which people have to 
cross to go to their villages. These villages viz. Ramnagar, Sitanagar, Radhanagar, 
Nischintpur, Kalighat are 40-50 years old. Funds should be provided for 
construction of bridges in these areas.  
(xviii) Yatri Niwas/Dharamshala run by Municipal Corporation was constructed 50 years 
ago. However, with the increase in popul ation, no additional rooms have been 
added in spite of foundation stone laid do wn by the then Union Minister of State 
for Home Affairs. Muni cipal Corporation should  be provided funds for 
construction of Dharamshala.  
(xix) There are many old villages viz . Soal Bay, Flat Bay, Kalsi, Shyam Kunj, etc. which 
have no road connectivity. Rural roads in  these villages should be constructed for 
providing road connectivity. 
(xx) Seven projects under PMGSY were sanctioned in 2004. Some funds were 
realeased. However, roads are still to be constructed in  North and Middle 
Andaman. Roads under PMGSY should be constructed at the earliest. 
(xxi) Police Personnel in the A&N Islands sh ould be granted Rati on Money like their 
counterparts in Delhi Police and CRPF. 
(xxii) Chatham Saw Mill which was established during British period is in dire need of 
modernization. Modernization of this Mill is included in the Plan every year but 
nothing is being done. Similarly, Betapu r Saw Mill in middle Andaman also needs 
modernization. 
(xxiii) About 80,000 agitated educated youth are jobless. Group ‘D’ Posts in various 
departments of A&N Islands Administration has been banned. The youth in the 
Islands does not have job opportunities as compared to their counterparts in the 
mainland. Therefore, special attention needs to be paid for employment of youth in 
the Islands. 

(xxiv) Agricultural land of a large number of farmers has submerged after Tsunami. One 
person of each of such affected family should be given Government Service. They 
should also be given alternate land. Free ration may be given till the allotment of 
alternate land is made. 
(xxv) According to BPL Census 2000, BPL families in Nicobar Tribal Area were 67%, 
South Andaman District 13%, North Andama n District 7%. Many families living 
in de-reserved blocks or jungles in North and Middle Andamans have been left out 
in BPL list. The cut off score for identifyin g BPL families is discriminatory. As per 
Planning Commission there should be 28000 BPL cards wh ereas only 17000 
BPL/AAY cards have been issued. Remainin g 11000 BPL cards should be issued 
immediately. 
(xxvi) LADLI Scheme on the pattern of Delhi,  for promotion of sex ratio, institutional 
birth and education of girls should be implemented in the Islands. 
(xxvii) Workers and Helpers in Day Care Ce ntres are getting honorarium of Rs. 1500-
2000 per month since the year 2003. Th e honorarium should be increased 
immediately. 
(xxviii) The earthen bunds have not been constructed scientifically, by the Zila Parishad, 
South Andaman and the same are in the danger of collapse/wash out. These 
bunds should immediately be reinforce d otherwise the Andaman Trunk Road 
and some villages will be submerged in the sea waters. (Para 5.5.1) 
DEMAND NO. 96 - DADRA  AND NAGAR HAVELI 
  The Committee is unable to understand why th e data relating to per capita income of 
Dadra & Nagar Haveli is not being maintained  by the UT Administration. The Committee 
strongly recommends that a surv ey be conducted, at the earlie st, to ascertain the per capita 
income of the UT not only for assessing the eco nomic health of the UT but also for various 
comparisons required for economic planning in the UT. (Para 5.7.5) 
  The Committee is of the view that adequate number of teachers in the UT is an essential 
pre-requisite to make the ‘Right to Education’ a reality. The interest of those who are working 
on daily wages should be protected. In this light, the Committee recommends that the UT 
Administration’s request to the Union Governme nt to approve regulariz ation of adhoc/daily 
wages teachers who were recruited against th e anticipated creation of vacancies by the 
Government of India and who fu lfill educational qualification as prescribed in the Recruitment 
Rules, as an one time measure, may be accepted. The Committee also recommends that 
expeditious action may be taken to fill up other vacancies of teachers. (Para 5.7.9) 
PAY OF HOME GUARDS IN DADRA  AND NAGAR HAVELI VIS-A-VIS OTHER UTs 
  The Committee recommends that the allowa nces of Home Guards may be suitably 
enhanced, at least at par with the private security services. (Para 5.8.2) 
AVAILABILITY OF DRINKING WATER 

  The Committee recommends that the water pr ojects at Mandoni and Dudhani Patelad be 
completed at the earliest as a permanent solution to the drinking wa ter problem especially 
during the summer season.  (Para 5.9.2) 
URBAN WATER SUPPLY SCHEME 
  The Committee desires that the scheme proposed by Silvasa Municipal Council may be 
cleared at the earliest by the Ministry of Ur ban Development. The Co mmittee hopes that ‘The 
Integrated Water Management Plan’ for the whole of the Union Territory would soon be put in 
place as a long term solution for the depleting water resources. (Para 5.10.2) 
MID-DAY MEAL SCHEME 
  The Committee, however, has been given to understand that the quality of food provided 
is of inferior quality and has compromised on the health and hygiene of children. The 
Committee is also constrained to learn that  there were instances in  the recent past when 
children had fallen ill after taking such food. The Committee desires that an inquiry may be 
held into the irregularities in implementing the Scheme. The Administrati on should ensure that 
the Scheme is implemented in a proper manner and carefully monitored on regular basis.(Para 5.11.2) 
DEMAND NO. 97 - DAMAN AND DIU 
GOALS/T ARGETS 
  The Committee recommends that a holis tic Vision Statement regarding overall 
development of the UT be prepared comprising of  realistic goals achievable within a given time 
frame and quantifiable benchmarks prescribed so that progress made in the Plan period could 
be measured and the development policies/schemes adjusted according to the performance on 
the ground. 
(Para 5.13.2) 
VILLAGE AND SMALL INDUSTRIES 
  The Committee has learnt that the subsidy provided to the fishermen has been increased 
but the number of fishermen co vered under it has been drastically reduced which is quite 
surprising. This does not augur well for the we lfare of the fishing community. The Committee is 
of the opinion that the matter needs to be reviewed. (Para 5.14.2) 
  The Committee is also of the considered vi ew that the Coast Guard should take up the 
issue of safeguarding the fishermen who venture into the deep sea and ensure their safe return. 
The Committee also recommends that the MHA may take up the matter with MEA, so that the 
fishermen languishing in the Pakistani jails are released at the earliest. (Para 5.14.3) 
EDUCATION 
  In the light of remoteness of the UT, physical proximity of school s and other educational 
institutions is essential so that  children get schooling closer to home and do not have to travel 
long distances for the same. The Committee is also  of the view that upgra dation of schools and 
syllabi should be done at regular intervals to meet the requirements of the local students. (Para 5.15.2) 
GENERAL RECOMMENDATIONS 

  Besides the specific recommendations made in  the previous paras, the Committee desires 
that the Government should also address the following problems of the UT of Daman & Diu: 
(i) The main sources of livelihood of D&D are Fisheries and Tourism. Some 
development activities like Distillery in Daman, have come up but the main economic 
activity is coastal tourism on which a s ubstantial part of the population survives. 
D&D is one of the most beautiful Union Territories in the country with vast sandy 
beaches and non-polluted coastal waters. The Committee recommends that the 
tourism potential of this Union Territory ha rnessed fully, so that tourists visit in 
large numbers to the UT and the means of livelihood of its residents multiply with 
the income generated from the proceeds of tourism. Steps should also be taken for 
encouraging the opening of resorts and hotels in the UT. 
(ii) One of the main reasons affecting the economic developm ent of D&D is the existing 
Coastal Regulation Zone Notification, 1991, which is very restrictive in nature. As 
57% of land is covered under Coastal Regulati on Zone, this notification has affected 
the livelihood of the fishermen communities including construction of their dwelling 
units apart from affecting the developm ent of coastal tour ism. The Committee 
understands that the Ministry of Environment and Forests is in the process of 
amending the Coastal Regulation Zone No tification, 1991 and trying to make a 
region specific notification on the lines of Prof. M.S. Swaminathan Committee 
Report. Necessary steps are required to be taken for removing the restrictions in the 
existing Coastal Regulation Zone for the promotion of tourism sector. 
(iii) The UT Administration had proposed construction of New bridges between Nani 
Daman and Moti Dama n and between Diu and mainla nd Gujarat. A few of the 
projects have been technically approve d by the Ministry of Shipping, Road 
Transport and Highways in June 2009 but the same have not seen the light of the 
day. Their financial approval is pending in the MHA. The Committee recommends 
that the Ministry of Home Affairs should expedite the approval so that the long 
pending demands of both Daman and Diu are taken care of at the earliest. 
(iv) The Committee was informed that the 51s t Battalion of the India Reserve Battalion, 
with headquarters in the UT of Lakshadw eep, has a very peculiar instance on the 
recruitment policy. The recruits to this Battalion cater to the needs of the UTs of 
D&D, DNH and Lakshadweep. The recruitment to this Battalion is from these three 
UTs. However these recruits are posted for a total period of four years away from 
their native place causing a lot of hardship, mainly due to the language problem. The 
Committee feels that the local recruits should stay within their Territory which will 
help them to lead a better family life  and thereby improve their operational 
efficiency. 
(v) The Committee has been apprised that the discharge of effluents (mostly untreated) 
from the chemical units in the GIDC Estate of Vapi into Daman Ganga River leads 
to contamination of the river and the polluted water flows through Daman emitting a 
lot of foul smell. Most of the potable water in Daman, which is ground water, gets 
contaminated due to seepage of the pollut ed water. Coastal Marine life and tourist 

spots are adversely affected. The Commi ttee feels that the problem needs to 
addressed urgently which if allowed to continue may lead to drop in revenue earning 
as the tourists have already started moving away from the coastal areas of D&D. 
(vi) The Committee, notes that both Daman and Diu depend on the Gujarat Government 
for water supply, which at present is not sufficient. Therefore, the Committee 
recommends that additional allocati on from Madhuban Da m for Daman and 
Narmada Canal for Diu has to be considered at the earliest. 
(vii) The UT of D & D does not produce elec tricity and are dependent on NTPC and the 
Western Grid for their requirement. A solu tion to the demand-supply gap has to be 
filled in at the earliest so that the existing industrial units are not forced to shift base. 
(viii) To improve health care,  a Government or Governme nt sponsored Super Speciality 
Hospital is the need of the hour for Diu and should be considered on top priority, 
(ix) More incentives to industrial houses to set up units in Da man and Diu to help 
generate employment for the locals and al so to generate revenue for the UT, may 
also be considered. 
(Para 5.16.1) 
CLARIFICATIONS SOUGHT IN RESPECT OF UTs OF DADRA & NAGAR HAVELI AND 
DAMAN AND DIU 
  The information as forwarded by the Minist ry of Home Affairs has been perused. While 
information in respect of tax and duty evasion ca ses and in respect of some other items in the 
two UTs in the first quarter of 2010 have been furnished as directed by the Committee, certain 
interesting aspects emerged therefro m. Initially the Ministry of Home Affairs sought a month’s 
time to compile the information as it was voluminous in nature and was to be compiled from 
different Ministries and agencies, but in replies to many of the questions, it has now been stated 
that the requisite information may be collected from the Mini stry of Finance (Customs and 
Excise Department) and the Ministry of Commerce. In respect of some other questions one 
month’s time has again been sought  for submission of replies on th e plea that the material to be 
collected would be voluminous. It has further been found that in respect of those questions 
where time has been sought to reply, the administ rations of the two UTs have already furnished 
voluminous information to the Committee.  
(Para 5.17.5) 
  At the outset, the Committee would like to observe that as per established practice, 
whenever, it requires any informat ion, the concerned Ministry und er its jurisdiction is asked to 
supply the same irrespective of the fact that the informatio n may concern other Ministry, 
Department or State Government. It is perhaps the first time, in the history of the Committee 
that the administrative Ministry  concerned is asking it to pr ocure the requisite information 
from other Ministries directly. In the same vein, the Ministry is also seeking extension of time 
for supply of material in respect  of certain other questions. While  information has already been 
furnished by UT Administrations, the Committee takes a serious note of the casual approach of 
the Ministry of Home Affairs and the Administrator in respo nding to the request of the 
Standing Committee. The Committee therefore direc ts the Ministry and the Administrator to 

supply the requisite information to it positively within fifteen days  from the presentation of the 
Report. (Para 5.17.6) 
  In reply to the query of the Committee regardi ng instances of evasion of taxes and duties, 
if any, by enterprises, including violations of the Goa, Daman & Diu Excise Duty Act and Rules, 
1964 detected year-wise since 2004-05 by the Da man and Diu Administrati on and others, cases 
registered etc., the UT administration has prov ided incomplete and contradictory information 
instead of facilitating the Committ ee to understand the ground realitie s, particularly relating to 
the level of alertness of the concerned Depart ments in ensuring collection of Government 
revenue commensurating with th e business activities of the units operating from the Union 
Territories of Daman & Diu and Dadra & Nagar Haveli. (Para 5.17.7) 
  For instance, the volume of Excise Duty coll ection is stated to have “more than doubled” 
since 2004-05, from 24.24 crore to Rs. 57.90 crore, but the reply is silent about what this increase 
in collection of Excise Duty has been attr ibutable to. It was incumbent upon the UT 
Administration to explain as to whether the incr ease in Excise Duty collection every year was 
due to enhanced volume of production by the units, or revision of rates or for any other reasons, 
to enable the Committee to und erstand the ground situation. By  providing such incomplete 
information, the Committee has been denied that opportunity. (Para 5.17.8) 
  More glaring in the reply is the attempt of  providing contradictory information to the 
Committee. While, on the one hand, it  is stated in the reply that “no evasion of Excise Duty has 
been detected during the period in  question” on the other hand it is  stated “...... recently during the 
meetings of the Joint Regional Intelligence sharing, apprehensions have been raised regarding 
probable evasion of the excise duty by the distilleries in Daman” and that “.......In the interest of the 
Government revenue, the Department has moved swif tly and only on the apprehensions of the 
excise duty evasion the show cause notices have been issued to the Distilleries of Daman as to why 
the excise duty of Rs. 300 crores ( approximately. total of all distilleries) should not be raised upon 
them for last five years.” It is fu rther stated that, “......notices have been issued just to pre-empt any 
time barring of the demand in case the raising of demand found justified and also as apprehensions 
were raised”. 
  The Committee, it appears, is  being sought to be kept in th e dark as to the genesis of the 
“apprehension” leading to issuance of notices for recovery of ev aded Excise Duty for periods 
covering past five years. Whether the said Jo int Regional Intelligence has been exercising 
surveillance over the region encompassing Gujarat, Goa, Maha rashtra, besides the UTs of 
Daman & Diu and Dadra & Nagar Haveli, and had pi cked up clues leading to the conceiving of 
“apprehensions” about the alleged evasion of Excise Duty by distilleries operating in Daman? In 
that case, the genesis of the said “apprehension ” should have been explained for removing the 
confusion of the Committee. 
(Paras 5.17.9 & 5.17.10) 
  Moreover, it remains unclear as to how th e UT Administration could issue notices for 
recovery of Excise Duty to assessees simply based on “apprehension” that it had been evaded for 
the past five years. Does it not weaken the case and strengthen the alleged taxes/duty evaders to 
challenge the demands of the Department in the Court? 

  It should be incumbent upon  Government to ascertain the facts of the above developments 
with particular reference to the questions raised  by the Committee, as above, and enlighten the 
Committee about the ground realities of the matter. (Para 5.17.11 and 5.17.12) 
CENSUS 2011 AND NATIONAL POPULATION REGISTER 
  The Committee takes into account the deta iled arrangements made for Census 2011, 
formation of UIDAI for issuance of Unique Identi fication Number and compilation of National 
Population Register (NPR). The Committee unders tands that Census of the country is a 
cumbersome process. However, it is significant in view of the fact that all the policies, 
programmes and schemes of the Government are fr amed on the basis of the conclusions drawn 
from the Census. (Para 6.9.1) 
  The Committee observes that the first phase  of the Census 2011 will be held during April 
and September 2010 for collection of data on Ho uselisting & Housing Census as well as for 
preparation of National Population Register. The Committee apprehends that it will be difficult 
and improper to carry out both exercises together for the following reasons: 
(i) The Census information is collected u nder the Census Act, 1948 which guarantees 
complete confidentiality of  the individual and household data collected. This 
confidentiality clause is known to be a ke y-stone that ensures accuracy and sanctity 
of the Census Data; and 
(ii) The National Population Register (NPR) information is to be collected under the 
Citizenship Act, 1955 read with the Citi zenship (Registration and Issue of National 
Identity Cards) Rules, 2003 and the data collected are not confidential and are meant 
to be used for identifying individuals and household. Thes e make the purpose, 
objectives and the process of the two exercise different and, hence, it is important to 
keep the two exercises separate in order to maintain sanctity of both. (Para 6.9.2) 
  With regard to the confidentiality of the Census Data, the Committee apprehends that it 
would be difficult to hold back information contained in the Census Schedule in view of the RTI 
Act, 2005. It is quite likely that even if exem ption clause is invoked under RTI Act, 2005, the 
CIC, the Supreme Court or the High Courts may pass orders for disclosure of information. The 
Committee therefore recommends to Government to keep this aspect in mind. (Para 6.9.3) 
  The Committee also took note of the statemen t made by the Ministry of Home Affairs that 
the creation of National Population Register would be a comprehensive identity database in the 
country for better targeting of the benefits and services under the Government 
schemes/programmes, improving planning and strengthening securi ty of the country. However, 
the Committee is of the considered view that the ‘Nationality As  Declared’ clause in the NPR 
Household Schedule has the potential to confer de facto Indian Citizenship to illegal migrants, 
despite the disclaimer attached  to the clause. The Committee is of the opinion that the 
Government should not act in haste. There is a need to have a rethinking on the NPR exercise. 
The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends th at the ‘Nationality’ clause should be 
removed as it has the potents in the long run which may pose a threat to the security and 
integrity of the country. Therefo re, it may be worthwhile cons idering suspension of the NPR 

exercise for the time being. The Committee fu rther recommends that th e work relating to NPR 
preparation should be de-linked from the Census operations. (Para 6.9.4) 
HOUSING SCHEME FOR DELHI POLICE 
  The Committee is constrained to note that  there is undue delay in allocating land for 
housing projects by the DDA. Being a Government organization, DDA should not function on 
purely commercial lines like private builders. The Committee recommends that DDA should 
make more concerted efforts to acquire land exclusively for police housing of Delhi Police so 
that the pressing need of the police personnel could be met. Keeping in mind the sensitive law 
and order situation in Delhi, proper functioning of Police Stations and keeping the morale of the 
police force high are of utmost importance. Therefore, making land available for Police Stations 
and Housing Projects, requires immediate attention of the authorities. The Committee therefore 
urges upon the DDA, , MCD and other agencies to make land available for Housing projects and 
Police Stations in Delhi with least delay.  
(Para 7.7.1) 
To achieve 40% satisfaction level, Delhi Police needs a total of about 28,000 houses against about 
15,000 houses available with it, thus, leaving a gap of about 13,000 quarters. The Committee is 
concerned that by 2012-13 only about 7,000 flats are likely to be added, including the projects 
under PPP model. This is grossly inadequate. Co nsidering the rate at which the quarters are 
being constructed and that additional land is not available with Delhi Police, the target can only 
be achieved in a reasonable period by outrig ht purchase of ready built flats from DDA. The 
Committee is of the view that the Ministry of Home Affairs should take  up the matter of ready 
built flats for Delhi Police with DDA and the D DA on its part should also make provision for 
500-550 flats every year for Delhi Police.   
  The Committee feels that on the one hand De lhi Police faces scarcity of Housing for its 
personnel and on the other the fu nds allocated, particularly non- plan allocation, are not spent 
fully. During 2006-07 only a fraction was spent where as in 2007-08 and 2008-09, more than 10 
per cent could not be spent. For achieving physi cal targets, achieving financial targets is 
necessary. Delhi Police should take necessary steps to achieve financial targets. (Para 7.7.3) 
  The Committee is of the view that police bu ildings should be decl ared as operational 
buildings as has been done in the case of Railways and P & T so that construction could start 
straightaway. The Committee is also of the view that the average time  taken for declaring 
buildings as operational should also be brought down so as to expedite the process. (Para 7.7.4) 
  The Committee feels that for achieving 40 per cent satisfaction level,  present allocations - 
both Plan and Non-Plan - are not sufficient. Government should therefore assess the total 
requirement of funds. Allocations should be increased to enable Delhi Police to achieve desired 
satisfaction within the next five years. (Para 7.7.5) 
  The Committee does not understand why Delh i Police makes such a target which cannot 
be achieved. During tenth plan 40% satisfaction level was proposed and only 22.91 % was 
achieved. Again 40% is being proposed for Eleven th Plan. This is not a right approach. Delhi 
Police should try to keep realistic targets only. (Para 7.7.6) 

  The Committee desires that the pitfalls of the Tenth Plan have to be avoided and the 
physical targets fixed for the eleventh Five-Y ear Plan should be achieved. Clearance of 
proposals and clearance for lands may be taken up at the highest level so that these areas do not 
act as impediments for the achievement of targets. (Para 7.7.7) 
  In the non-plan scheme also, things are not moving in the right direction. Out of 17 PS 
buildings, only 3 were expected to be ready by 31.3.2010 and out of 1,4 81 staff quarters, 13 were 
expected by ready by 31.3.2010. This needs to be  looked into and steps may be taken for timely 
completion of the buildings and quarters. (Para 7.7.8) 
  It is a welcome step that about 5,202 quarters are proposed to be constructed under Public 
Private Partnership (PPP) model at Dheerpur and Police Headquarters are proposed to be 
constructed at Parliament Street . As mentioned in the Outcome Budget, the financial mode for 
the projects may be finalized quickly. All other st eps may also be taken to ensure that both the 
projects are completed by 2013.   
  All the projects, which are under way in both Plan and Non-Plan Schemes, may be 
completed as per the targets and there should not be any cost and time over-runs. (Para 7.7.10) 
  The Committee was given to understand that one of the reasons for the shortfall in the 
housing project was non-completion of constr uction by PWD in time. The Committee takes 
strong exception to this. If PWD does not impr ove its performance, Delhi Police may go in for 
construction by other public sector undertakings. (Para 7.7.11) 
  The Committee appreciates that a Proj ect Monitoring Committee (PMC) under the 
Chairmanship of Joint Secretary (UT), Ministry of Home Affairs has been set up to monitor the 
building plans of Delhi Police. The Committee de sires that this Committee should strictly 
monitor the implementation on a regular basis and ensure that the projects are completed in 
time. 
(Para 7.7.12) 
HOUSING SCHEME FOR CENTRAL POLICE FORCES 
  The Committee observes that just a little mo re than 15% of the Force personnel entitled to 
family accommodation, are able to avail the fac ility. Force personnel, who remain deployed at 
international borders as well as in violence infested regions, are engaged in grappling with 
militancy and insurgency throughout the year. They do not even have the mental comfort of 
their families having proper accommodation, medi cal facilities etc, while they are away from 
home. 
(Para 7.17.1) 
  The Committee taking note of the findings of  the BPR&D study feels that the Scheme is 
beneficial and recommends that it should be continued. (Para 7.17.2 
  The Committee takes a serious note of the fact that the deficiency of the housing units as 
on 1.4.1009 was 114634. If the new battalions, which had been approved by the Government and 
were likely to be raised by 201 3-14 were also taken into ac count, forty thousand personnel 
would be further added. The allocations have been made as per the current requirements only. 
As this rate, the gap would keep on increasing and it would be a never ending phenomenon. The 

Committee agrees that the allocations have not kept pace with the increase in number of 
personnel in various Central Police Forces and this situation is likely to go from bad to worse. 
The Committee therefore recommends that the Government should seriously assess the financial 
requirements for the housing of the CPF personne l. The Ministry informed the Committee that 
due to massive housing shortage, the funds were in adequate and they would move for additional 
funds at RE stage. The Committee suggests that a dditional funds may be sought at RE 2010-11 
stage. (Para 7.17.3) 
  The Committee recommends that to supplemen t the funds requirement, not covered by 
the Planning Commission, Ministry  of Finance may be allowed to make adequate allocation 
from the ‘Non-Plan’ side also so as to enable the forces to meet the housing requirements.(Para 7.17.4) 
  The CPFs are facing the problem of land acquisition for construction of houses. The 
responsibility of acquiring land lies with the Stat e Governments and a lot of time is taken for 
completing the acquisition process. This area needs to be addressed. The Committee desires that 
the Home Secretary may address the Chief Secretar ies of all the State Governments about the 
necessity of early clearances for the land acquisition. (Para 7.17.5) 
  The construction of houses for CPFs also su ffers due to problems of capacity utilization of 
the implementing agencies. The Committee ther efore suggests that besides CPWD, more and 
more public sector agencies may be considered for executing the projects. (Para 7.17.6) 
  The Ministry proposed to launch a mega housing project for CPFs to address problems of 
accommodation for the forces personnel on Public Private Partnership basis. The Committee 
desires that an early decision may be taken in this regard. (Para 7.17.7) 
  The Committee appreciates the amendment made in Rule 126 of GFR allowing the 
involvement of other Public Works Organisati ons as the same would help in avoiding 
unnecessary delays and other regulatory bottlen ecks in completion of housing projects for 
Central Police Forces in time. However, the purpo se will be defeated if there are inordinate 
delays in giving nod/concurrence. The Committee, therefore recommends that a reasonable time 
limit should be imposed in giving clearance by Ministry of Urban Development/CPWD. The 
Committee also recommends that Engineering Wing s of CPFs be further strengthened so that 
they can be entrusted more project works for spee ding construction of residential as well as 
other type of accommodation. (Para 7.17.8) 
  A well approach coordinated is needed amon gst various agencies so that the regulatory 
and logistic requirements are me t well before the projects are given a go-ahead and that the 
projects do not get stuck at the implementation stage due to procedural hurdles. Clearances from  
local bodies through an institutionalized  mechanism, with all-round support from the clearance 
agencies should be done on fast track basis. (Para 7.17.9)

2.  In the absence of the Chairman, Shri S.S. Ahluwalia was voted to the Chair. 
3.   The Chair welcomed the Members and apprised them of the agenda for the day. Thereafter, he 
asked the Home Secretary to make a presentation on the Demands for Grants of the Ministry of Home 
Affairs for the year 2010-11 and al so asked him to focus on the budgetary aspects as also the major 
programmes and policies of the Ministry. 
4.   The Chair expressed his displeasure over the absence of heads of many organizations under the 
Ministry of Home Affairs and desired that they should remain present in the next meeting. Home 
Secretary noted the direction. The Chair then advi sed the Home Secretary to make a presentation on 
“Arms, Fire Arms and Ammunition” and “Draft Arms Policy” after the presentation on the Demands-
for-Grants. 
5.   The Home Secretary then briefed the Committee about the general internal security situation in 
the country. He underlined the weaknesses in State Police Forces both in terms of strength of 
personnel and their training and intelligence capabilities. He also briefed the Committee about the 
setback in the procurement of bullet proof jackets and assured that retesting and re-tendering process 
was on and by middle of April, they would be able to place orders for bullet proof jackets. 
6.  Thereafter, the AS and FA made a Powerpoint presentation on the Demands for Grants (2010-
11) of the Ministry of Home Affairs. 
7.   On being enquired about reduction in allocation for rehabilitation of J&K Migrants, the 
Committee was informed that only when the migrants go back to the valley, that the package for 
rehabilitation can be disbursed. 

8.   During the course of presentation, the Committee was also informed that MHA has been 
assisting the Government of Tamil Nadu in maintaining 150 refugee camps, though technically they 
have not been given the status of refugees as I ndia was not a signatory to the various international 
conventions in that regard. The Secretary (BM)  promised to send a note on the same to the 
Committee. 
9.  The Chair and Members then raised some i ssues regarding National Disaster Management 
Authority, Department of Official Language; fire services; security during Commonwealth Games; 
Crime and Criminal Network System; National Intelligence Grid. Police Training allocation made to 
various States for tackling Left Wing Extemism; outsourcing of security related studies by BPR&D to 
private agency; second phase of Modernisation of  Prison Administration; and compensation to the 
1984 riots victims. The queries were replied to the Home Secretary and other officers. 
10.   The Chair then sought clarifications from the Registrar General of India about the National 
Population Register (NPR). The Census Commissioner and the Registrar General of India informed 
the Committee that the NPR for the coastal areas was taken up in 3331 villages for which the data 
collection was almost over and thereafter the process of issuing of cards would be started. He also 
informed that the Census would start in 2011, while data collection for NPR in the entire country 
would start in April, 2010. Once the database was created it would be sent for collection of 
Biometrics. 
11.   The Chair advised the Home Secretary to k eep, as far as possible, uniform benchmarks for 
allocation among various. 
12.   The Chair then asked the Home Secretary to make a presentation on Dadra & Nagar Haveli in 
the subsequent meeting. 
13.  A verbatim record of the proceedings of the meeting was kept. 
14.  The Committee then adjourned at 2.15 
P.M.

2.0  In the absence of the Chairman, Shri S.S. Ahluwalia was voted to the Chair. 
2.1  The Chair welcomed the Members and officials. He also informed them about the agenda for the 
day. He then ascertained facts regarding the incident of bomb scare in a Kingfisher flight, blowing up 
of rail track in Lal Garh area in West Bengal by Maoists and reported beefing up of security in Media 
offices in Mumbai, from the Director, IB. Queries were also raised regarding security at Airports in 
the country, by various Members. 
2.2  Director, IB responded to the queries. 
3.0  Members then raised queries relating to the Commonwealth Games. 
3.1  Thereafter, the Chief Secretary, Delhi respon ded to the queries of the Members regarding, the 
Commonwealth Games, package being implemented for 1984 riot victims and old age pension 
scheme. He also responded to the Chair’s query regarding preparedness of the Fire Department of 
NCT Delhi for the Commonwealth Games. He assured the Members of the Committee that projects of 
the Commonwealth Games were on as per Schedule. He assured the Committee that most of the works 
would be completed by the end of June. 
3.2  One Member raised the issue of welfare of construction workers and the migrant labour, 
working in various Commonwealth Games Projects. As  most of the workers were contract labourers 
and migrants, he enquired about the welfare meas ures taken by Government for them and whether 
they were being paid minimum wages as per the minimum wages Act. 

3.3  Chief Secretary responded to the queries. 
4.0   The Chair then enquired about the Disaster Management Authority and training programmes for 
Civil Defence imparted by the Ministry which wa s replied to by the Home Secretary. The Chair 
further enquired about the training given to various paramilitary forces and the adequacy of funds 
released for the same. Thereafter, the Home Secr etary was asked to give a detailed report on 
budgetary provisions for training of paramilitary forces. 
5.0  The Committee was informed by the Home Secretary that the proposal of having Quick 
Reaction Teams and Commandos all across the state had been accepted. Regarding National 
Intelligence Grid (NIG), he informed that a paper was currently before the Cabinet Committee on 
Security and the Government was hopeful that the implementation of NIG would be possible during 
2010-11. Regarding outsourcing of study by BPR& D, the Home Secretary clarified there was a 
confidentiality clause in the agreement in each such study. 
6.0  The Committee then decided to invite the Registrar General of India in a separate sitting of the 
Committee for a detailed presentation on the Census related issues. 
7.0  The Chair raised issues regarding the grieva nces of Group A Direct Recruit officers of CISF and 
also enquired about the feasibility of filling up some vacancies in the IPS from amongst the officers of 
CPMFs, through a limited Departmental exam. The Home Secretary replied that though the UPSC had 
turned not followed the proposal for filling up vacancies in the IPS from State Police and Paramilitary 
Forces Cadres, the Ministry was contemplating to go ahead with the proposal, subject to the approval 
of the Prime Minister. 
8.  The Joint Secretary (IS-II) in the MHA made  a powerpoint presentation on the subject, Arms, 
Firearms and ammunition. 
  [Committee then adjourned for Lunch at 1.00 
P.M. and reassembled at 2.00 P.M.] 
8.1  Specific queries regarding the proposed Arms Policy were also raised by Members. In this 
context, the Chair suggested that the Bill amending the Arms act also should first be brought before 
Parliament before doing so, the draft Amendment Bill should be posted on the website for suggestions 
and comments in the general public. After introduction, the Bill should be reported to this standing 
Committee. The Chair also suggested that afte r the Arms act had been amended the Arms 
(Amendment) Rules should be notified and laid on the table of both the Houses, for a statutory period 
of thirty days, for Parliamentary approval. In that context he advised the Government not to proceed 
with announcing of the new policy and its implementation unless Parliament approved the 
amendments to the Act and the Rules because in the event of Parliamentary approval not forthcoming, 
the new policy would become infractuous. 
8.2  The Home Secretary clarified that the Governme nt would take note of the suggestions given by 
the Committee. The Government would then finalis e the rules and thereafter would place the rules 
before both the houses of Parliament. The Parliament would then discuss those. He, therefore, felt that 
there would separate need for bringing Committee it before the Standing Committee. 
9.   Thereafter, a presentation was made by the Joint Secretary (UT) on financial allocation to Dadra 
& Nagar Haveli. 

9.1  Some queries were put by Members which were not replied satisfactorily by the Administrator. 
The Committee directed the Administrator to send the replies in writing and to appear again before the 
Committee. 
10.  The Commissioner of Police, Delhi then made an oral presentation on security preparedness of 
Delhi Police for the ensuing Commonwealth Games. After the presentation, he also replied to the 
queries of the Members regarding the law & order situ ation in the city and the proposed move of the 
Government to ask all citizen to surrender licensed arms in the area police station during the duration 
of the Commonwealth Games. Commissioner of police responded to the query by explaining the 
position of Delhi Police on the issue. 
11.  The Committee then decided to meet on 29th March, 2010 to discuss the Demands for Grants 
(2010-11) of Ministry of DoNER and also to hear  Joint Secretary (UT) and Administrator, Dadra & 
Nagar Haveli on the issues related to the above UT. 
12.0   A verbatim record of the proceedings of the meeting was kept. 
13.0   The Committee then adjourned at 4.00 
P.M.
